Actually, it's probably better to highlight and display the planned move like we used to, and then on engage or end turn actually animate and resolve, with engagement happening after the animations complete. Makes it easier to see who's moved." So a move shows as its route from where the machine stands, the next move or order reads from its end, and ENGAGE and END TURN play every planned move before the fight opens or the packs walk; a move into a hex another planned move left plays after it. And "undo should happen in global order, not on a per-unit basis. So the undo stack is stored in order, and undos in order, always", attack orders included (his "attack orders on the map should be able to undo"): an order with its move into range is one step. A fight is the stack's floor (Ryan: "yes"): what came before it has been played out and fought over, so UNDO reaches back to the last fight or the turn's start, where the per-machine undo took a machine back until it fought. Retired: a move walked at once, which made every next action wait on its walk, and UNDO MOVE by machine. A move is given in one go (Ryan, 28 October, the movement proposal): one move an action, the whole of it, then the machine may fight or stand; what moving in pieces bought, a look at the attack band after a step, the band shows before the move. That closes the line his play of 25 October left open (a move given in one go, or a clearer mark that a machine has move left) by removal: no move is left to mark. Planned moves play on EXECUTE too, and the turn goes on (Ryan, 2 November: “when entering movement, it shouldn't ‘end turn’ when a command is entered. Movement should be able to be executed in sequence if the player wants, and undone. It should show ‘execute’ when a move command is entered, and only end turn if no order is entered. If end turn is hit and there are movements/actions remaining, it should alert the player”): the one button reads ENGAGE while a fight is ready, EXECUTE while planned moves wait, END TURN otherwise; an executed move stays undoable, its machine set back where it stood with nothing to play twice; END TURN asks while any machine can still move or fight. Retired: END TURN playing the plan and ending the turn in one press while moves waited. A caught machine's fight shows as its attack order, the order's line drawn to each hunter that caught it and the board naming it (“when caught, the unit should show the attack order on the enemy that caught it for clarity”).

Decided

The enemy's rings are always on the map, in its violet; a selected machine shows where it could attack from.

Ryan, 25 October, from play: "Enemy engagement ranges need to be a lot more clear. They need to be always visible, and become more visible when the enemy is tapped, and done in red not hud colour", and "when a unit is highlighted to move, it needs to show not only its available move spaces, but its available attacking spaces at the edge of that move, in red". Every pack's ring is a dashed edge at rest, and the pack tapped (or one that would join the fight shown) has its ring tinted under a heavy solid edge with bright dashes crawling on it, in place of the display's-light reach a tap used to show. A selected machine's lit move is ringed by a band, tinted under one solid edge, every hex within its attack range of where its move reaches, and a pack it could be ordered onto is bracketed. Both wore red until 30 October and wear the enemy's violet since, the band with the rings (Ryan: "keep purple for enemy activities"; the band's move his agreeing Claude's lean), parting from each other by structure (a dashed edge against a filled band; the move's lit cells against one band) and from the packs' violet routes the same way (a ring of dashed hex edges against a line with its target), since Ryan is colourblind. The hex grid's resting light came down a fifth on 25 October to calm a busy screen ("base hex outline brightness can be reduced a bit") and went back once the grid's lines held one width at every zoom (Ryan, 30 October: "With these changes, I think it could go back how it was"). A hunted machine wears a tag in place of the orange target under it (Ryan: "a tag hovering by the unit with the hunter's portrait (probably cropped) and some kind of target symbol"): a plate a hunter, up and left of the machine, the hunter's map picture and a target turning. Exit: his read on the phone.

Decided

The button pulses once nothing else is left to do.

Ryan, 25 October: "when no actions remain in a skirmish turn (excluding rerolls), end turn should pulse. Same with the map phase, when all units are out of actions". In a fight, once no die has anywhere to be spent, rerolls left or not; on the map, once every machine has fought, is ordered, is caught, or can neither move nor reach a pack where it stands (Ryan agreed: nothing useful left, "since technically that incorporates eventual commander actions if they appear"), so ENGAGE pulses too when orders wait. Rings breathe out past the button and its face brightens, by motion as well as light; RIFTGATE_PULSE_DEBUG=1 keeps it pulsing to judge it running.

Decided

A machine moves before it fights, never after.

A fight spends its action however it ends, so a hunter pins what it catches until the team kills it. The reason is the pin; a machine that moves after it fights is pinned below, not rejected, and a passive could bend this as a one-off.

Decided

Four machines, split as the wall asks; each split priced by the packs’ threat ratings.

Ryan, 13 October, after the one-to-four measurement (docs/measurements.md): the squad stays four, and the fights come in sizes. Since the long wall the packs say their size (the pack row), and a true four is kept for the elites and the bosses (Ryan, 6 November: “a 'true' 4 should be reserved for elites and bosses, with formations a 3. Then the decision becomes a 1-3 split, a 2-2 split where one is safe and one short (or taking two 3 fights short, or two patrol fights safe), or all 4 on the formations and ignoring the flanks.”). The reason: concentration halves the hull a fight costs with every machine added, so four on one pack is the lightest hull and, on a long wall, the heaviest gate, which made fighting as four the safe play while a formation was a four; with a formation a three, every split has a price the player can read. A high-move machine is the flanker and the firefighter (Ryan, 5 November), so each chassis has a solo value beside its team value, which balancing for real measures. Four was revisited 27 and 28 October and kept, three an option if four proves too difficult to balance (Ryan, 28 October); closed as a question 4 November. Retired: the squad moving as one by default, with a detachment priced by time and bailed out by the skirmish withdrawal (13 October); the test it failed is Ryan’s play of 5 November, where the safe play was always the whole squad. Exit: the campaign runner’s fights by rating and machines in them, the promise read (docs/measurements.md), and Ryan’s play.

Decided

One pack is one fight: the machines beside it fight it, nothing joins, and a hunter’s catch is fought first.

Ryan, 5 November, retiring the engagement system: “it was easy to pick off targets or head off pushers, meaning that the formations we were trying to balance around, I was able to circumvent completely... it just adds so much complexity for not a lot of gain.” So a fight is one pack and the machines ordered onto it, each standing beside it (an attack range of one, a placeholder); no pack joins another’s fight, a formation is fought whole, and the fights of a turn are fought one at a time in the order the player opens them. Hunters keep the catch: a hunter that ends its walk with what it comes for inside its ring forces that fight first, fought to its end. A hunter’s ring is one number and two at least, so it pounces over whatever stands between (Ryan, 29 October: “a minimum range of 2 instead of 1 (wolfs pounce, etc.)”); it keeps its prey while the prey stands and goes for it by any route; only when walls and machines seal the prey off does it take the machine a walk this turn can put inside its ring that stands nearest the prey, its caveat breaking a tie, and with none in reach it closes on its prey (Ryan, 29 October: “my target if I can reach him, otherwise the closest machine to my target that I can reach this turn, otherwise chase my target”; “Safety in speed, not hiding, seems more fair to me”). Nearest the prey and never nearest the hunter, so a prey hidden costs whoever stands nearest it. Retired: the rings by which packs joined fights (front-rank foes small, back-rank foes large) and the machines’ attack range of two, which set a fight’s composition by formation and the order of attack; the test they failed is Ryan’s play of 5 November, the formations circumvented by picking a member off or heading a pusher off. Retired before: the re-pick (28 October), which let a line of machines hide the prey; the hunters’ ring of one. Exit: the pounce overbearing in play, when the ring comes back down (Ryan: “If it’s overbearing then we send the engage range back down”); a formation circumvented again, which reopens the fight’s shape.

Decided

The boss is attacked like any pack, and its fight takes every machine and cannot be left.

It guards the rift, immune until the Rupture meter fills (Ryan, 24 October). The seal is built (30 October): a sealed boss cannot be ordered onto, its readout SEALED, until the last Fracture or the stall guard breaks it (the pacing row), and nothing re-seals it (Ryan, 6 November: “do we need to re-seal the boss? Isn't that just a feel-bad, or a non-choice?”); the tide quickens after the Rupture instead (the one-clock row). The multi-hex boss stays the field row's. Its fight opens with the reveal and the domain's boss theme. Ryan, 25 October, from play: "The boss fight should automatically make all units attacking when one unit targets it, regardless of their range. Disable withdraw for the boss fight - the player is committed to closing the rift or getting scrapped trying. That sorts out the defense issue, and there's no reason to move any units once engage is clicked, just go right into the boss animation. Doesn't matter if a unit has fought this turn already, the only way that would actually happen is 1) the player is greeding for farm, 2) a hunter needs to get resolved first, which is fair." So one order onto the boss brings every machine, ENGAGE goes straight to the reveal, and the fight ends the battle, the rift sealed or the team scrapped; the rift is sealed only when the fight is won, escort and all, and a team wrecked after the boss falls is scrapped (Ryan, 27 October; the mechanics audit's 26). Since the long wall nothing joins a fight (the fights-picked row), so the boss’s fight is the boss and its escort and a fight on a pack beside it is that pack’s alone; the joining Ryan kept on 26 October (“I think it's correct since it forces the player to think about when to engage, but if it's really unbalanced we can re-evaluate”) went with the rings. Retired: withdrawing from the boss’s fight (13 October), settled by Ryan’s commitment to the rift before the withdrawal went everywhere (the withdrawal row); engaging the boss is the battle’s last decision. What reopens it: a boss that is fought more than once (a regrowing boss).

Open

A battle withdrawal: not encouraged, not yet refused.

Ryan, 13 October: never built, by intent, since what the gate then takes is hard to evaluate and easy to abuse, and he would rather not encourage it; then, the same day, that a team wipe already grades a lost battle, so a withdrawal may be possible. Claude's proposal: the map prices it. To withdraw, the machines walk out through the gate: hunters catch stragglers, pushers strike the gate while they retreat, Rift Pressure spawns, flyers leave cheap because that is what flyers are, and a team deep north pays six turns for leaving late. The city then takes the wipe's unopposed phases; the machines are not wrecked. A player forced to play out a twenty-minute loss is the helplessness section 2 refuses. Ryan to decide. Ryan, 13 October, later: the walk-out makes thematic sense; decided later.

Decided

Damage sticks.

Hull never repairs during a battle and damage never slows a machine; the shield is full and the heat zero as every fight opens (the shield row), so hull is what a battle wears, and where it is spent, on which packs and in which split, is the player’s call. The race to close the rift went with the tide (6 November): the seal breaks only at the Rupture, earned by holding the wall (the three-clocks row).

Decided

One clock, Rift Pressure: the tide, waves on a beat, ticking on the map and never in a fight.

It sends packs when it peaks, in place of any cap on a fight. Ryan, 13 October: the tick per round fought went. It stood in for the round cap he refused, but it could not tell an epic fight from a stall, taxed the five-to-eight-round fights the pacing wants, and punished care invisibly, since nothing in the fight said a pack was forming; measured the same day (docs/measurements.md), it had been spawning packs into the plain player’s long solo fights. A long fight costs one map turn like any other. The guard against a stall is enemy damage outpacing sustain (section 5) and the runner marking fights past sixty rounds and stalemates of ten. Since the long wall (6 November; Claude’s design, Ryan: “We are aligned, your reasoning is sound.”) it is the tide: the beat the ramp’s, three turns at a run’s start and two from Convergence 8; a peak sends the cycle’s next composition from the rift and a patrol from every tear open before it, and creeps the seam (the tears row); the battle opens with the siege under way, the pressure a turn short of its first peak (the three-clocks row); and the beat halves once at the Rupture, never below a turn, so each turn of delay costs more at the wall and the sally is the release (Claude’s lean, unmoved, in place of the re-seal Ryan refused: the boss row). All placeholders. The step by which each peak shortens the next is on the table at zero (the stall row); a tick per fight is the lever not pulled, and the backstop held in reserve, pressure ticking late in a fight, stays unbuilt.

Pacing

Decided

A battle is a short run of fights toward a boss the player earns.

Ryan, 13 October: four to five meaningful skirmishes and then the boss, the count flat across tiers and the threat rising with them, never twenty two-round fights, which would make every build burst. Claude's targets until play says otherwise: a meaningful fight costs a four about a tenth of its hull, a battle 60 to 80% of a fresh squad's hull at competent play, the boss with its formation the exam, a battle twenty to twenty-five minutes. The enemy’s compositions and the tide produce the shape. Built 30 October (the pacing session): the Rupture meter fills with the menace of the kills (section 3's tally row) against three, four or five Fractures by the rift's tier, the thresholds fractions of the menace of the field at the start and of the rift’s first waves (as many of the cycle’s compositions as the tier has Fractures, at the ramp’s density: since the long wall the field is thin at the start), the last at two thirds of it and the steps growing (1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3), so later Fractures cost more, the thresholds follow the tier’s numbers and the ramp’s count without a table of their own, and the Rupture is earned by fighting the tide, never by sweeping a still field; a Fracture pays out as a fight ends, the log naming it and the elite the tier names emerging at the rift (the first, the first and third, the first, second and fourth); the last Fracture is the Rupture, which breaks the boss's seal, and the stall guard breaks it too when no pack but the boss's stands as a fight ends, so a team that cleared the field never waits on the clock. The prize a Fracture may bring is not built (Ryan, 24 October: the picks are the battle's roguelike part, “maybe not warranted at all”), and whether one rides each Fracture is decided after the meter is played, with the economy pass. Exit: the campaign runner's reads of Fractures reached, turns to the Rupture and the stall guard's share (docs/measurements.md, 30 October), and Ryan's play.

Decided

Three clocks, and what each prices.

Rift Pressure is time, the Rupture meter is progress, the gate is neglect (the pacing session, 29 and 30 October; Claude’s lean, Ryan: “your reads on everything seem great”). Since the long wall (6 November; Claude’s design, Ryan: “We are aligned, your reasoning is sound.”) the tide is the meter’s fuel: the battle opens with the siege under way, the field thin, the formations a move from the wall and the patrols on its flanks two moves out, the pressure a turn short of its first peak; the waves come on the beat and the elites at the Fractures; and the thresholds count the first waves. So progress is earned by holding the wall, and every turn asks which segment to hold, with how many machines. The reason: at a timer of twelve a bot battle ended before the clock fired (29 October), and a field standing full at the start was swept still, its first turns empty; a siege under way gives the first turn a decision (the escalation the clock’s, never the kills’: a Fracture brings the elite and nothing more, Claude’s third round, unmoved). The opening’s rows and pressure are placeholders. What reopens it: a read that the three pull the player the same way every battle.

Decided

The names: the Rupture meter, a Fracture, the Rupture.

Capitalised. A Fracture earned in a fight pays out when the fight ends.

Decided

A stall is priced, never free.

Ryan, 30 October: “As a player, I like tanking and tanky builds. I know that if allowed, I'll build a thick team that refuses to take hits and burns enemies down slowly. I also know that in this game, I don't want a 2-hour battle where I take no damage and get crazy amounts of farm to be a legitimate way to play.” The flood alone cannot answer it: with each peak shortening the next, a stall is finite but not unprofitable, every spawn a kill and every kill salvage, so the flood feeds the farm until the break, and a thick team at a five-hex apron kills it as it lands. So three prices that exist or are decided: the gate, through the tears (a pack landing on the apron strikes that turn, and the whole wall is struck, so a team holding the gate pays the fee whatever its shields); the yield, by menace (the reinforcements light); and the flood’s break. Since the long wall the tide prices it too, waves on a beat of three turns, two late in a run and halved at the Rupture, and every fight is fought to its end, the pack’s fall or the machines’ wreck (the withdrawal row), the runner counting a stalemate at ten rounds with nothing changed. The step inside a battle, each peak shortening the next, is on the table as a ramp entity at zero until the turtle read asks for it (Ryan: the escalation rate “should be on the table”, since “a team that specs for it could maybe stall better than a team that doesn't”; the flood's bite is relative to the team's power). Two design tests: the flood breaks any team at about the same turn whatever its kit, read on the thickest fours; a fought battle meets one or two tears, a stall five. Refused, with the reason: a loss when the seam reaches the gate (a round cap by another name, and a loss with nothing to fight is the helplessness section 2 removes), and a forced boss (Loop Hero's: the boss's fight takes every machine and nobody leaves, and there is no battle withdrawal to retreat into). On the bench, each with the rule it bends: the battle's salvage capped at the rift's bounty and shown on the card, against section 10's kill row, the cleanest kill of the farm if the weighting leaves it worth doing; a long battle bleeding the other fronts, against a turn being one battle; the seam swallowing the map's loot as it advances (FTL's fleet), the one form that adds a decision, waiting on loot lying on the map. Exit: a turtle’s read on the campaign runner decides whether the step moves and whether the bounty cap leaves the bench; the turtle persona (docs/measurements.md, 30 October) went with the defence rule (6 November), so the read waits on the runner’s player (the to-do).

Decided

Rift Pressure feeds the boss fight only by what the rift itself sends.

Since the long wall nothing joins a fight (the fights-picked row), so what the rift sends reaches the boss’s fight only as the hull it has cost on the way: the price of lateness is the wall’s and the hull’s, bounded, and no boss grows harder with the clock. The tears carried the flood off the rift’s ring for the same reason (30 October), when a late engage still met the rift’s last composition in the boss’s fight; before them, every spawn landed beside the boss and a rush would have raced a boss growing harder, which hung on in-battle rewards that did not exist.

Decided

Tears: the mouths beside the rift's, on the ramp.

Ryan's name (30 October) and his shape: “The Battle starts with just the Rift. As it ramps and the bleed pushes towards the gate, more micro rifts open, and stay open, creating more spawn points without crowding and the packs getting hung up on one another. A hunter spawning at a forward location is more favourable for the hunter, just like the pushers have more threat the closer they are to the gate. The most threatening formations can spawn from the main, large rift, but spreading other rifts across the X axis of the map makes it harder for the player to stay grouped, which forces riskier detachments to prevent chip damage from stray packs.” Built the same day: one clock, Rift Pressure, with more mouths as the ramp rises. At a peak the rift sends the cycle's next composition, every tear open before the peak sends the patrol cycle's next, the seam creeps south, and the ramp's count of new tears open on the seam's row, each on a free land hex far from the other mouths and no machine beside it, to send from the next peak, so where the next flood comes from is known an interval ahead (Ryan's question, “do you mean the tear appears before spawning a unit?”, taken: a tear opens a peak before it first sends). Tears stay open. A spawn lands at the end of the enemy's turn on the nearest free hex to its mouth with no machine adjacent, so the player sees it and its walk for a whole turn and nothing appears on top of a detachment. Since the long wall (Ryan, 5 November: “every hex closer to the wall matters so much more”) the field is twelve deep, the seam starts on the first row past the rift’s mouth and creeps two rows a peak as far as the apron, so each peak’s tears open nearer the wall and matter more for it, and on a wall twenty long a tear on a flank is several moves from the squad. The hole named: at twelve deep the seam reaches the apron by the second peak, so later tears open beside the wall; the creep is a placeholder the runner reads. Exit: Ryan’s read of the tears and the seam running on the phone, and the campaign runner’s reads by tears open and gate damage.

Open

The landscape twisted by the rift.

Ground shaped to funnel the horde; rift tiles per domain (bone yards, lava, the nether) as real hexes with rules, commoner near the rift. A flyer weakness, if wanted, lives only on a rift tile. If a skirmish ever reads terrain, it reads the ground the enemy paints and never our own (Ryan, 30 October: “If there’s ever a skirmish terrain effect, it will be on enemy painted terrain, never normal terrain.”); height is a look alone (the crossing row). Ryan, 29 October: art only for now, no rule on a rift tile (“I don't know how much mechanics we want to cram into a feature that only becomes relevant late into a battle, plus the fact that the foe carries a flattened area with them would make it very hard to play around. Goes against the simplicity we just gained. I will think about what makes sense.”). Decided within it, 30 October: the flattening is the clock's picture. The seam, the row the rift's bleed reaches, creeps south two rows a peak of Rift Pressure as far as the apron, the tears open on it, and no rule reads the flattened hexes; a loss when it reaches the gate and a rule on the flattened ground are refused (the stall row). What stays open is the rift tiles' own rules, if any, Ryan's.

Open

The round's numbers by play.

A hunter’s ring, the attack range (one since the long wall, a placeholder), the hunters’ moves, the fights’ sizes. The rift sends formations since 30 October (compositions, section 5's pack row), and a dedicated hunter foe landed as the Reaper, an elite placeholder for Ryan's marks (section 9's tiers row).

Pinned

A chassis passive that moves after a fight; a map taunt for a slow Arsenal bruiser.

The round stays move, then fight.

5

Skirmish layer

The skirmish is where the depth lives. Its rules (the round, dice, reach, keywords, buffs and debuffs, mitigation, heat, injury, foes) are in docs/rules.md; this section says why they are so.

Shape

Decided

The player side has no positioning, and the round is one interleaved phase.

Roll, lock, spend and reroll in any order before the enemy acts. A zoomed-in hex grid was set aside: it repeats the battlefield's decision and fights thumbs in portrait. Built, played, and Decided 4 November.

Decided

The enemy stands in two ranks of four, never wider.

A phone-readability cap. Ranks are enemy-side only, bodies in front and problems behind, and they hold.

Decided

Foes strike in the order they stand, and the heaviest hitters deploy left.

No initiative numbers.

Decided

Reinforcements fill gaps at the end of each enemy turn, and a kill clears its slot at once.

A brief window on the back rank.

Decided

The reinforcement phase is the mine's alone.

Nothing the player does acts on the step where waiting foes take the empty slots but a laid shot waiting there (Ryan, 4 November: “that's the mine's niche alone”). What reopens it: a piece whose fantasy is the trap and that is not a mine.

Decided

No round limit.

A cap punishes defensive and status builds, and so did a Rift Pressure tick per round (section 4). Tuning keeps a fight finite, enemy damage outpacing sustain and every formation able to end a fight (the withdrawal row), with a meaningful fight five to eight rounds (section 4's pacing row); a stall, which the runner marks past sixty rounds, is a number to tune, never a clock.

Decided

There is no withdrawal: a fight ends in the pack’s fall or the machines’ wreck.

Ryan, 6 November: “what if there's just NO withdrawal? Tactical options get removed, obviously, but it does simplify things a huge amount.” So the price of a bad commitment is the wreck, and two guards keep it fair: the threat rating, shown on every pack before it is engaged (the pack row), and every formation able to end a fight, which is content’s (the runner counts the stalemates). Retired: withdrawal at any point of the player’s turn for one enemy turn (13 October), and with it the detachment’s bail-out and hit-and-run farming; the test it failed is Ryan’s, a tactical option not worth the rules it took. What reopens it: a fight the player could not have judged before engaging it, in his play.

Decided

The enemy's turn replays blow by blow.

Parting blows included, before the next dice or the outcome. The beat is the ENEMY PACE setting. The player's spends land the same way, a strike at a time (Ryan, 2 November: “flechette pod and other multistrikes need to clearly show the strikes in order, with individual ticks of damage. The damage animation is individual, but the damage is not - damage needs to be timed to the animation across the board”): each strike fires on its own beat, its blows land with it, every gauge stepping down as its blow lands with the blow's number rising off the foe, a killed foe standing until its killing blow, a last act's damage landing as it is named; the gap between strikes is a placeholder to judge running. Each foe is lit as it acts, a violet glow behind it and heavy corners breathing round it, from a part of the beat before its move lands until the next foe's lights (Ryan, 2 November: “enemies need to highlight during their attacks, starting right before”), the lead taken from the beat after the last blow so the turn keeps its pace; RIFTGATE_ACT_DEBUG=1 cycles it to judge running.

Decided

Every kill counts, however a fight ends.

Toward salvage and the Rupture meter. Defeat wrecks the machines (section 3).

Weapons

Decided

Rolled damage is gone: a weapon is one number.

Ryan, 2 November, decided in play testing: “I want rolled damage gone for sure.” The form that went: a range rolled as the blow lands, a draw after the decision, which hid the number from the round preview (the copy could only count the lowest roll) and cut an undo at every spend of a rolled gun (the randomness pass of 2 November, section 2’s randomness row). Every range landed at its mean, the halves rounded up (Ryan, 3 November: “Round up is fine”; the Twin Autoguns and the Ankle Mortar 2, the Slugthrower, the Howitzer, the Arquebus and the Salvo 4, the Maxigun, the Harpoon and the Airburst 2, the Chainsaw Claw 3, the Breaching Plow and the Flechette Pod 4), to be read again at 200 seeds. What the old row keeps: heat is a resource and a balancing attribute beyond damage, the secondary cost beside a cooldown (section 8; Ryan, 13 October), set by the component’s niche like its reach; reliable against swingy is no corner any more, so a cool gun against a hot one is the choice heat alone makes. The Muzzle Velocity Booster and the Tactical Databank, which moved a range’s ends, are planned until they have rules. A blow’s sound follows its tags since the same day (a Beam barrel a laser, a Plasma one a plasma, the rest by shape), rolled-or-flat having been its label.

Decided

No weapon classes, no crits, no Execute, no Steady.

Each failed a test of section 2, and the test is the rule: crits put a chance on the damage; Execute was a binary kill; Steady was an average, a number dressed as a rule; classes asked for parity. A new idea that is none of those is free to be proposed. Chances live only on riders. Tags carry no rule of their own (section 7). Damage that grows with the target's missing hull is a unique rule on one piece today (the Coriolis Accelerator); a second carrier would make it a keyword, as with the Boar's spill. A foe that gets back up is answered by Suppress, Disrupt and kill order.

Decided

Direct and Indirect, and no falloff; Sweep is the third, a different question.

Direct reaches the back only through a hole; Indirect reaches anyone. Close, a worse Direct whose price existed only against a back rank, went in October: two archetypes of who may be struck are plenty. Sweep (3 November, the row below) answers where the strikes go, not who may be struck, so it is no third archetype of the first kind. Melee is a tag; the old close weapons came down to Direct's band with the stock pass.

Decided

Reach is priced.

At equal heat Direct hits harder than Indirect, unless the runner shows heat is the better price for Indirect: the numbers carry the price (measured 11 October, docs/measurements.md).

Decided

Indirect is reach only, and area belongs to a keyword.

Ryan, 11 October, agreeing the line-up's proposal: danger close goes, so a beam or a phased emitter can be Indirect without exploding, and an explosive says so with an area keyword on any reach; danger close priced nothing and only steered Indirect toward the back. Gone since 22 October, the Artillery Rangefinder's rule that spared it replaced by the Ram Frame's mirror (the catalogue row); which explosives carry the area keywords is the row below's.

Decided

Sweep is a reach: a rank picked, its filled slots walked, no strike wasted on an empty column.

Ryan, 2 November, in place of the spray’s random darts and the salvo’s random targets (the randomness pass): “I would make target a rank, not a unit. Starts left to right always.” Built as a keyword on the 3rd and moved the same day on his word: “for simplicity's sake, let's move sweep from a keyword to a targeting mode, since that's what it is. Not direct or indirect. You pick a rank and that's the rank it hits. Empty columns at any point are skipped, so it's allowed to concentrate fire on the remainder. That's what differentiates it from spread or a potential hit-all, in that the shots aren't ever wasted. Yes, this means that the Skua gets indirect targeting after a fashion, and I think that's ok for its role.” So the entry is dropped on any foe and its strikes walk that foe’s rank from its first filled slot, left to right, one a slot, wrapping; a kill clears its slot at once and the walk skips it; each strike is aimed at its foe, so a Shock there pops, the riders land and an area keyword’s shape rides each (a Charged Flechette Pod shocks the whole rank: “That's the flechette pods exact purpose. Spread status wide.”). The die shows the reach as its own mark, and no passive turns a sweep Direct or Indirect. The Skua’s Flechette Pod and the Musk Ox’s salvo, an ammo a strike, carry it. A rank emptied mid-walk loses the strikes left (Ryan, the same day: “if the rank dies, the attack dies, yes”). While a sweep is dragged over a foe, the HUD lights that foe’s rank whole and brackets it, so the drop reads as the rank it is (“fix the UI for sweep so its intuitively targeting a rank”).

Decided

Area by shape, never by strength.

Ryan, 11 and 12 October: three shapes, three keywords, Penetrate the column, Spread the row (the neighbours, never the whole row, so the aim still matters) and Splash the plus, each a footprint with its own aim; an explosive needs no price on the firer (measured 12 October, docs/measurements.md). The number the foes past the aimed one take is the keyword's, printed: Spread N, Penetrate N and Splash N (Ryan, 28 October: “it fixes the most issues and opens the most design space”, big hitters that chip their neighbours as shrapnel does, and dedicated area pieces that clear trash with flatter spreads), read on the die as Burn N is, each piece's own number, and a piece carrying the row and the column carries Splash N; the firer's modifiers and consumers then land on N as on the aimed foe's blow, and each foe struck reads its own (the blow's order, below). Retired 28 October: the half, rounded down, decided at the old scale as a proportional price (three points on the Badger, 11 October) and built 26 October at single digits, where it failed the kill-time test's arithmetic: a neighbour's 1 or 2 kills no rank and file at full HP, so it was a removal and not a half, and rounding up or a fixed 2 read the same as it (docs/measurements.md, 27 October); whole area, the other form, made the carriers must-picks (the 23 October grid) and the aim moot on a swarm, a price on the firer buying nothing at 100% wins. Each carrier's N is content's, a placeholder set 28 October on Ryan's go from Claude's proposals in chat. A debuff's area carries no number, and the validator holds every weapon's to one. Built 28 October. Chain is the fourth footprint since 28 October, the conductors, a status in place of a geometry (the Chain row below); area is no part of Kestrel's brief, so the Kite is never balanced around it. A reach over every foe, Every Foe, and its mirror on the team's side, Every Ally, are targeting modes (Ryan, 4 November: “Silence should not narrow targeting modes, that's why I'm switching them to targeting modes”; working names after the foes' Every Machine): each its own mark in the die's targeting slot, never a chip, and a Silence, which mutes keywords, leaves them whole, where the shapes stay keywords, an area round the target a reach picks. Retired with that: the two as keywords of 2 November (“Every foe is a targeting mode, just like spread, penetrate, and splash, it should be listed as such and given a keyword”), which a Silence narrowed to the one target, the test they failed. Every Foe stays a debuff's (the Heliographic Siphon's lure-all), since a weapon reaches wide by a shape with its number, and no passive turns it into Direct or Indirect; Every Ally is a shield's or a buff's, never a vent's, in two modes, the whole team or every machine but its own, Every Other Ally (the Mooring Screens'; Ryan, 4 November: “the difference is whether the center one is lit or not, its two modes, even if the ‘every ally’ doesn't actually exist in game, yet”). Built 2 November as keywords, 4 November as targeting modes. The column keywords earn their keep once the foes' fresh start puts back ranks in more fights.

Decided

Chain is Shock's other payoff: the blow arcs to every Shocked foe on the field.

Ryan, 28 October, on Claude's proposal in chat; its form 29 October, his pick from six looks measured that day. Chain N: the blow lands on the foe aimed at as ever, and N on every other Shocked foe on the field, wherever it stands and whatever the gun's reach, the firer's modifiers riding each arc; no strike is aimed at a conductor, so its Shock neither doubles the N nor is spent (Shocked in his words the same day: “when targeted and struck by a weapon, double the damage recieved and remove Shock”; the foe a vent's blast, a random hit or a spray's strike draws is targeted too, by the game rather than the player, so it reads Shock: 2 November, “still targeted, just not by a player”, “it's not an AoE”). What it sells is a conversion of single-target burst into area, and it costs the setup, a Shock and the arc, “so it needs to be worth the two spends”; its Shock comes from the setters, a Shock weapon, Retaliate and Charged (the primers row below). The decision it makes: which charge an aimed strike cashes, each one cashed a conductor fewer for the next arc, and when to arc; over rounds, since Shocked never fades, the field charges, which the foes' Cleanse and Ward answer. A fourth footprint beside Spread, Penetrate and Splash, a status in place of a geometry, numbered as they are; each is read from the strike's own foe, so an arc spreads nowhere and a foe an area reaches never arcs, and a foe both beside the target and Shocked takes one blow, the area's (Ryan, 29 October: “Chain is an AoE shape, so it doesn't proc on spread/penetrate/splash procs, right?”). A playstyle, “the thinking man's AoE”, and Aphelion's lean (the maker row of section 7): most Shock and Chain setters land in its content, any maker crossing over as usual, the Arc Fault Cannon the first carrier; the amps that ride every blow ride every arc (the catalogue line). Retired 3 November: the charge, one other foe at random after the arcs, neither the target nor one already Shocked (29 October to 2 November; Ryan then: “This random is ok by my books. Thematically, it's lightning, and it should be harder to control”, the draw its cost; and on 2 November, as the randomness pass weighed every draw, with the primer proposed in its place: “Chain just is the situational aoe, and if weak we can either add more things for it to proc on, or give it the self-charge again”), so the Shock supply is a kit choice and Charged the primer; what reopens the self-charge: Chain reading weak on the ladder, or Flak as a second conductor. Claude's spatial charge (the nearest foe without Shock) was declined the same day: “a position based mode kind of cheapens the identity”, lightning walking to the guy on the right reads as arbitrary. Retired 29 October: the arc that doubled and spent a conductor's charge, built the 28th and read as a plain 4 on today's kits (another machine's doubled blow spent nearly every charge before the arc fired), and with it “nothing charges itself” (Ryan, 28 October: “Don't let it charge itself”), which his pick of the self-fuelling look reopened for the charge's day and the charge's retirement closed again. Retired 28 October: the kill and repeat (Ryan: “a no-go, its unbalanced at best and janky at worst”; it was also the Kite's identity on a chassis, which keeps it), and the ping, an on-hit on a random foe for half, since a random target is noise and a half is area by strength. Built 29 October (the conductor from its brief, retired, git show 0cea0ee:docs/briefs/chain_conductor.md; the charge and Shock's reading the same day). Read 29 October (docs/measurements.md, Chain on its form of the 29th): Chain 2 is worth about 0.8 vanilla points over the sixteen big fights (the Arc Fault about 4.8, inside tier one's band), nothing against a lone massive foe and about 2 on the formations, where it outruns a vanilla 6 (3.4 with a spread setter); the charge carries it, since the team's aimed strikes cash each charge within the round and the arcs land on a few spends in a hundred, so the charges never pile up on today's kits. Chain N is each weapon's own number, priced as Spread's is: on the Arc Fault, a 4, the keyword reads about 0.75 vanilla points at Chain 2, 0.9 at 3 and 0.95 at 4 over the sixteen (2.2, 2.6 and 2.8 on the formations), a point of N pricing only the arcs, which are rare while the team cashes the charges, and the charge, which no N moves, the rest; read again when a spread setter and a player who holds charges make the arcs common. The Arc Fault keeps its Chain 2, and the Buzzard its stock, a Chain gun its own setter (Ryan, 29 October: “Keep the Buzzard stock”).

Decided

The primers: Charged and Supercooled ride the weapon fired as its keywords, a stack a fire.

Ryan, 2 November, from the rider Claude proposed for Chain’s charge: “what would be even cooler is if it applied to the next weapon fired, not next foe struck. At that point, though, we’ve made a new version of Primed for shock, which is great, but I feel like doesn’t need to get baked into Chain by default. Paired with, maybe, but not part of.” A machine buff with stacks: while it lasts the machine’s weapons fire with Shock (Charged) or with Cryo, one more a strike (Supercooled), the keyword on the die and in the hit’s line, landing as the keyword does, after the blows; each weapon fired or laid spends a stack, a salvo one, a blast none, equipment nothing. On weapon fire and never per hit (his question the same day): per hit, every strikes gun would double or cool itself down its own strikes, and the count would mean something different on every gun; per fire, “Supercooled ×2” reads the same everywhere, the next two weapons. As the keyword rather than a mark on the first strike’s foe (3 November): “Cryo should work the same way, resulting in 2 applications, not apply-cash-apply-cash, and not hit-hit-apply once, since that just removes the purpose of multi-hit strikes (weak against armour, good with riders)”, so a strikes gun gets a stack a strike and a sweep marks every foe it walks (Ryan: “That's the flechette pods exact purpose. Spread status wide.”). Supercooled lays one a strike (Ryan: “laying 1 cryo at a time makes sense, keeps it a little unique, and avoids massive cooling spikes”), the name his pick. No piece grants either yet: capacitor pieces are content to propose.

Decided

The blow's order: the firer's modifiers on every blow, each foe struck read for itself.

Ryan, 28 October: the base for each foe hit (the roll or the flat number for the foe aimed at, the keyword's N for the others); then the firing unit's modifiers and consumers on each strike and each foe, flat then multiplied (Empowered, hopper loads, Spun Up, the Cassowary's sixth shot, the Kite's doubling: “a consumer from the firing unit should apply to the whole attack”); then the struck unit's debuffs and the consumers that read it, each foe for itself, flat then multiplied (Flak, then Shocked last, read only by a weapon's strike aimed at the foe, never by a blow that only reaches it: Ryan, 29 October; the Backdraft Charge eats the stacks on each foe it hits and pays that foe alone: “If only one unit is on fire, that's the only one that gets spiked”; the Coriolis's bonus each foe's own missing HP at the moment it is struck); then the struck unit's armour and buffs, each for itself (Armoured; Exposed zeroing it); then the final damage. One formula on both ends, and the rule the mechanics audit's 9 and 19 found missing, where the firer's modifiers were read once on the aimed foe and that blow carried to the rest. The Kite's double stays on the printed number with the entry's own reads inside it (an area's N, heat fired, hopper loads, each foe's own missing HP and stacks consumed) and the buffs added once after (Ryan, 28 October: “keep the kite how it is, my version would make it stronger and I'm not sure it needs to be”); doubling after the firer's flat adds, his first reading of the order, is its buff if it ever needs one (“Note it as a buff target if it ever needs one”). A foe's own reads (its missing HP, the stacks a consumer eats on it) land after the firer's multiplies, flat, beside its Flak, as his chain has them (Ryan, 28 October, on the question whether they sat inside the printed number: “The die should show the calculated result after going through the described chain. There should be no ambiguity”), so neither the Kite's double nor the sixth shot touches them. A foe's reads are taken as the die is spent, and every blow of a strike is read before any lands, so a spend never feeds its own read and a kill feeds the next strike, never the same strike's other blows. Built 28 October.

Decided

A spend’s riders land after its strikes, one a strike; no strike cashes or feeds on what its spend lays.

Ryan, 3 November, on the primers and the strikes guns: “a multi-hit weapon with Shock would apply it after both hits, that’s how a player would expect it. Not apply shock, then cash and apply again on second hit. Cryo should work the same way, resulting in 2 applications, not apply-cash-apply-cash, and not hit-hit-apply once, since that just removes the purpose of multi-hit strikes (weak against armour, good with riders). That’s how I would expect it to work as a player, and if not, the first time you do it its immediately obvious. The other shapes aren’t as immediately obvious what happened.” So each strike’s Cryo and Flak gather on its own foe and land once the strikes are done, an application a strike: a ×2 Cryo 1 leaves Cryo 2 and cools nothing, a ×3 Flak 1 lands 5, 5 and 5 and leaves Flak 3. Shock already landed after the blow. Burn, Suppress, Web, a passive's lays, Purge and a Primed machine's Burning land the same way (Ryan, the same day, on a first cut that left them a strike at a time: “things do read that. Baboon for instance. It should be standard, not branching code”): one gathered list, landed in order once the strikes are done; what a felled foe takes is the death-last row below. Retired with it: Flak’s later strikes feeding on the first’s stacks (21 October, a code reading, never a row), the apply-feed-apply shape he names; Ryan, 3 November: “The flak interaction is exactly the intuition that I meant. 5,5,5 makes way more sense to me.”

Decided

Death is the last thing checked in a spend and in the prediction; the enemy’s turn is sequenced as it happens.

Ryan, 3 November, on a first cut that kept Shock, Expose, Lure and Disrupt for the foes a blow left standing: “Shock and expose should apply too. And lure should show the altered target. Basically, everything, the point is that ‘death’ is just another chip, and is the last thing ever to be checked - the whole attack, or whole turn if it's a predicted death from burn or the like, should be played out. That way, we never accidentally miss a trigger for a passive or other effect, there's no exceptions flying around, and the rules and code stays clean.” So no effect of a spend reads whether its foe still stands: every strike lands on its foe, felled or not, with its riders, its Shock, its Expose and its Lure’s turn, the round preview shows every chip beside the skull (his 26 October: the skull “is just another predicted chip”), a felled foe’s gained status still fires the Baboon’s Paradox, and the dead are cleared once the spend is done. Where the next strike goes still reads the field: a sweep walks past an emptied slot (his Sweep row). On the 4th, on a cut that read the words literally and let a foe burnt to death at its tick strike anyway: “What I meant about ‘death last’ was in regards to the player turn prediction, not the enemy turn sequencing. The prediction has to be done in phases, if burn ticks first and they're predicted to die from it, we still show the intent since that is still possibly relevant information that would be confusing if not there (say, an effect that grants something based on how many intents targeting a unit. Doesn't matter if those intents are actually going to land or not). What we absolutely do not want to do is have a zero-hp enemy attacking and then dying after, because burn ticked first. Use logic, not literal interpretation to fill in holes.” So the enemy’s turn stays sequenced as it happens: a foe its tick kills never strikes, a wreck takes no more strikes, and the prediction keeps a doomed foe’s intent shown while leaving its blow out. Retired: a kill denying a spend’s riders, Shock and lure, which the forecast read as exceptions. Whether a foe’s ticks come before or after its action is section 12’s question (his: “it's a fair point”).

Decided

The whole die is in view in the codex; tapping a die shows the entry showing.

A reroll needs the whole die in view, so the codex, a double-tap on the machine, lists every entry; the die's readout, a tap, turns the entry showing into text, a number a passive moved highlighted there and never its source listed (the tap-hold-drag row, Ryan, 25 and 26 October); passives belong to the machine.

Control and statuses

Decided

Burn never stops healing.

Damage over time soft-counters healing, taking back some or all of what is healed.

Decided

The Pocket Paradox is a meter.

It counts applications of other statuses, never stacks, so tripping it takes several components or a hit carrying several statuses, and it tunes by its threshold. A chance to suppress was binary; a debuff that zeroes damage was toxic against fodder and bosses alike. A trip that Numbed resists still spends the meter, so it never sits primed to try again, and so does a trip on a foe already suppressed (Ryan, 17 October: "Suppressed is suppressed - they lose their next action. It does not stack, it does not carry. That way lies madness"). Its existence never dictates another design: a status that feeds it counts, and the count is tuned for the final ecosystem (Ryan, 17 October).

Decided

Numbed is a buff nothing cleanses, and only a Purge touches it.

A buff with a cannot-be-removed line, not a kind of its own (Ryan, 18 October: its own kind was one type too many, "no reason to have a billion types of objects"), which nothing reads, deepens, spreads or cleanses, and only a Purge lowers (2 November, a buff like the rest): a stun resistance that effects can change is where other games' combat loops broke. It decays, so rotating stuns is a slope, not a wall, and its slope says spread the stuns. A foe's resist is a count by its kind (Ryan, 3 November: "1 small, 2 massive, 3 for elites and bosses, by default"; content may set its own), never an innate figure on the chip, which shows the rounds. If a sure first stun on a boss proves too strong, this is revisited. A suppress on a foe already suppressed for that action does nothing and numbs it no further (Ryan, 17 October: Suppressed never stacks and never carries, a Paradox's trip included). Built 17 October; the defaults of 3 November retired those of 30 October (a boss and a massive foe three, the rest two). What reopens it: a foe that wants a number of its own (his “Maybe it's a per-unit thing?”, section 12).

Decided

A status stacks or renews, as its definition says.

Ryan, 17 October: renewal without stacking must be supported, "a pretty common thing for a powerful or non-number-related buff to do". The engine reads the policy off the status: add, the default, a stack a grant, a timed grant keeping its own end (Ryan, 16 October: "Timed stacks should decay based on when they started. Not stack and refresh."); renew, one grant whose end is the later and whose count the larger (Suppressed, Retaliate; and the untimed one-stack statuses, on which a renewal changes nothing: never stacking and renewing are one state, parted only by a timer). Section 6's table checks its stacking word against the policy. A status either runs down to zero on its own clock or is timed, never both (Ryan, 18 October: "Things either decrease to zero, or are timed, not both"), and the validator refuses rounds on a halving status. Built 17 and 18 October.

Decided

Armoured and Empowered count in points, and Empowered halves.

Ryan, 17 and 18 October. Both sides say Armoured, and a stack is an armour point, so the chip's count is the armour ("Easy to read at one glance"); its duration is the row below's. Empowered is a damage point a stack and halves at the end of each round, rounded down, whatever granted it, until it is gone: a stack fading a round let a source feeding two a round climb without limit, where a halving one settles near twice its feed ("to prevent stacking to the moon and fueling massive ramp strategies"), and Ryan would grant it to more things on that clock. Rounded down, because a status decays to zero or is timed, never both (the row above): rounded up, one stack would never end. The Ammit's Quintessence Tap keeps every Empowered on it for the battle whatever granted it (Ryan, 18 October: "that's the rules text, it doesn't care where it came from"), never between battles, everything resetting at the hangar; its release valve was to be an enemy silence or purge (Ryan: ok); since 28 October no purge takes the Tap's stacks (the silence and purge row), so should the Ammit "prove stupid overpowered" at single digits, where each stack is worth more, the valve is the to-do's test and nothing else is considered before it. The Overcharge Coil grants 2 (Ryan, 18 and 22 October: +10 for a round was "silly high" for a support action spent the same turn as a combo, and at 1 the halving leaves next to nothing). The halving stays the clock of every status fed by events (Ryan, 22 October): a stack a round climbs without limit under a steady feed, so one clock everywhere would need a cap on every feed by another name. Built 18 October. Reopens if the count moving by halves reads badly on the chip in play, or if a buff wants a run-down the halving cannot give. Named Empowered on both sides since 3 November (Ryan: “Go with Empowered for now”), the foes’ rally buff too (Kindled until then), each side keeping its own clock as Barrier and Ward do: the machine’s halves each round, the foe’s goes with its next move; the ids overcharged and kindled stay.

Decided

Burn is a numbered keyword, and Burning a damage point a stack.

Ryan, 18 October: "It should be fixed to a numbered keyword, like a weapon applies 'Burn 2' for 2 stacks, irregardless of damage. Also, in the spirit of flattening numbers, burn should only do 1 damage per stack." So an entry with Burn says its stacks (Burn 3: three stacks of Burning on each foe hit), the first numbered keyword; a foe's Burning loses a damage point a stack at its action, so Burn 3 is 3, 2, 1 over three actions. On a machine a stack is a heat point (section 11's scales row). Each piece's number is content's. Built 18 October.

Decided

Stun is the best mechanic in any game, so adding hard control is always weighed first.

Ryan, 13 October: Suppress is prevalent and needs dialling back, and designs kept reaching for a stun or a burn in place of build space. The roster analysis's caps (no hard-control piece below tier two, one locked on a die, one hard-control passive a maker) were struck as artificially limiting (Ryan, 15 October) for a rule: adding hard control is always carefully considered, and other options explored, before it is suggested or committed to. Ryan, 17 October: a cooldown is no price for a Suppress, since Numbed is in effect one (on one foe the landed rate settles near one stun in three rounds however often it is tried); the more mitigation the game adds, the more Suppress must be priced so the other options stay attractive, since scarcity would make its carriers must-picks; without hard control a tank can become required, so damage must stay a viable way to win, Suppress may be allowed more widely if gated by a condition worked for or a price paid, and off-tank mitigation (a Barrier) is part of the answer. Ryan, 18 October, on what prices it: not the damage it deals, which is incidental, since a stun's worth is a turn where the party is not under threat (Darkest Dungeon's lesson, taken less far); so a Suppress is gated by a condition, a setup, a self-sacrifice or its timing, and the player decides whether to build a team that makes it worthwhile. The Paradox is the model. Retired: a Suppress debuff (Claude's), which priced the stun where it is weakest, on fodder, and not where it is strongest, on a boss.

Ryan, 22 October: no Suppress piece sits in any chassis's stock, the Baboon's least of all ("no reason for it to be able to stock a suppress that powers a suppress, that should be a build choice the player has to make"), and none below tier two, by reason rather than cap, so a gated stun is a find and the team that uses it is built for it. The stuns by gate: the funnel-web's web by its timing and the Paradox by setup; the Umbra Inducer by Volatile, the Arsenal's at tier three ("probably") with no condition, its hull price compounding with the Arsenal's other Volatile pieces; the Backdraft Charge, the Arsenal's at tier two, a consumer of Burning (a point a stack consumed, and a Suppress when it consumes two or more, Claude's fill, so one Burn 2 piece opens it), each stun trading the fire's ticks for the turn of safety; and the Suppression Battery, Wainwright & Sutler's at tier two, out of the Clydesdale's stock, Suppressing only a foe carrying Flak (Ryan: the maker's condition), its blow low on purpose. Built 22 October. The exits: a gate that leaves its stun unpicked loosens before the piece is cut, and a stun the runner shows as a must-pick gets a stiffer gate, never a cap.

Decided

The action economy: Rapid is priced by a gate, and each of its wants has a home of its own.

Ryan, 20 October: "rapid is turning into the new Suppress in terms of build ubiquity"; more Rapid pieces will come ("which we can and will do, but it will feel samey regardless"); "action economy is often a chase in these styles of games. Commander actions are one obvious area." Rapid sold five wants in one keyword and was the only component-level source of any of them, the Suppress pattern: another look at the die, a second action on one machine (setup then cash), spends counted (the line frame's combo, the Cassowary's sixth shot, the Paradox), a small effect that costs no turn, and tempo lent. The direction is the stun row's applied to tempo: Rapid is priced by a gate and never rationed, and each want gets a home that is not a Rapid piece. Another look: own rerolls as a number (a module or a passive), on the machines whose identity reads its die, priced so no module is the universal pick. A second action: the team's order before self-sequencing (an ally's action as the pair: the Dingo's Shock to the Kite's cash); a die mechanic where the sequence is the identity (the Cassowary's stepping); and where one machine must act twice, a price in a banked resource (rage, the hopper, Spun Up, Empowered, Kestrel's heat), a cooldown burst or a delayed action (the bomber's mines, the web). Spends counted: counters count blows, not spends, so strikes and area pieces are their fuel. The cantrip: combined entries, two effects on one spend (a vent that shields), with nothing to chain and nothing to limit. Tempo lent as a status is Rapid by another name, ranked last. Beyond the dice, commander actions on map-turn cooldowns (section 8): action economy nobody can stack.

Three rules of thumb for every design, rework and rebalance: a design that wants many blows is pointed at strikes; one that wants order is given a die mechanic; one that wants a second action pays in a banked resource, a cooldown or an ally's turn; Rapid stays for the cantrip, gated (if it kills, on a cold spend, once a round, by a cylinder). The stock pass applied it (22 October): the ungated carriers priced, the Laminar Deflector a tier-two blueprint by Ryan's rule that a Rapid shield is never below tier two and a Rapid vent never below tier three, the Wake Diffuser a vent that shields, every new Rapid gated. The exits: if the gates leave Rapid unattractive on every carrier, they loosen before the pool shrinks, since the keyword is a Kestrel lean and not a pest; if an own-reroll module becomes the universal pick, its number comes down or it moves to passives. The reasoning in full is docs/briefs/README.md's.

Decided

Purge strips a foe's buffs, and the foes carry buffs worth stripping.

Ryan, 12 October: the EMP is the heart of the Science Vessel homage, so a dispel is wanted as a keyword whatever the count. Its reach, widened 28 October ("having more than one purpose for a player purge is important though. Enemies can certainly carry offensive buffs such as primed in addition to kindled on more units, and defense buffs like barrier, ward, Regen, or a delayed heal or something, shouldn't be out of the question. Whatever you think adds healthy depth"): the foes' own buffs (Armoured from a brace, Empowered, Undying) and the machines' Primed, Barrier, Ward and Regenerating crossing to the foes under the same words (section 6's convention), each granted by a telegraphed special and each with an answer beside the purge (kill, suppress or disrupt the rallier; Expose or breach the brace; kill the reraised twice or mine its slot; Ward the stoker's target; pop a Barrier with a cheap blow; wait out a Ward; burst what regenerates), so a Purge is a decision and never the one key; a delayed heal is a countdown, Suppress's and Disrupt's. Guarded, the parts' rule, stays out of its reach; Numbed is in it since 2 November, a buff like the rest. Numbered the same day (Ryan: “I would keep it simple and lower everything by N and price accordingly”): Purge N takes N stacks off every buff, an unstackable one being a stack of one, on both sides, so it is never all-or-nothing and prices cheap and common, “a softer way on both sides to deal with armour and damage stacking in particular” (the Cinder Tortoise its first target); a Ward turns a Purge away and stands, on either side, where before the foe's Ward was one of the buffs it took, and a Ward turns a stun too, the exception that let a stun through retired with Numbed's roll. A keyword on any entry aimed at a foe: on a capstone's blow (the jellyfish's EMP) or alone on a debuff piece, Indirect, one at tier two by lean. Its name is Purge on both sides (Ryan, 20 October: "Use Purge for both, for now"), Strip the hangar's verb. No player silence: Suppress holds a special and Disrupt turns one, and the stun discussion kept control scarce. The set, its carriers by domain and the ladder they ride are git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md's, for Ryan's marks. Built 28 October with the ladder, its piece the Null Field Emitter (Aphelion's, tier two, an Indirect debuff in no stock, a blueprint found); read on pick share.

Decided

Silence mutes a machine's keywords, Purge takes what a die face bought, and a foe's Cleanse takes the team's marks off its own: the enemy's questions to what the tiers buy.

Ryan, 28 October, on Claude's analysis ("Agreed on silence, purge, and cleanse"). What the tiers buy is options and stacks (section 3's row, the measurement), and nothing on the enemy's side questioned either but killing faster, so these three are aimed at the archetypes rather than at everyone, each a domain's lean (section 9's levers row), each a special and never a rider on a blow (a tax with no decision), each a one-round window shown a round ahead with answers on the die: it costs time and never ability (Ryan, 17 October). Silence (Ryan, 27 October: it "makes sense to only disable (positive) keywords, and not touch buffs"): the machine it lands on is Silenced, its components' keywords muted through its next round, printed and granted alike (a Rapid acts once, a Spread lands one blow, a Suppress suppresses nothing, its own Cleanse a plain shield), its buffs counting and its stores filling as ever; cast with no condition, aimed at the armed; a Ward turns it away and an ally's Cleanse lifts it, the team's answer. Retired 28 October: buffs alone (25 and 26 October, built that day), a one-round purge that returns and so a duplicate; and the muting of 21 October that took Volatile's price with it, which Volatile as a cost (26 October) leaves nothing to mute. Purge (Ryan, 27 October: "not that threatening with the new mechanics we introduced, regarding resource bars and active buffs ... I think that would let purge be an actual mechanic and not limited to rare bosses"): the machine loses N stacks of every buff an entry's spend bought, for good (Purge N since 2 November, the row below; the Djinn's Unmake at 1), and N of what a store, a form or a passive grants (the Armadillo's seal's armour, the Badger's plate, the line frame's stacks) goes for the rest of the enemy's turn and comes back at the start of the player's turn after its start resolves, the cooling, the roll and the round-start passives first, "a mild punishment"; the stores stay (the store row); the Ammit's kept Empowered is its passive's own ("overcharge never decays and cannot be purged"), noted for testing at single digits where each stack is worth more (the to-do). Aimed at the buffed, cast where a machine carries one; a Ward turns it away; a Last Act never purges, since a death blow lands in the player's turn and would hold through the whole enemy turn after. Its placement is its threat: a special lands at the purger's place in the strike order, so a front-rank purger strips before the hitters and a back-rank one after them, and the Swap moves it. Carriers by lean the Waste's casters and the bosses, a tier-two and tier-three question, read on the buff pieces' pick share and the Cassowary's row (its Spun Up bought and carried) once a purging pack exists. A foe's Cleanse (Ryan, 28 October: "a domain lever - debuffs are hard here", the Marsh's): a healer's second job, a special on one of its own carrying a mark that takes every mark the team laid (Burning, Flak, Cryo, Vitriol, Shocked, Exposed, Webbed, the Paradox, the Drone), never Numbed (a buff a Purge lowers since 2 November) and never Suppressed (a cure that un-stuns an ally before it acts would kill the stun answer the 22 October discussion kept scarce); weight one, never twice running; its answers kill or suppress the cleanser first, spend the Shock before it acts, burst. One word for one effect on either side (section 6): Purge, Cleanse and Silence on both, Dispel a move's name (the Augur's, a purge special) and the engine's id. The front's card warns of a purge among the leaders (the mission map). Built 28 October with the ladder (section 9's recolour row), the silence's mute from the 21st's code; the first cleansers the ladder's Spectre and Wraith, and no foe silences until Ryan marks the carrier (the to-do); the Djinn's Unmake is the first purge (2 November). Exits: the duration and the carriers' weight if one bites too hard, never the exemptions. Bench: one verb for keywords and buffs alike, nothing permanent (the domains could not differ, and the stack question needs the bought buffs gone).

Decided

A store is a capped resource with a bar of its own.

Ryan, 21 October: "All stores have bars, since they are resources with a cap. A bar is under the heat display ... depending on how it fills. Being silenced does not prevent a unit from filling or using its stores (balance lever if silence doesn't hurt enough). If a bar grants an active effect, that effect is displayed as a buff. That effect can be silenced. If it would be purged, it effectively gets silenced for a turn instead." So a store (the Muster, the rage, the hopper, the Cassowary's shot count, the Musk Ox's magazine) is no buff: no Silence or Purge touches its filling or its use, and only an effect it grants is a buff. Since 28 October a silence touches no buff at all, so a store's effect sleeps under a purge alone, until the start of the player's turn after its start resolves (the silence and purge row). The shape follows the fill: the Muster a smooth bar in a segment a charge; the rage boxes up to its cap of five (Ryan, 22 October: "5 for the nemesis is fine"); the shot count its pips; the hopper boxes up to its cap with each load's armour a buff, as the Badger's rework has it; the line frame's combo a smooth bar with a tick for each power. The Ammit's kept Empowered is a buff and no store, and since 28 October its passive's own, which no purge takes (Ryan: "overcharge never decays and cannot be purged"; noted for testing at single digits). Built 21 and 22 October; the hopper's cap and its plate, the first store grant, 31 October with the Badger's rework.

Open

Buffs and debuffs in place of the word "status".

Polarity belongs to the effect, not the carrier: burn is bad on anyone, a damage boost good on anyone. Two kinds, buff and debuff, and no third (Ryan, 18 October): Numbed is a buff nothing removes; a banked one (Rage, the hopper, the Muster) is no status at all but a store, a capped resource with a bar as heat and the magazine are, and what a store grants is a buff (Ryan, 21 October: the row on stores). A Rapid component's switch-off is not a debuff and never cleanses. Ryan, 12 October: an Arsenal cleanse that moves a machine's debuffs onto a foe waits on this, since only a debuff that means something on either side can move; since 13 October one does, Burning, a heat tick on a machine and a hull tick on a foe (section 7).

Decided

Corrupted stays its own debuff.

It acts like Volatile, a spend that costs hull, but a foe puts it on, a cleanse takes it off and it ends with the skirmish, where Volatile is innate to its component (Ryan, 11 October).

Decided

Armoured lasts one turn of the other side, from every source today.

On a machine until the round's end, every stack at once; on a foe, from its brace, until its next action (Ryan, 16 October: "If it's effectively braced, it should be braced too"), so each guards against the other side's next turn of blows and neither lingers. A defensive buff that lingered, a stack fading a round, would stack into a wall (Ryan, 12 October), and armour is the dearest stat there is (section 10's prices). A longer Armoured is not banned: it needs a drawback of its own. Built 16 October.

Decided

A rally lands at once, Empowered halves on both sides, and the intents count it.

Ryan, 26 October, on the mechanics audit: "howl is more clear if it gives kindled for the next action, which could be the turn its granted. Makes it more difficult, certainly, but that's not a bad thing at this point. Kill the wolf if you can reach it, or if you can't, take out as much fodder that is going to hit you this turn, or get swung on hard"; and on how the player reads it: "The intents should take into account incoming buffs. They should have a pulsing version of the incoming buff as a preview chip, so the player knows it's incoming and that's the reason for the increased damage." So Empowered lands on every other foe on the field the moment the rally does: a foe acting after the rallier hits harder this turn, one that has acted carries half to the next round, since a foe's Empowered halves at the round's end as a machine's does (Ryan, 4 November, one clock for the one word: “we try and make it work. If it's unbalanceable, we go back to separate keywords with different clocks”, the exit; retired with it the next-move clock of 26 October, under which the move spent it whatever it was and a Suppress took it). Every intent counts the rallies on their way from the round's start and drops them the moment the rallier is killed, Suppressed or Disrupted, so the enemies' numbers stay exact (section 2's randomness row); a foe whose blow a rally lifts wears the coming Empowered as a chip on a dashed edge, its opacity pulsing on the gauges' clock (Ryan, 26 October: "Similar in concept to the health and shield bars"), never told by a hue alone. Empowered on a foe is the machines' Empowered under one word (3 November), an add before the move's factor: "Rules parallel on both sides, so that the player doesn't have to know two formulas", and "One formula means one damage formula followed by the whole game. Not that we need to display a formula. The text is irrelevant. If it's unintuitive that holy multiplies after, that's a problem either with the move itself, or a problem with how we display attacks." The form it retires: Empowered live from the next round, granted once the enemy's turn was over (13 October), which kept the intents exact by keeping a rally out of the turn it was announced in; the test it failed is clarity, a howl that lifted nothing until a round later. Built 26 October, the swarm's add moved before the factor with it (no number moved: every swarm move's factor is 1); the pulse's swing is a placeholder, judged running (RIFTGATE_PREVIEW_DEBUG=1). The exit: the runner's bands reading the Meadow's or the Peak's cells as a wall for the rally's sake.

Decided

Retaliation punishes the striker with a debuff; thorns need a drawback of their own.

Retaliate is a buff on the machine whose work is the debuff it leaves on whoever strikes its hull. Thorns, damage paid at the blow, scale with enemy blows, strong against a horde and useless against a boss, and on a tank they stack into a wall: dangerous rather than forbidden, a thorn is balanced only by a drawback it carries and never rides a megatank (Ryan, 12 October). Retribution is the Arsenal's strongest suit (a lean, section 7); a retaliation elsewhere needs a fantasy of its own. Shocked doubles the team's next weapon strike aimed at the foe, so the tank sets up kills, and does nothing else: the damage a Shocked striker once paid on every blow was thorns left in, on a tank, and it went. What reopens a reward for being struck (Ryan, 20 October, the Whip Scorpion's Siphon): the machine spends its own turn to be struck, deals nothing that turn, and the reward comes later and at random, so it is paid for in tempo, not the old discharge riding free on a turn the tank took anyway.

Decided

Each foe picks its own target as the round opens: at random by default, by an aim where its threat calls for one.

Shown and committed. Random is the baseline the numbers are balanced on, so they read the same whatever the player's composition; an aim serves a foe's threat and the response it asks for, on any foe: the Black Mage goes for the hottest machine, and a late-game rank and file might go for the lowest hull or pick its area move to hit the most. The foes pick in the order they strike and never over-commit: a machine the blows already picked would wreck, through its shield and armour, is passed over while another stands, Slice and Dice's rule. The pick is smart as it is made and never after (Ryan, 26 October: "there's a difference between setting the intents smartly, and using the preview to change intents"; Slice and Dice's foes avoid "a unit that is already prescribed to die, as of game state at the intent pick moment"): during the player's turn it moves only by a Lure, by its machine being Phased, or by a Disrupt whose foe finds its machine gone or Phased, or whose old move aimed at no one machine, so a brace turned into a blow picks afresh ("otherwise changing the ability should still keep its initial intended target"; "If disrupt would change a buff to an attack or something ... it should still retarget"); a foe whose machine has fallen when it acts strikes the next machine in line then, whatever its aim ("I disagree with breaking the next in line rule even for special targeting rules like black mage. More unpredictable info"). The round preview never moves one: "don't get stuck in a preview loop where a tank lures a bunch of foes, the lure predicts it to die, so then the subsequent attacks on it get retargeted, and then it looks like the lure didn't work". There is no taunt stat: redirection is a spend that shows on the intent (Lure), and there is never a dodge chance. Ryan, 24 October: "if every enemy has a targeting rule, it's impossible to get a true baseline that's agnostic of player composition", so "Take away, balance, then add where appropriate"; enemy AI rules the player feels they must learn add overhead and little depth, and a foe goes far on "this guy hits hard", "this guy protects his allies", "this guy heals", "this guy debuffs" and "this guy is going to kill my unit". And, the same day: "It's the baseline that needs to be random, not everything, otherwise we have nowhere to go other than adding stats to make harder enemy types. The targeting needs to serve the type of threat and the response required", while "sometimes a treant just hitting hard randomly is more than enough". Slice and Dice's foes target almost purely at random a larger party, and it "feels plenty fair and challenging". An aim is the curve's targeting lever (section 3). Retired: a sense on every foe, the exposed weighting its default, which read the team's shields and armour into every foe's numbers so that no baseline was agnostic of the player's composition, and the codex saying what each foe goes for. Built 24 October.

Decided

A foe's blow may land on every machine.

Ryan, 15 October: a gap the foe kit refresh filled, the storms first (Root Storm, Quake, Bone Storm and Cleave, each one blow on every machine at the factor its three used to share). A move tagged all ("Every Machine" in the codex) lands each of its strikes on every live machine in turn, left to right, with its riders on each, so no aim and no Lure moves it; its arrows reach every machine, the forecast counts it on each, and the plaque names it once. Its number is what each machine takes, so a move with it is priced well under a single blow from the same foe. A shield saves only the machine it stands on; the answers that stop the whole blow are at the striker: kill it first, Suppress it, Disrupt it.

Decided

Every threat shape has two answers or more, and none has all.

Lure, Suppress and Disrupt touch a telegraphed move; shield and armour take it; Breach beats the buffer by design, and a blow at every machine beats Lure. Cleanse stays while riders persist.

Decided

Damage redirection is common, never a tank's lock.

Lure today. A draw by an ally (an interpose) comes back only on a locked piece, never as a core mechanic (Ryan, 12 October): Guard was built and taken out because a standing draw made one machine the target of everything, which is the lock; a conditional draw on a locked piece passes (the Onryō's Pall is the proposal, the Warrior's Cover its mirror on the foes' side).

Pinned

A deterministic reflect as a boss effect.

Decided

Phased: cannot act, take damage or be targeted, and only before it acts.

Ryan, 20 October, on Claude's ruling: "cannot act, take damage, or be targeted" is the cleaner wording, and it holds until the round's end; granted only to a unit that has not acted this round, so the status always costs the action it protects (his first form, invulnerable now and the next action lost, offset the two halves).
