Not targetable means allies cannot vent or shield it either and a foe's intent on it re-picks, which moves the intent lines as a dock should without a lure; it still cools by its own cooling, so an overheated machine phases with its problem intact, and ticks and overheats still land. The one rule gates every grant of it: the Swallowtail's Mooring Screens, and Aphelion's planned pieces.

Mitigation, heat and injury

Decided

A shield absorbs its number, and the excess passes to the hull.

Ryan, 14 October. Under the old rule (a standing shield blocked a whole blow, however small) a small shield entry was secretly "negate one blow", worth the biggest blow it met rather than its number, and the content kept rediscovering it (small stand-up shields; a screen on every ally negating four blows for one spend). Now a shield is an honest number, a breach ignores it outright (the shield stands), and the one-blow block is a priced, single-target buff of its own (the Barrier, below). What follows: shield answers the big blow by its size, not by standing; the Peak's telegraph wants a shield sized to it or a Barrier; the Buzzard's regen and the Puffin's overshield are hit points. A shield is full as every fight opens and nothing returns it on the map: the field recharge went (Ryan, 6 November: “removing field recharge is fine”), and the shield is the fight’s as heat is (Claude’s answer, unmoved, to his “if there is in-battle recharge it's going to feel very disconnected”), so hull is the battle’s; a chassis’s shield regen is the buffer inside a fight (the triangle row). Built 21 October; the fight’s shield 6 November.

Decided

Barrier and Ward: the block is a buff, one machine at a time.

Ryan, 14 October, the upside of the honest shield. A Barrier on a machine means the next blow a foe lands on it deals no damage, so it never injures, until the round's end; its riders land, and a blow of nothing leaves it standing; a blow it takes shocks no striker. A Ward means no rider a foe lays lands on it that round, a blow's or a move's of its own, breach included; a blow's damage lands (Ryan, 25 October: "Should stop all riders, even from non-damaging attacks", after the mechanics audit found it passing every special by, the Peak's Eruption, Fira and Pyroclasm, the Drake's Stoke and the Summoner's Diamond Dust among them). Both up is the old whole block at twice its price. They answer different shapes, the Barrier the Peak's telegraph and a boss's blow, the Ward the Waste's breach and the Peak's scorch, so a team carries what its fronts ask. A Barrier is never granted to every ally at once: four blows negated for one spend is the exploit the shield rule removed. The Ward lasts the round because riders come in volume where the big blow comes alone (Ryan), and its worth is thin while few domains lay riders on machines, which the foes' fresh start may change. Ward is the buff's word, so the Oblation Ward is the Oblation Mantle and the Votive Ward the Votive Screen (Ryan, 15 October). Ryan, 17 October: Barrier and Ward want the same care as Suppress, and if a Barrier grows common the foes need more damaging riders to carry past it (damage over time, on-hit effects, a countdown's blow, a Barrier's intended prey). The carriers since the stock pass (22 October): the Blast Door (Minerva's) grants the Barrier and the Decoherence Veil (Aphelion's, a working name) the Ward, self or ally, tier one, in no stock. Built 21 October.

Decided

Armour is a flat number off every blow, set by role, and a blow it covers is nothing.

Shield answers the big blow, armour the swarm, and straight reduction stays the rule on both sides: a blow at or under the armour does nothing, a bigger one loses the armour, never a damage floor. Ryan, 21 and 22 October, at the single-digit scale (section 11), where a point of plate walls a whole class of blow. Base armour is priced by role, never by size ("I would price it based on role"): a tank 2, a heavy worker 1, a car, a hover, a flyer or a glass cannon 0; timed armour (a brace until the round's end, the Armadillo's seal until it opens) lifts a unit well past its base and is meant to wall ("armour numbers that are only reachable conditionally should allow complete blocks"). A standing point is the dear one, since it walls one more blow size for the whole game, so module armour is scarce, +1 at most, tier two or three, and paid in a big stat. The forms tried and the test each failed, so a new one is free to be proposed: a cap at half the blow (no wall anywhere, and not sharp enough at low armour for Ryan); the same cap with a Braced state walling ("Might as well have no base armour and the flat armour stack as an ability"); a window that stops a blow up to the armour and nothing at twice it ("armour is useless against a boss"); the armour squared over the blow ("the math is cryptic. You need a chart to understand it"); a percentage a point (it rounds to nothing at single digits); a saving throw (randomness the player cannot see or shift; Ryan: no); base armour folded into hull with Armoured the only armour (the Elemental's plate by nature goes with it). What answers a wall is the pack, not the rule: a breaching move (the breach row) and a rally's Empowered lifting a small blow over the plate, while a walled tank takes only its share of the blows unless it lures (a foe without an aim picks at random, section 5's aim row), so tanking is lure plus plate. On our side Flak and Empowered add before armour, Shock doubles before it, Exposed sets it to nothing, Burning and Vitriol pass it. What reopens it: the tank rows reading as free wins against packs with no breacher, or the bracing foes' cells reading as walls for a basics team, in the runner's bands.

Decided

The Boar keeps its spill, written out, and Breach is not its word.

Ryan, 12 October: the spill matches the Boar's brief and serves "a passive opens design space": it gives flexibility, which the runner cannot measure; if it proves weak it is buffed. No keyword the player learns while it is the Boar's alone (a second source granting it would make it one): the engine runs it as Spillover, a keyword the passive grants every weapon the Boar fires, written out on the die (the star, the passive's line in the die's readout, no chip). Ryan, 15 October: "All its damage or subsequent strikes spill over": the killing strike's excess and every strike after it carry into the foe behind. A carry past a kill stays the Boar's: a multi-hit weapon strikes its one target, because that is intuitive (Ryan). Breach is not the spill's word: it is one rider on both sides (the breach row). Built 15 and 22 October.

Decided

One breach: the blow ignores the shield and the armour, and the whole blow reaches the hull.

Ryan, 22 October: "Is there any reason to merge breach and pierce to simplify it into one anti-tank threat?" Yes, and it is merged. The whole roster goes to one front (section 3), so a rider that beats shields and another that beats plate would sort the makers' leans by domain, a tax on a roster that leans one way at a front it cannot avoid, where one straight-through threat is answered in play: the Ward turns it away, the Barrier eats one, the kill order puts the breacher first, and a seal is not spent while it stands. The rider sits on the move, not the foe, so a foe threatens armour exactly as far as its breaching move comes up, by the content's three dials: how often (a move's weight), when (below half, the finisher going through) and how far ahead (a countdown); the tank reads it each round off the intent chip, where every rider of the blow shows by its mark after its number, a numbered one with its stacks (Ryan, 2 November: “Enemy intents with a rider need to show that rider as part of the intent chip”; the breach was a “!” after the number until then). Set aside: Pierce N, a numbered rider ignoring N armour, which let a lance beat base plate but not a seal; a breach beats the seal whole, so sealing is a decision about who is still standing. Foes carry no shield, so the same word on our guns ignores a foe's armour: the Overclocked Railgun alone carries it (Ryan, 22 October), Kestrel's answer to plate. What it costs: the Waste becomes the anti-mitigation domain outright, the Barrier and the Ward its dear answers; plate keeps its showcase in every swarm that does not breach. A breach's whole blow counts toward an injury, so the breacher is also the injurer. The foes' carriers: the Waste the most, one mid foe in each other domain by fantasy, bosses mostly not, so the seal keeps its job against the big single blows. Built 22 October.

Decided

Machines cool at the start of the round and overheat at the end of the player's turn.

So a hot machine can vent first, and an overheat is the machine's own doing on its own turn: with volatile, the one way to die on your own turn. A foe adds no heat outright: its Burning lands at the end of the player's turn, before the overheat, and its Scorched heats only what the machine aims at a foe (below). An overheat is hull damage for every passive. Cooling stays well under a weapon's heat.

Decided

Heat bites where it is carried; a cold build pays elsewhere.

Ryan, 13 October. Heat starts every fight at zero and is never carried between fights (Ryan, 4 November, against heat as a battle resource: the skirmish’s own bite comes first), the shield’s rule too since 6 November (the shield row). Heat is the one cost that competes with damage on the same die (a cooldown is the other, printed on the pieces that carry one: section 8), and what makes a cool gun against a hot one a choice (rolled against flat was a preference, never a price, the effectiveness row of section 11, Ryan, 28 October, until rolled damage went on 2 November); priced per piece but never felt, it is decoration. Before the stock pass it was barely felt outside the Peak; the stock pass's loops lowered the maxima so the hot kits bite and the cold ones stay cold (22 October, docs/measurements.md). The test, Claude's placeholders until play: a build that runs hot overheats by about its third round of firing without a vent, so a formation fight forces a cooling turn or two, by vent or by cool weapon, and a short fight forces none; a cold build is a healthy build and pays somewhere else, in a short reach, a lower number or fewer strikes, never for free (a rolled range was flavour priced at its mean, Ryan, 28 October; gone 2 November), and collects in the long fights; the Peak's Burning and Scorched press a resource the hot builds already feel. Ryan's caveat: heat is not judged from a lack of content, so whether it bites is judged once the roster has hot builds to feel it. A cool weapon is also soft cooling (Ryan, 13 October): spending it instead of a hotter one is a cooldown turn, so heat builds are a spectrum from a kit tuned to its passive cooling that never vents to a hot kit that buys its heat back with vent turns. Heat is a budget (the maximum plus cooling times the rounds) against a demand (what the damage-optimal spends would add); the efficient build ends near the maximum, which is good play. The worst case for balance is slack: a kit with the hot build's damage that ends the fights that matter with budget unused never paid for its damage, and everything converges on it. What the runner reads, per row, spikes rather than actions: peak heat as a share of the maximum and the rounds above four fifths of it; overheats and the hull they burned; forced choices, where the best-damage entry would have crossed the maximum; the damage forgone to heat (near zero on a hot kit is the worst case); and the slack left at a fight's end. The levers are the maxima, cooling, the weapons' heat and the vents' numbers.

Decided

Foes heat machines only through debuffs: Burning and Scorched.

Heat applied straight is so polarising that it needs several avenues of counterplay, or it spirals. Ryan, 12 and 13 October: direct heat goes, and two debuffs carry it, the player's words reused and acting differently on a machine. Burning, the harsher, adds a heat point a stack at the end of the player's turn, before the overheat, then loses a stack, so the player can vent the heat already there or cleanse it: two answers. Metal does not burn, it heats: one word with two effects by body, the hold saying which, and the first debuff that means something on either side, which an Arsenal cleanse moving debuffs onto a foe would need (section 7). Scorched, the softer, makes every weapon and equipment the machine aims at a foe run a point hotter a stack until the end of the player's turn, when every stack goes, so a shield, a vent, a buff or a cool weapon plays round it; the die shows the hotter heat. Both cleanse. A move on every machine takes the softer, since four machines ticking at once is the spiral direct heat had (Claude's fill, Ryan's to move; which foe lays which is content's). Burning on a machine has its own glyph (Ryan, 13 October): Burn's flame filled solid with the thermometer cut out of it, apart from the overheat's bare thermometer and Primed's hollow flame. Heat reads adds before multiplies, as a blow's damage does, so a multiplier on a gun's heat multiplies Scorched's extra too. Retired: Scorched losing a stack a turn, which piled up under several scorchers. Built 13 October. Ryan, 24 October: heat needs to be more threatening, and because the heat debuffs can be countered, with multiple release valves (a vent, a cleanse, a cool spend round Scorched, the Ward, the caster killed or suppressed), they can be significantly buffed; a foe whose jam is heat tries to heat when no machine is hot, and one may aim at the hottest to overheat it, as a boss would try to execute an easy target. The first cut doubles every foe heat stack, its riders numbered as the player's Burn is (24 October; docs/measurements.md): the cold teams feel the Peak now and the hottest starters are walled on the greedy bot, so Ryan's play judges it, and a wall that survives a player working the valves brings the stacks down a step. Exit: the Buzzard's loss (the old direct tick paid its shield every round) and the Peak's numbers are Ryan's play to judge.

Decided

An injury is one threshold on bare hull, it breaks the entry the die shows, and it lasts the battle.

The roll that picked the entry retired 2 November (section 2's randomness row): what faces the enemy is the player's choice. The one permanent cost of a bad fight, answered by shielding before a big telegraphed blow. The threshold is a share of maximum hull on purpose (Ryan, 11 October): asymmetric balancing, so the tank and the glass cannon both have a place, and full greedy aggression risks something against its reward. The threshold may move and the rule be reworked; it goes only if it stops serving that purpose, never because a fight's numbers barely feel it.

Pinned

A low-hull berserker or last-stand entry, an anti-spiral.

Recommended

Terrain in a skirmish is a fight-wide condition the machines feel.

Nothing acts on it yet; if a skirmish ever reads terrain, it reads the ground the enemy paints and never our own (the twisted landscape row, section 4).

6

Die faces and iconography

What the player sees, and how it reads on a phone.

The die

Decided

A die entry reads at a glance from fixed places.

One standard face for every component (Ryan, 2 November: “the faces are crowded and they don't need to be ... We need a standard format, using combined or layered icons, that works for every component across the board and doesn't crowd the die”). One main mark top centre says what the entry is and holds what it delivers; whom it is aimed at stands under it (Ryan, 3 November: “moving the number inside of the icon and putting targeting underneath, since the targeting icons are narrower”); its costs run along the foot, each a mark and a number with no word and no sign (“the player knows the direction the cost goes”; the cooldown's clock among them, “Cooldown should be a cost in the bottom section as well”); its place top left as a grey Roman numeral; a chip per keyword down the right edge, one star for any number of rules (Ryan, 12 October: stars never need merging, the readout lists them); what else a spend does beside the main mark (a weapon's strikes, a status's stacks: “how would that look for weapons? Would let the crosshair fit better”); what the machine's passive adds in its passive box at the foot of the edge, hanging out past the chips clear of the costs (“can we move the passive box to the right?”), his “passive helper box” “for all these types of effects” (4 November), never joined to the blow (“Just the number is fine, and the tap popup will clarify”); a dark entry blank with its clock and turns along the foot.

The marks: a weapon a sight, its ring broken where four heavy ticks cross it, an inner broken circle and a thin outer one (3 November: “I like your second option the most”); equipment a gear, one mark for a buff and a debuff alike, holding what it grants or lays and no number (“Equipment don't generally need numbers”; the buff and debuff arrows retired, “those arrows look like push/pull to me”); a shield the shield as it was (“shield is pretty perfect at the moment”); a vent a plate cut on its top corners with three waves of steam, two thin among them; shield and vent in one the shield under steam across its whole top, “shield | cool” in their colours a size down; a reload its shells. Whom a piece reaches: a line of fire, a solid run breaking into three shorter dashes, every piece one height (Ryan, 4 November: “You lost your 4-segment direct and indirect that I approved”: the four pieces his “I like your choices” took, made one height on his note, had been cut to three beside it), straight for Direct and lobbed low for Indirect (“we had it right with straight versus curved before”), for a Sweep the line lobbed with a strike rising for each of the rank's four columns, a solid run and a dash (4 November: “curved to indicate it has indirect reach, and have the lines poking out have the same kind of segmented look as the others”; “Can we give it 4 lines instead of 3, symbolizing the 4 columns?”), every lob even about its middle (“is your arch actually even? looks like the right side is flatter”), three connected hexes for a machine (“center filled for self-only, left/right filled for ally only, all filled for self or ally”; the self and ally chips retired: “those are targeting rules. They need to be on the main icon”), and the targeting modes' own marks (the area row): Every Foe the enemy's formation, its far rank of four smaller and narrower (“I actually like the 8 squares with the 4 rear ones smaller”), its ranks close (“we can gain a bit by narrowing the gap between the two rows”), in square brackets at its sides (“Can we get side-brackets on All Foes?”; “Square brackets are good”), Every Ally and Every Other Ally the three hexes a little smaller under one low arch carried out past the outer cells, touching none, the middle lit for the whole team and dark for every other ally (“the difference is whether the center one is lit or not, its two modes”). Every number a main mark holds is one size, the widest a two-digit blow. Every mark wears a lining and the frame is dressed to match, accents outside its four corners (“extra little flairs” are what move it “out of amateurish”). The foes' intents are a register of their own (“The arrow can be changed but not to the equipment one”). The sight drawn small is plain, a whole ring and its ticks.

The tests the rounds of 2 to 4 November applied, which a new mark meets: reads as its kind at a glance on a phone (the chevron frame failed as a weapon, the diamond as equipment, a bare number beside a vent as more cooling); nothing crowds the number or the costs' row; no mark reads as a chip's container or a plug. The rounds themselves, each benched mark and his word on it, are git's (git show 1fa1155:docs/design-bible.html, this row) and the sheets docs/looks/die_marks_candidates.png, die_polish_candidates*.png and targeting_modes_candidates*.png; tools/die_sheet.gd draws the whole iconography and docs/rules.md says what each mark means. The glyphs are placeholders, judged on the phone, running. Built 21 October; the standard face 2 to 4 November.

Decided

The die shows the number one blow will deal.

One number. Dragged over a foe it shows that foe's number. The game does the arithmetic; the player reads it. Its heat is what the spend will add, every modifier in (13 October, so a Scorched machine's die shows its weapons running hotter, and the Kite's its double). A debuff shows no number, and a gun whose blow is nothing here shows "0" (Ryan, 21 October; the row above).

Decided

Every entry is a component, a blank or a destroyed component.

Nothing fills an empty hardpoint, so a full, undamaged machine is reliable. Shown text never says "face" (the player sees no polyhedron); Face is the engine's word for one entry. Whether it says "die" is open (Ryan, 13 October; section 12).

Decided

Keywords are a list, at most three on an authored entry.

Upgrades may add more.

Decided

Gameplay first; the vocabulary is trimmed once the content is complete.

Ryan, 16 October: the keyword budget goes, since it got in the way of good ideas: "We need to build good gameplay, and then if our vocabulary is too big, trim the fat. We're not at the fat trimming stage yet, not even close." The form retired, about ten keywords in all judged per die on a phone, returns as the trim once the content set is complete. Variety still comes from combining keywords with numbers, reach, heat and passives, as in Slice and Dice. For the trim: whether an effect is a keyword or a rule written out is a matter of how common it is and whether something can add it to a component during a skirmish (Ryan, 12 October); what a player must learn is the keywords, the printed rules and the statuses together, so moving an effect from a keyword to a printed rule saves nothing; Rapid and the Nemesis's roll again are one niche with two words (Ryan, 14 October). The merges across the two sides still open are section 12's.

Decided

Two rerolls a round, never onto the entry showing; a copy of it may come up.

Two since 6 November, on Ryan's word (“I actually think we should go down to 2 rerolls now. It makes getting additional rerolls more valuable, and it's pretty much standard amongst other games so we might as well try it”), from three: a four-entry die found a named entry in about 78% of rounds at three and finds it in about 67% at two, a six-entry die 57% and 47%, so a reroll gained (Rallied, the Giant Petrel's bank and its Spindrift Salvo) or drained (a Scream, a Shriek) weighs half a round where it weighed a third. The Immelmann Booster's price of three is now met only with the bank, and the Mandrake's Scream takes a whole round: content numbers for Ryan's pass (the to-do). A trial, read on his play once the fights are tight (docs/long_wall_proposal.md), where a reroll's agony is meant to come from the blows and never from the count; the exit is back to three if two reads as a punishment rather than a choice. These are not really dice, and a reroll that lands where it was reads as a bug, so the entry showing is skipped. Its copies are not (Ryan, 15 October): skipping them too was abusable, since a die of one key entry and copies of one filler sent every reroll from a filler onto the key, so a copy comes up at the odds its copies give it, and the entry's numeral shows that the die moved. Rapid's free reroll works the same way, so copies of one Rapid component chain, each switching itself off (a valid, if silly, build); every Rapid piece carries a limiter, a cooldown, heat or Volatile (section 7), which keeps a chain finite. One exception, a design one-off (section 7): the Cassowary's Spin-Up spins its whole cylinder, itself included (Ryan, 19 October), since a revolver's spin may stop where it was. The player must be told; die size is a balance lever. Retired: Scrambled, a foe's debuff that let a die's next reroll land on the entry showing (Ryan, 24 October): a reroll that lands where it was is what this row removes, and nothing had laid it since the Whisperer, whose Corrupt it was, left with the families on 9 October; the foes' interrupts on the die are Disable and Corrupt.

Decided

UNDO takes back every spend since the last roll, in the order made; a draw is its floor.

Ryan, 2 November, opening the randomness pass: “I'm just wondering about allowing undo. Obviously we can't undo past a reroll”; and on the holes: “if we are pursuing Undo as a goal, then rerolling breaks the undo chain, which I guess is a player choice. Rapid also does, though.” So the fight keeps a snapshot before each spend and reroll of the player's turn (the save's own dictionaries, written back in place, so the field's units keep their numbers and the log and the stream go back with them), and UNDO puts the last one back; its badge says how many it reaches, and the trail shows a bar at every roll, where it stops. The floor is any draw from the stream, since a draw taken back would show its result for free: the round's roll, a reroll (not a stepping die's step, which draws nothing and is taken back like a spend), and a spend that draws inside itself (Rapid's free reroll, a roll again, the Scorpion's blast's pick). The pass retired every other draw of the player's turn (section 2’s randomness row), so the undo reaches the whole turn in the common case; the Scorpion's blast, tabled for its rework, is the one spend that still cuts the chain. Ryan, 3 November, on Claude's reading that the undo is for a misdrop and never a search: “Not sure the semantics between undo as a search versus a misdrop. Undo should be a way of scumming information.” So it is a search tool by design: try a spend, read what it did, take it back, within the roll's floor; the round preview answers before the drop and the undo after it. His own hole, decision paralysis (“there's a point where too much control results in extended planning turns for the player where hyperoptomizing turns into decision paralysis”), is a thing to watch in play, not a reason to cap the reach.

The vocabulary

Decided

A keyword and the buff or debuff it creates share a name.

Ryan, 16 October, a global convention: "unless there's a really good reason not to, if a keyword creates a buff or debuff, the keyword and the chip should have the same name." So a foe's brace leaves Armoured, the machines' status (Ryan: "If it's effectively braced, it should be braced too"). The rule binds the words the player reads to their chips; a foe's effect in content (rally, revive once) is no keyword and takes its chip's name. The good reason to differ: one status from many sources keeps its own name, as Empowered does from the Overcharge Coil, the Soulcutter Talons and the Grievance Reliquary. Settled 3 November: Empowered is the one word on both sides (section 5's Armoured row). Still open (section 12): Reraise, which takes a name of ours under the homage rule. Built 16 October.

Decided

A foe has HP; a machine has hull.

Ryan, 22 October: "hull is the label for a machine, not for a goblin." The shown text says HP of a foe and hull of a machine, the vocabulary has both, and heat stays one word, since only machines carry it; the content's and the engine's field stays hp, its comments changing as they are touched. Built 22 October.

The two tables below are generated from content by tools/pages/bible_tables.py (Ryan, 16 October: both maintained in the bible): rerun it after any change to content/*.json, before the bible is republished, and never edit them by hand. A status's effect is its vocabulary codex, the text the player reads; its kind, stacking and duration are its vocabulary entry's design.table, the stacking word checked against the engine's policy on the status (section 5's stacking row). Unique means only a chassis passive or a locked component grants it, or on the foes' side only one foe's moves; global means anything else. Both are counted from the content as it stands, never declared, so a status unique today turns global when a second source lands. A count in brackets is planned content.

Keywords

KeywordWhat it doesUnlocked componentsLocked componentsPassives and modulesFoe moves
On our components
BreachA breaching blow ignores the shield and the armour: the shield stands, the armour counts for nothing, and the whole blow lands. On a foe's move it reaches a machine's hull, and if what gets through is a quarter of its maximum hull or more it destroys a component; a Ward turns it away, and the shield and armour then take the blow like any other. On a weapon it ignores a foe's armour.100—
BurnKeyword with a number. On a player's weapon or equipment, Burn 3 means each foe hit gains three stacks of Burning. On a foe's attack the same: each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Burning on the machine it hit.520—
ChainKeyword with a number, a weapon's. Chain 2 also lands 2 on every other Shocked foe on the field, wherever it stands, where the target takes the number: the attacker's bonuses land on that 2 as on the blow, then each foe's own statuses and armour. No strike is aimed at them, so their Shock stands.100—
CleansePlayer keyword, on shield and vent components. Whoever the component is spent on also loses every Burning, Scorched, Crushed, Silenced, Disabled and Corrupted status. A rapid component's own switch-off is not a status a cleanse touches. On a foe's move of its own, the same word: every mark the team laid (Burning, Flak, Cryo, Vitriol, Shocked, Exposed, Webbed, the Paradox, the Drone) comes off the most-marked of its own, never Numbed or Suppressed.210—
CryoKeyword with a number. Every foe the entry reaches gains that many stacks of Cryo a strike (Cryo 1: one stack), laid once the strikes are done, so a fire never cashes its own frost. Any machine's hit with a weapon on a foe carrying Cryo spends every stack and cools the hitter 2 a stack.010—
DisruptPlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe. The foe does its other move instead, this round, and its new intent shows at once. A foe with nothing else open is not moved, and a follow-up it announced is not either: the move it came after was the window.100—
ExposePlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe. Every foe the entry reaches is Exposed through the next round, a felled one too: in Direct reach of every machine, and its armour counts for nothing. A second Expose refreshes it, never adds.020—
FlakFlak N: each foe hit gains N stacks of Flak a strike, laid once the strikes are done, so no strike of the spend feeds on what it lays.220—
LurePlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe, a weapon or equipment. Every foe the entry reaches turns on this machine for the round, a felled one too, intents and all; reach and area decide who and how many. A lured foe whose move has no target keeps doing it.130—
OvershieldPlayer keyword, on shield components, rare. The shield may raise its target past the target's maximum; shield past the maximum fades at the end of the round, or as the fight ends. A shield die spent on a machine already at its maximum is never wasted when it carries this.010—
PenetrateKeyword with a number. Penetrate 3 also hits the other rank of the target's column, which takes 3 where the target takes the number: the attacker's bonuses land on that 3 as on the blow, then the foe's own statuses and armour. A massive foe fills its column, so it is hit once. On a debuff it carries no number.101—
PurgePurge N, on either side. On a player's entry aimed at a foe, a weapon or equipment, every foe it reaches loses N stacks of every buff it carries but Guarded (an unstackable one is a stack of one), after the blow, so a foe the blow kills stays down. On a foe's move of its own, never a rider on a blow and never a last act's, the machine it lands on loses N stacks of every buff an entry's spend bought, for good, and N of what a store, a form or a passive grants until the start of the player's turn, after its start resolves; its stores stay, and a stack its passive keeps stays. A Ward turns it away and stands, on either side.100—
RapidAfter this component is spent the machine acts again: the die rerolls for free onto any other component (a second copy of this one included, so copies chain). Rapid carries no cooldown of its own: a Rapid component prints its cooldown, a cost the die shows by its clock, or pays heat or hull every spend instead. Any component may carry it, a vent or a shield as well as a weapon.241—
ShockPlayer keyword, on any entry aimed at a foe. Every foe the entry reaches is Shocked, a felled one too: the next weapon strike aimed at it deals double. The entry's own blow never spends the shock it leaves.210—
SpilloverThe Wild Boar's rule, granted to every weapon it fires by its Juggernaut Drive and run by the engine as a keyword: when a blow kills a front-rank foe, what it had past the kill carries into the foe behind it in the same column. Nothing stands behind a massive foe. No component carries it; it becomes a keyword the player learns on its own if a second source ever grants it.001—
SplashKeyword with a number. Splash 1 also hits every foe round the target, those beside it in its rank and the other rank of its column, each taking 1 where the target takes the number: the attacker's bonuses land on that 1 as on the blow, then each foe's own statuses and armour. On a debuff it carries no number.120—
SpreadKeyword with a number. Spread 2 also hits the foes beside the target in its rank, each taking 2 where the target takes the number: the attacker's bonuses land on that 2 as on the blow, then each foe's own statuses and armour. On a debuff it carries no number: its effects reach those foes, and it deals nothing.221—
SuppressPlayer keyword. The target skips its telegraphed action this round, and recovers with its stun resist in Numbed. On a Numbed foe the stun strips a stack and lands nothing; a Ward turns it away.310—
SwapPlayer keyword, the Dingo's Combat Translocator's: the spend takes two targets, a foe and then another foe or an empty slot, and the two trade places; no damage. A massive foe does not move. Drag the die to the first, drop it, then tap the second; drag it again to move it, or tap the ground to set it back.010—
WebThe funnel-web's Web Bomb, locked: the foe hit is Webbed, no damage. After its action the web bursts, Suppressing it and the next foe to act beside it.010—
Printed on our components (the star)
StrikesAn attack's blows per use. A weapon with three strikes lands three separate hits: each lands its own number and carries its own effects, so a rapid-fire weapon procs often (the Skua's strikes share the number, halved and never below one). Against a shield, each strike is its own blow, so once one breaks the shield the next reaches the hull; against armour, every small strike loses the armour, so many small hits are the wrong tool for a heavy target and the right one for a soft one. Foe attacks have strikes too.230—
Vents allAn attack that fires the machine's whole heat as it is spent: the heat gauge is empty after the shot, and every step of heat fired, the step printed on the entry, adds the component's bonus to the blow before the passives. The shot's own heat cost still lands.010—
Empowered a killA rule printed on a weapon (the Soulcutter Talons', locked on the Ammit), with no word of its own: every foe it kills gives its owner a stack of Empowered, which the Ammit keeps for the battle.010—
Grows with missing HPA flat blow that grows with its target's lost HP: it adds its bonus for every step of HP the target is missing, both numbers printed on the entry, read as the die is spent. No roll is involved, so the number is known before the spend, and the die shows it while held over a target.100—
GatedA keyword printed with a condition, such as a Suppress on a foe carrying Flak or on a foe carrying two Burning or more: it lands only on a foe that meets the condition, read as the die is spent and before the blow's own riders, so a spend never opens its own gate. Held over a foe where the condition does not hold, the die shows the keyword's chip struck through.200—
ConsumesA rule printed on a weapon: its blow consumes every stack of the status it names on its target and deals a point more for each stack consumed, before the multiplies. The die held over the foe shows the number with the stacks in.100—
Burns its firerA rule printed on a weapon: spending it sets its own machine Burning, the stacks it says, after the blow. The machine's Burning heats it at the end of the player's turn, before the overheat, then loses a stack, so a vent makes room for it and a cleanse takes it off. Fuel for a machine that wants heat, a price for one that does not.100—
The hopperA rule printed on a weapon, for a machine with a hopper. One fills it: the loads it prints for each foe its blows damage, after the blow, in place of what the machine's own passive puts in. The other fires it: each load it takes adds its bonus to the blow, up to its most a shot, and the loads it takes are spent, with the armour they stood for. The hopper shows under the heat; the die shows what a shot would fire.020—
BracesA rule printed on a component (the Breaching Plow's). Spending it Armours its owner, the stacks it says (an armour point each), until the round's end: the armour stands behind the blow through the enemy's turn.020—
Rolls againA rule printed on a component (the Nemesis's Knuckle of Fury). Spending it with a stack of the status it names spends one, and the die rolls again, a plain roll as the round opens, so the machine acts again and the same component may show again. Unlike Rapid nothing switches off: the stacks are the limit.010—
ResetA rule printed on a weapon: if its spend kills, every entry dark from a cooldown comes back, itself included, and its machine acts again, the free roll never landing on the entry just spent; a team reroll may bring it back up, and every kill resets it again (the Kite's Pinion Driver).010—
PullA rule printed on a component (the Lammergeier's Lorentz Contractor, a debuff): every mine on the field gathers into the slot it is spent on, in laid order, and a foe standing there takes them now. Spent on an empty slot, it gathers them there.010—
DroneA rule printed on a component (the Lammergeier's Syrinx Drone, a debuff): the foe carries the Syrinx Drone. If it already carries Exposed, the spend is Rapid; otherwise it gives the foe Exposed, never refreshing it.010—
RegeneratesA rule printed on a component (the Storm Petrel's St. Elmo's Regenerator, a lure): spent on a foe, its own machine gains Regenerating, the shield it says at the start of each of the rounds it says after this one, a grant of its own each time.010—
An ally's actionA rule printed on a buff (the Giant Petrel's Immelmann Booster): spent on an ally whose die is spent, the team pays the rerolls it says, banked or fresh, and the ally's die rolls again, never onto the entry it showed, and it acts again. Spent on its own machine it grants what it says.010—
Rolls its dieA rule printed on a buff (the Giant Petrel's Reheat Uplink): the die of the machine it lands on rolls again, never onto the entry showing. A die already spent rolls nothing.010—
Rerolls a killA rule printed on a weapon (the Giant Petrel's Spindrift Salvo): if its spend kills, the team gains the rerolls it says this round, once however many fell.010—
CooldownA cost on a component, printed along its foot as a clock and a number: once spent, its entry goes dark for that many rounds, this one included, and the die shows it dark with the clock and the turns left; the die can still land on it, and nothing in it acts. Rapid carries none of its own. No cleanse lifts a cooldown.010—
On foe moves only
Breach (a foe's)The blow ignores the machine's shield and armour: the shield stands and the whole blow reaches the hull; a Ward turns it away.———34 (the Antiquarian, the Antlion Queen, the Balloon, the Bane, the Barbed Needler, the Basalt Elemental, the Black Mage, the Bomb, the Burrowing Tentacle, the Death Knight, the Dread Knight, the Fuming Orb, the Gargantuan King, the Great Needler, the Grenade, the Lancer, the Magma Elemental, the Mantis Ant, the Needler, the Night Sandworm, the Old Man of the Desert, the Piercing Eye, the Pilgrim, the Pit Antlion, the Reaper, the Salt Sandworm, the Sandworm, the Sulphur Elemental, the Warrior, the Wyvern, the Zombie Knight)
ScorchFoe attack keyword with a number. Each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Scorched on the machine it hit (Scorch 2: two): every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe runs hotter.———25 (the Balloon, the Bane, the Basalt Elemental, the Black Mage, the Bomb, the Cinder Tortoise, the Drake, the Fiend of Fire, the Fire Lizard, the Firelord, the Gargantuan King, the Goblin Nob, the Grenade, the Magma Elemental, the Magma Lizard, the Obsidian Tyrant, the Red Dragon, the Rotbloom, the Salamander, the Sulphur Elemental, the Summoner, the Wyvern); 6 of them a move of its own
Burn (a foe's)Keyword with a number on a foe's attack: each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Burning on the machine it hits (Burning, above).———5 (the Bane, the Drake, the Fiend of Fire, the Rotbloom, the Wyvern); 2 of them a move of its own
DisableEnemy keyword. The component the target's die is showing is switched off through the target's next turn: if the die lands on it, it comes up blank.———12 (the Alraune, the Basilisk, the Cockatrice, the Emperor Scorpion, the Gorgon, the Leshy, the Prising Eye, the Rider of the Wild Hunt, the Rotbloom, the Rust Monster, the Storm Orb, the Thunder Ape); 6 of them a move of its own
CorruptFoe attack keyword with a number. Each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Corrupted on the component the target's die is showing (Corrupt 2: two): it deals damage to its owner whenever spent, a bite a stack.———14 (the Antiquarian, the Barbed Needler, the Emperor Scorpion, the Fell Rider, the Gorgon, the Marsh Serpent, the Reaper, the Rotbloom, the Rust Monster, the Shuffler, the Summoner, the Thief, the World Eater, the Wraith); 1 of them a move of its own
CrushFoe attack keyword with a number. Each blow that lands leaves that many stacks of Crushed on the machine it hit (Crush 2: two): every blow it takes through the next round deals a point more a stack. A Ward turns it away; an ally's Cleanse lifts the mark.———1 (the World Eater)
StealEnemy keyword. The component the target's die is showing is switched off through the target's next turn, as a Disable, and the foe takes it: its next swing lands that weapon's number, and anything that was no weapon is a fake, for 1. A Ward turns it away.———1 (the Gilgamesh)
DrainEnemy keyword. The foe regrows what the blow took from the hull, no more than the machine had, so a shield starves it. It lays nothing on the machine, so no Ward turns it away; a Barrier's blow deals nothing to drain, and Burning on the foe takes back what it heals.———2 (the Vampire Lord)
SilenceFoe move of its own, never a rider on a blow. The machine it lands on is Silenced: its components' keywords are muted through its next round, printed and granted alike; its buffs count and its stores fill as ever. A Ward turns it away.———0
Purge (a foe's)A foe's move of its own, never a rider on a blow and never a last act's: the machine it lands on loses every buff a die face bought, every stack, for good, and what a store, a form or a passive grants until the start of the player's turn; its stores stay; a Ward turns it away.———1 (the Djinn); 1 of them a move of its own
SwallowFoe move of its own, never a rider on a blow. The machine it lands on is Swallowed: out of the fight for the rounds it says, until the swallower falls, or until the swallower has lost a quarter of its HP since the swallow, the damage still to deal counting down on its chip. It never takes the last machine able to act. A Ward turns it away; a Lure turns it, and a Suppress or a Disrupt stops it, as any move.———2 (the Floraison, the Ruby Colossus); 2 of them a move of its own
MergeFoe move of its own. Every other foe on the field of the kinds it names is taken into it, gone without falling: it regrows the HP they had left, and its next move hits harder for each one. Thin them first; a Suppress or a Disrupt stops it, as any move.———1 (the King Gel); 1 of them a move of its own
Golden RageFoe move of its own, never a rider on a blow: the foe goes gold (Golden) for the rounds it says, so the round it roars is the window to shield up or to start the burst. It goes gold only while calm, so one rage at a time; a Suppress or a Disrupt stops the roar, as any move.———1 (the Thunder Ape); 1 of them a move of its own
Self-DestructA blow that spends the striker: after it lands the foe is gone, whole, and nothing brings it back. The blow is the foe's whole life, so it is announced like any other and the answer is to kill it first or to shield what it has picked.———4 (the Balloon, the Bomb, the Fuming Orb, the Grenade)
Every MachineA blow at every machine: each of its strikes lands on every machine in the fight in turn, so no aim and no lure moves it, and the number shown is what each machine takes. A shield saves only the machine it stands on; the answers that stop the whole blow are aimed at the striker: kill it, suppress it, disrupt it.———31 (the Antiquarian, the Astromancer, the Augur, the Balloon, the Bane, the Bone Wyvern, the Cinder Tortoise, the Dusk Treant, the Elder Treant, the Emperor Scorpion, the Fallen Weapon, the Fell Rider, the Fiend of Fire, the Frost Treant, the Goblin Shaman, the Grenade, the King Gel, the Marsh Serpent, the Night Sandworm, the Obsidian Tyrant, the Old Man of the Desert, the Phantom Train, the Rotbloom, the Ruby Colossus, the Salt Sandworm, the Shuffler, the Storm Orb, the Thief, the Vampire Lord, the Warrior, the World Eater)
CountdownA move that gathers before it lands: announced as the round opens, it lands the stated number of rounds later, its warning chip counting down the whole time. Nothing hits while it gathers. Suppress holds it a round, Disrupt breaks it off, and killing the foe ends it.———15 (+1 planned) (the Antlion Queen, the Augur, the Balloon, the Fallen Weapon, the Gargantuan King, the Great Needler, the Lancer, the Marsh Serpent, the Obsidian Tyrant, the Phantom Train, the Pilgrim, the Reaper, the Red Dragon, the Ruby Colossus)
Follow-upA move that names the move its foe announces next round. The first lands, and the second is the next round's intent, so the warning comes a round ahead: Red Capote, then Andalucia. A Suppress on the first, or a Disrupt that turns it, and the second never comes; once announced, the second cannot be Disrupted, only Suppressed.———5 (the Cinder Tortoise, the Firelord, the Gilgamesh, the Mantis Ant, the Matador)
GrudgeA blow that grows with the fight: a point heavier for every foe the team has killed this fight, its intent showing the number as it stands. Kill order answers it: the grudge-bearer first, the rest after.———1 (the Pilgrim)
Last ActA blow a foe lands the moment it falls, shown on it by the skull and its number. It lands at once, before anything else, even when the foe was the last one standing, so shield up before the kill rather than after.———2 (the Dread Knight, the Gargantuan King)
PartsA foe in parts: a core and the parts that stand with it. While its parts stand (all of them, or any, by the foe) the core is Guarded and takes no damage; a fallen part may grow back after a few rounds; when the core falls, its parts fall with it. Choose what to focus: a part to open the core, or the core to end them all.———4 (the Astromancer, the Fallen Weapon, the Obsidian Tyrant, the Ruby Colossus)

The screen

Decided

Minimal chrome.

Flat vector overlays in a HUD colour the player picks from three presets (green, amber, blue), never a meaning's colour: not the enemy's violet, heat's red-orange or harm's red. No unit names, rank labels, passives or help text on screen; enemy health shows. The reinforcements waiting show by rank (Ryan, 15 October: wanted; built 22 October, a first cut to judge on the phone): in the formation's right gutter a plaque at each rank's height, an arrow pointing in over the count, and between the ranks a tall one for the massive foes that take a whole column; a rank with none waiting dims at nought while any foe waits, so a mine is laid against a count; the header's REINF stays the total. The plaques stand fifty wide over the last column's margin, their counts large, and a tap pulls out the waiting foes' list beside them, each kind by what it steps into with its picture and its count, the plaques lit while it is out (Ryan, 2 November: “reinforcement icons need to be bigger and more obvious. Ideally we can tap them to pull them out and see the queue”); the pool is drawn at random, so the list is what waits, not an order.

Decided

One chip block under each unit, wrapping into rows.

The chips run in rows as wide as the unit's place (a foe's column, a machine's spot), kept on the screen, with room for three rows under the machines clear of the controls in either layout (Ryan, 2 November: “chips can go off the sides of the screen if there are too many ... Need to wrap rows, which means there needs to be space for 2-3 rows of chips under the player units specifically without bumping into the UI”; one row that ran off the edge retired). A foe's intent (action and number, not target: Slice and Dice's model, with its blow's riders inside the chip) and its statuses; a machine's statuses (every one, however long it lasts, so the Ammit's battle-long Empowered reads there too, and in its codex with its count, kept for the battle: Ryan, 16 October; built that day) and the forecast's warnings (the shield-break warning went on 26 October, Ryan: “now that shields don't prevent all damage. It's clutter, the bar previews are more than enough”), recomputed after every action, its gauges pulsing on one clock where they will change, a foe's HP among them, and a foe the round kills wearing the machines' destroyed chip (the round preview's row, below). A buff on its way, a rally's Empowered lifting a foe's blow, wears its chip on a dashed edge, its opacity pulsing on the gauges' clock, until it lands (Ryan, 26 October: "a pulsing version of the incoming buff as a preview chip", "Similar in concept to the health and shield bars"; the row on rallies, section 5). A unit's open readout draws its arrows; the eye button draws them all.

Decided

The round preview shows everything the round will do but chance and the player's own choices.

Ryan, 26 October: "It should account for every single thing that will happen other than 1) random active effects, which I don't think exist and 2) player decisions such as spending dice faces. Die faces only apply to the preview when hovered over a valid target", and "showing healing on the enemy side as part of the preview is incredibly important for decision making": a foe's heal is set before the player acts, so it is no choice of the player's. So the rest of the round is played on a copy of the fight, every rule as it runs, and the gauges, the warnings and the foes' bars read it: a heal, a death before a foe acts and the last act it sets off, a web's burst, a rally, a self-destruct. Ryan, the same day, on the rolls the engine does make: "None of your random effects are relevant ... Those are all game state changes during the player turn that the preview should recalculate after", and on a pick drawn again when a foe's machine falls, "No such thing should exist. If a machine phases, the targeting recalculates immediately, showing the new game state. Same with everything." So a pick made again (its machine fell before it acts or is Phased, a Disrupted foe's machine gone) is the next machine in line, left to right, Slice and Dice's rule ("it just targets the next ally in line"; Ryan, the same day: "Yes let's build next in line"), which the player can tell without the preview, and the preview shows it with a second arrow, dashed, the foe's own staying solid on its pick, the intent unmoved (the targeting row, section 5; Ryan: a dashed arrow in its place "masks the true intent", "We either need to show both arrows, or do like Slice and Dice and just preview the damage, not the target cascade"). Retired the same day: the pick drawn again from a stream of the fight's own, which the copy shared so the preview knew the draw; randomness the player could see only through the preview, against section 2's rule that randomness is something the player can see and shift. The Scorpion's blast, a strike at random among several, is the die's spend, settled as it lands and read in the next preview, so the copy leaves it out, and the reinforcements are the next round's (an injury's entry and a stun's roll against Numbed were draws until 2 November; the entry showing breaks and a stun strips a stack of Numbed, so the copy does the same). Every status the round will land wears a pulsing preview chip, on a machine or a foe ("All incoming chips should appear as pulsing opacity versions"), beside the skull when the round kills it ("It should be in addition ... some of those chips might be the cause, like adding burn to your own units"), and every warning chip pulses the same way, the skull first ("the skull is just another predicted chip, like any other. It just symbolizes death"); the recorder in Effects notes every grant the copy makes. A web's burst against a Numbed neighbour was the one roll of the enemy's turn until 2 November, its stun shown only when sure ("it can't show an incoming stun unless guaranteed"); it strips a stack now, which the copy shows. A foe the round kills keeps its intent ("the intent line is still relevant for effects and watchers. Especially if the intent is tied to its death") and wears the machines' destroyed chip, so a Burn hit enough to kill shows the fall at once. Retired: the forecast summed by hand (the blows aimed at each machine, then a foe's ticks), which missed whatever it was not taught, a heal first among them; the bot still reads it, by measurement (27 October, docs/measurements.md: the greedy reads some 390 forecasts a round, and the copy costs twelve times a hand sum, so a round on the copy would run seven times as long and the matrix a day), Ryan having left the call to Claude ("Act on the bot however you think"). Built 26 October. What reopens it: the copy's cost on the phone (about 2 ms a refresh on the desk machine); for the bot, a cell whose band the greedy misreads for a heal or a last act, when a memoised copy, one play a decision point, is the first thing to try.

Decided

Numbed's chip has bracket corners, the mark of what nothing cleanses; a status chip has a box.

A Paradox chip shows its count.

Decided

Tap, hold, drag.

Tap a die for the entry showing, double-tap or hold it to lock or unlock it, drag it to spend: a tap reads a die as it reads a unit (Ryan, 30 October: “can we just change lock/unlock to hold and double-tap, and tap to inspect for dice? That way it's standard across the board”), its readout a toggle, one open at a time with the units'. A die's tap lands once the double-tap window has passed with no second tap, the die lit from the touch meanwhile, so a double-tap only locks and nothing flashes (Ryan, 30 October: “Is there a reasonable double tap that wouldn't flicker the popup?”, then “Yeah, agreed to all” to the beat it costs a read, since a lock comes every round and a read grows rarer as the player learns the kit), a fifth of a second, under Android's own 0.3, which read as lag on a single tap (Ryan, 2 November: “double-tap buffer on die is too long and feels laggy on single tap. Lower to 0.2 and I'll try again”); a unit's tap lands at once, its double-tap opening the codex over any flash. The readout lists that one entry, a mark on the die a line with its unique effects last, each line headed by the mark it says, the die's big glyph on what the piece is, a keyword's chip on its line, the star on each unique effect, whom it reaches in the words the die writes under its glyph (Ryan, 2 November: “any icon on a die face NEEDS to be represented in the popup, star icon before the unique effect for instance”), and the whole die is the codex's (Ryan, 25 October: “only show the visible component with slot number, name, and effects listed in a vertical list, with unique effects last”; no flavour for now). It turns the die's iconography into text and lists no source (Ryan, 26 October: “This popup doesn't show all effects on it, it just turns the iconography into text, basically”; listing a passive's change “reads to me like we're expecting the player to do math”): a number the live sources moved off the component's own is coloured instead, green where the change is good for its unit and red where it is bad, never judged for the player, its arrow on it for the way it moved, since hue alone does not read, and each number on its own (Ryan, 26 October: “Don't put the number in a chip, just change the font colour and add the arrow”; “Skua should have down on the damage and up on the count”). What a keyword means is the keywords list's, in the pause menu (Ryan: “We need a keywords list in the pause menu”), and a term in a readout opens its entry on a tap (the terms row, below), so the popup carries no explanation (“yeah this is wordy”). What reopens it: the popup or the list judged on the phone. Tap a unit for its state in the same format (Ryan, 26 October: “showing resource bars with numbers and modification up/downs if they exist, a list of all chips on the unit, and for enemies their current intent in the same format as the die hold press for a component”): its gauges with their numbers, its stats as the codex has them, every chip by its name and count with what it does a tap on its term away (Ryan, 2 November: “unit popups don't need the chips descriptions spelled out now that we can tap keywords”), and a foe's intent a line a mark under its field chip, the machine it is aimed at named, its colours the unit's. The tap toggles it and one unit's is open at a time (Ryan, 29 October: “Change the hold-press on player units and enemy units into a tap toggle, only one unit popup allowed at a time”): a tap on another unit moves it, a tap on nothing or the end of the turn closes it, it follows the fight while open, and it is translucent so the arrows it draws show under it (“Popup backgrounds need to be slightly translucent so that targeting lines can be seen underneath”; 2 November: “Increase the opacity of the popups in skirmish a bit”); it takes every tap that lands on it, a term's aside (Ryan, 2 November: “i managed to reroll when trying to click a keyword on a unit popup since it was underneath. Popups intuitively should prevent triggering things behind them, even other popup triggers”); a machine's rests on its gauges (“Tighten the bottom of the player unit popups to the top of the unit's bounding box”). Retired: a readout open only while the finger stayed down, a unit's (29 October) and then a die's (30 October: its terms could take no tap, and a die's tap locked where a unit's reads); a die's tap landing at once, which flashed the readout open on every double-tap's lock; and KEYWORDS, a button always on screen opening the list (26 and 29 October), a stop-gap once a term opened its own entry (Ryan, 30 October: “I asked for the keyword button as a stop-gap, if we have contextual help that can get repurposed or removed and just live in the menu”; “And yes remove keywords button”). Double-tap a unit for its codex, the base state between fights (the fight's effects live on the readout). A readout opens above its die or unit, clear of the thumb; a die's stays opaque over the log.

Decided

Every special, a boss's blow and a blow at every machine are announced on a plaque.

A JRPG message window in the foes' sixteen-bit register over their rows: the invasion at the interface layer, never HUD chrome. The replay holds for it (a second, a placeholder), once per move, so a storm of three strikes says it once. Plain blows and the player's components get none. A blow at every machine earns one (Claude's fill, 15 October, Ryan's to move): it reaches everyone at once, so it is named before it lands.

Decided

The HUD is a feed being analysed, not a print.

Rare, varied one-shot events (scans, telemetry, tears, signal drops, a die recomputing), never a rhythm and never competing with the pending-gauge pulse. Nothing else moves while the player thinks.

Decided

The skirmish's controls sit where the player sets them.

Ryan, 29 October: “Leave the layout as an option,” the setting Skirmish Controls. Middle puts the log, dice and Reroll between the two sides; Bottom puts them under the machines, which then stand right under the foes in one scene. Middle by default until play says otherwise; in both each die stays in its machine's column and Reroll far from End turn. A quick switch beside the intent eye moves them mid-fight (Ryan, 29 October: “a quick-switch that changes the control layout position”), saved as the setting is; the eye and the switch sit side by side inside the log's glass.

Decided

What the player sees of content.

The codex, the flavour quote, a passive's name and description, a module's description, a foe action's text. Internal text lives under design; planned drafts never show; tags show in the codex only. content/vocabulary.json defines every term and generates docs/vocabulary.md.

Decided

A term in shown text reads in its colour with its icon, and a tap opens what it means.

Ryan, 30 October: “in codex and help text, we should be highlighting keywords in their colour when applicable, and even including the iconography. Pretty standard fare for game clarity”; “Tapping a keyword is exactly what we need”. Every term the keywords list defines (a reach, a keyword, a rule on a component, a status, and since the bosses' slate the keywords a foe's move carries, Every Machine, Countdown, Last Act and the rest, under a heading of their own) reads bold in its group's colour (the colour row, below) with its icon before it, wherever shown text names it by its name or a keyword's verb form: the holds, the readouts, the codex and the list itself; a name that holds a term's word (the Web Bomb, the Pocket Paradox) stays a name. A tap on a term opens its entry beside the word, over any screen, its own terms opening theirs in its place; a tap anywhere else closes it. The words take their colour first, his call to judge (“Try bold in the colour first, and I'll judge”), though the darker colours read lower as text than the plate's quietest grey (heat 3.7:1, the blue 3.4, the red-red 3.0 at their worst; docs/measurements.md); a debug build's KEYWORD INK sets the words in ink, the icons alone coloured, to compare. A die's readout stays open as a unit's does, so its terms take a tap too. What reopens it: his read on the phone.

Decided

Few machines against a horde is the fantasy.

The answer to every foe striking each round is design (area damage, lure, mitigation, foes that misplay), never a cap.

Open

Teaching and icons.

A gesture tutorial and an icon cheat sheet in the pause menu, the reroll rule first; a licensed icon set (game-icons.net, CC BY) for the glyphs; terrain in the skirmish shows as the field's dress (29 October).

Colour and legibility

Decided

Semantic colours are fixed; the HUD picker changes chrome only.

HUD green #60ffb0, amber #fff21a, cyan #5cd8ff: three presets, the green minty since 30 October so it parts from the amber for red-green vision, where #33ff55 and the amber were one yellow (Ryan: “Move to the minty, yes”; docs/measurements.md), and no free picker (Ryan, 30 October: “lets just stick with the presets then”), since a free pick can land the chrome on a meaning or go too dark to read on the map, and the menus drawn in it with it. A register a meaning, every state with a glyph too, so no two marks part by hue alone. The enemy's is the rift's violet, for everything a foe does (Ryan, 30 October: “keep purple for enemy activities”): its rings and the band where a machine could attack from, its warning zones and tags, its intents and arrows, its plaques, the boss's reveal and what a foe did to a machine, light #c8a4ff on a deep #2a0e4a edge, which reads on every ground and plate where the red it replaced fell short (docs/measurements.md); the packs' routes wore it first (29 September), carried by a striped two-violet field. Heat is a red-orange, #ff4000, for what heats or hits harder (Ryan, 30 October: “Lighter, less red oranges sometimes look green to me. In isolation that's fine, but when green can mean 'good,' that can be a problem. That's why I'd rather redden the heat colour”). Any other red is placed case by case (his words: “either a slightly more red-red, the violet, or the heat orange-red”): harm to a unit is a red-red, #ff0000 (a number moved against its unit, a kill or a wreck forecast, an entry that cannot pay, a stop with no room, a foe Exposed, the gate's integrity low), a dead thing its dim #7a1400, and a cost wears its resource's colour, Volatile's hull cost the hull's. The shield's blue (#2f63d8) is what protects or holds: a shield, armour, a ward and a barrier, and the control that holds a foe (a stun, a lure, a web, a disrupt, the Paradox), kept where it was: under red-green vision the violet loses its red and reads as a light blue, so a cooler, lighter shield would have landed on it, where today's stands apart by being darker (Claude's lean, agreed; the control joined it on Ryan's word, 30 October, no hue his eyes keep apart from the rest being free). Green means one thing, a number moved in its unit's favour, the red-red the other way (#5cff6a, in the holds and the codex, never without its arrow; Ryan, 26 October: “If that means using green, this is the place for it”; “Positive and negative are always relative to the unit, not the player”, so a foe hitting harder is green); no other green carries meaning, and no coloured text sits on a map tile. Each term the keywords list defines wears one colour on the die, its chip and in text, its group's (Ryan, 30 October: “standardize the keyword colours. Use whatever is appropriate for each keyword. We don't need a unique colour for each keyword, obviously. Just groups that kind of make sense. Neutral white or grey or whatever is also available, even though it's text colour. Stay away from HUD colour obviously”): heat's for fire, heat and what hits harder (Chain beside Shock), the blue for what protects or holds, the violet for what only a foe does, the red-red for harm (Exposed, a destroyed component), the hull's for a price in hull, and ink for a shape, a reach or a rule; never the HUD colour. A debug build's COLOURS page sets these by wheel or hex, so he tries them himself and his picks land as these values (Ryan, 30 October: “Might as well put in switches for everything, actually, and I'll tweak everything and give you my take”), and since the scheme settled it holds only what is in play (the same evening: “Make sure the colours tab is simplified to only what we need”): the presets, the enemy's four violets (the violet itself, the bright its marks breathe toward, the intent field's deep and the edge under its lines, the routes and the rift's bars and tears wearing the violet) and the meanings; white, the neutrals and the plaque's look are settled and out of it. The HUD colour on text is decoration: flair, titles and subtitles; gameplay text reads in ink or a semantic colour (Ryan, 26 October: “Really the only HUD coloured text should be decorative stuff, flair things, maybe titles and subtitles like we have it. Actual gameplay text should be in a sensible colour”). Retired: the enemy in red (pure #ff0000 from 29 October, #ff1e1e before), which stood 3.0:1 on a chip's black for a protan and 5.6 from heat's orange for a deutan; heat's orange #ff6000, light enough to read as green to him; and a term's colour chosen apart on the die, a machine's chip and a foe's (Suppress blue on the die and the chrome's on a foe, Exposed the chrome's on the die and the red-red on a foe), most keywords in the HUD colour. What reopens it: his read on the phone, or the rings and the routes failing to part there by their structure.

Decided

No two marks differ by hue alone.

Ryan is colourblind (the type unstated), so his colour words are suggestions. A red or an orange is red and green alone, never blue, brighter and darker included (Ryan, 29 October: “I think the reason that I'm struggling with the reds is that you're moving the blues as well when I ask for brighter/darker and so on. Reds and oranges, compose them using RG only, not B”): past pure red the green steps 30, 40, 50 and 60 are each distinct to him, and past about #ff8200 an orange reads as yellow or green; even a lighter, less red orange can read as green to him, which is good's colour (Ryan, 30 October: “In isolation that's fine, but when green can mean 'good,' that can be a problem”), so heat leans red. Each mark carries structure too: an edge, dashes, motion, a glyph, a weight. What fails is a colour over its ground, never two UI colours apart, since every mark carries an icon (Ryan, 30 October: “the issues I'm looking at with colour are not differentiating between HUD elements. It's certain colours, over the BACKGROUND that's the issue”), so a new colour is scored by protan, deutan and tritan simulation (Machado) against the grounds it is drawn on (the field's, the map's, a chip's plate) and picked by the worst case.

Decided

Lean hotter than seems right.

Every effect judged on the phone has come back hotter, thicker or louder, never quieter: start at the exaggerated end and come down.

Decided

Judge an effect running, never from a still.

Every effect ships a switch that fires it constantly (RIFTGATE_FEED_DEBUG=1 is the pattern), and "faster" or "slower" is confirmed before it changes.

7

Mechs and the builder

A loadout is a real identity: MechWarrior's build depth, simplified, with no weight classes and no gated kit. The words: a component is one piece of kit and exactly one entry on the die, a weapon, a shield, a vent or equipment (the row below); a hardpoint is a free slot; a chassis's fixed components are built in and use no hardpoint; a module sits inside the machine and is never spent.

Machines and the die

Decided

Chassis come in any shape and differ by plain numbers.

The silhouette is the model and the footfall, and it decides what a machine crosses (section 4's ground row): tracks, walkers and wheels wade water at two, a hovercraft crosses water and a flyer water and walls, so a team is picked for the map by its crossers as well as for the fight.

Decided

Four squad slots; a run starts with three machines and buys the fourth.

A real choice between splitting up and staying together.

Decided

The die is the components.

Fixed components plus hardpoints, one entry each. A small die is consistent; a big one carries more but shows each entry less often (a thin deck as a chassis stat). Fixed components are never removed or bought open, because restriction makes builds fun.

Decided

Hardpoints are untyped.

The die is already the slot limit (a shield entry costs a gun entry on the same roll), and a niche the player can leave is an invitation.

Pinned

What a hand holds: locked, or a hand that takes more.

Ryan, 12 October: a gauntlet on the Nemesis's hip cannon loses too much immersion; tabled, the fists back as the Nemesis's locked piece. The shape he meant, if it returns: a hand as a superset, never a restriction. A hardpoint on a hand takes anything a mount takes and also what only a hand can hold (a gauntlet, a sword, a handheld rifle, a shield on the arm); a mount takes no hand-held piece; nobody loses a build and a chassis with hands gains the fantasy. It pays only with enough chassis with hands: almost every Arsenal chassis, many of Aphelion's, few of Minerva's and Wainwright & Sutler's, none of Kestrel's. It also raised a word: most players read Melee as a sword (the tags row).

Decided

A chassis's design is its passive, its numbers and its fixed components.

The stock in its hardpoints is generic kit the player replaces, so a passive must work with generic kit, and no machine has one straight-line build.

Decided

Toughness is not slots.

A tank is tough through its numbers and passive, never through a locked component that is only a stat line (plating by another name); its locked component is what only it can do, and a locked shield that does something passes (the Puffin's Backwash Ionizer). If a machine proves both unkillable and hard-hitting, the fix is its hull, not its slots.

Decided

One passive, one idea.

Nameable in a phrase (an upside and its cost count as one), at most three rule lines: a double-edged niche definer (the Kite) or a plain multiplier fantasy.

Decided

A chassis's identity must open build space.

Its passive, numbers and locked components together, never a convenient hole filled or a nice-to-have. A passive that only feeds its own fixed components adds nothing: it might as well sit on the components. The interesting part is how the player gets there: the Nemesis banks rage on any hull damage, so overheating on purpose, or anything that hurts it, becomes a build. A map rule has its place as a passive, but on its own it is a weak identity. Ryan, 15 October, keeping two of the roster analysis's tests: a new chassis earns its place by a new read (a state nobody reads), a new product (a state nobody produces), a new price, or a change to what a kind of piece does, since niches can and should overlap across a larger roster and the test reads the mechanics (a mechanic is its pattern in its context, never one trait it shares, such as a random target: Ryan, 16 October, "if you say 'can't do this thing because that already kind of does it,' then you quickly run out of things"); and a passive aims to be fed by, and to feed, other makers' pieces, ideally two routes in and two payoffs, an aspiration and never a pass or fail.

Recommended

A passive multiplies or redirects what components already do.

A verb several components share belongs on them as a keyword (so overshield is one).

Decided

A passive may break a rule, as a one-off.

That is the point of a passive: the rule bends for one machine and holds for every other, so the player learns the rule once and the exception where it lives.

Decided

A component is a weapon, a shield, a vent or equipment.

A weapon deals damage; equipment deals none (Ryan, 26 October: “Equipment is the label for all 4 forms of buff and debuff faces”): a buff is spent on a machine and grants one of the machines' own buffs, a debuff is spent on a foe, its keywords its whole effect. A shield and a vent are kinds of their own, spent on machines, each saying whom it reaches. The words drop “damaging weapon” and its caveat “regardless of damage dealt”, which is implied (Ryan, 26 October: “Much more clean and intuitive”), so a trigger reads one of three ways: damaging a foe with a weapon, hitting a foe with a weapon (whatever the blow dealt), and hitting a foe with a weapon or equipment; and a rule over what a machine aims at foes reads every weapon and equipment aimed at a foe, so a buff, a shield and a vent stay out of it (Ryan, of the Kite: “every weapon and equipment that targets enemies. Pretty clear and lets it have a backup plan of buffing allies if it chooses”; the Cottonmouth's drum, the Lammergeier's mines, the Cassowary's shots, Scorched and Thrust Overboost the same). A piece may be two kinds on one machine: the Whip Scorpion's vents blast a foe, so they read Vent, Weapon there, and the blast is a weapon's hit, doing all a weapon's hit does, resolved by the code every weapon's hit runs through (Ryan: “Vent blast should obviously proc cryo, that's the point of tagging it with weapon. It shouldn't need exceptions in code, it should just work now”). The die's readout shows the kinds and tags a passive or an effect gives a piece, and the codex the piece as built (Ryan: “hybrid shouldn't go in the codex, since that shows unmodified components”; “If a passive or other effect alters labels or tags, the same rule applies”). Retired: a weapon as anything spent on a foe, a debuff included, and equipment as anything spent on a machine, which needed “damaging weapon” and its caveat to say which hit a rule meant.

Decided

A component is intact or destroyed, for the whole battle.

Ryan, 25 October: "Cracked is gone." The mild state went, and with it the mechanics audit's finding that a crack made a Volatile piece better (its hull price a keyword, stripped with the rest). An injury destroys the entry the die shows (since 2 November; a rolled entry before), at a quarter of the maximum hull. The threshold is set by what it asks of the player, never by a loss rate or a win rate (Ryan, the same day: "It's not about win rate, it's about forcing player choices and balancing compositions"): half, set to keep the two states' losses, was retired within the day, since a machine could lose but one piece before it fell (docs/measurements.md). A wreck loses only what was destroyed, as any machine does: its price is being wrecked and out for a battle (Ryan: "That's pretty big"), and if it needs to bite more, the levers are the recovery time or a cost. "Never destroyed" is the Reinforced Skeleton module's since 31 October (Minerva, tier two; the Badger's identity row), so no stock machine carries the guard. What reopens it: a fight that wants a mild injury again.

Decided

The codex shows a machine between fights; its readout shows the fight.

The codex has base numbers, each with the change the loadout and the battle make in a bracket beside it, the change coloured with its arrow and the base in ink (Ryan, 26 October: “the base number, with a bracket containing semi-permanent changes, if any, also coloured with arrows, such as from modules or battle effects like from fracture rewards”; “just go Base (+/- change)”), never a combat effect. Under the numbers and the passive it lists the active effects, what else the machine carries between fights (“non-chip effects granted not as part of the passive, such as modules, auras, battle effects from fracture rewards”); then the die in one column, each component a row: its name, its type as its main icon shows it (“Face type is either Weapon, Shield, Vent, or Equipment. Equipment is the label for all 4 forms of buff and debuff faces”) and its tags, then three lines, its attributes, its keywords and its own rules, at its own numbers (“Remember codex shows base numbers on the components”), and a foe's moves the same way. What reopens it: the plate judged on the phone.

Decided

Content stays a draft until Ryan signs it final.

design.stage says so on components and modules; none is final yet.

Components

Decided

Every component is exactly one entry.

A passive with no entry is a module.

Decided

Volatile is a cost printed beside the heat, a shield cost stands beside it, and an entry a cost locks cannot be played.

Ryan, 26 October: Volatile goes "from a keyword with a chip to a cost, alongside heat. Simplifies things. Also opens the option to pay for things in Shield as well", which "can be coded in if you're already in there. It's pretty clear, if you don't have the shield, the face cannot be played, and overshield does cheat it out at the cost of using two actions for one effect."
