So a hull cost (Volatile 3) and a shield cost print on the entry in the heat's row, in the die's readout and the codex the same way, and no chip says them: the gauges' preview shows what they take. The hull cost may wreck its owner ("I would allow it"); the shield cost is taken first, overshield included, and an entry short of it is locked: the die shows the cost struck through, as a dry gun shows its ammo, it has nowhere to land, the drag finds no target, the bot passes it over and END TURN pulses. "This will be the first case we have for a face that needs a condition to be used, so that locking catch and UI will need to be built. I think that's a good thing as it will encourage the design space if the support is there for conditional faces, as advanced pieces": one funnel in the engine (Skirmish.shield_short, read by every target list) and one mark on the die, for the conditions to come. Retired: Volatile the numbered keyword with a warning chip (28 September, numbered 22 October). No piece carries a shield cost yet; its pieces are content's to propose. Built 26 October.

Decided

Rapid and volatile may ride on any component, and Rapid always carries a limiter.

So a shield can cost hull, and a vent can give the turn back. Ryan, 15 October: every Rapid piece carries a limiter, a cooldown, heat or Volatile, so no chain is free; most pieces meet it with a cooldown of one, printed on each (Ryan, 2 November: “Rapid does not have a native Cooldown, it needs to be displayed always”: the switch-off that Rapid itself once carried retired, each cooldown a cost the die shows by its clock), and a piece without a cooldown pays heat or hull every spend. The validator holds content to it: a built Rapid entry that prints no cooldown carries heat or Volatile. The Cassowary's Spin-Up (Ryan, 19 October) carries none of the three: its limiter is the cylinder and its odds, since its spin is a true roll that lands on itself one time in six, to be spent again (Ryan: "I think we can allow the high-roll for the revolver spin, landing on itself, as an exception to the global rule"), a fifth of a spin more a spend on average, and every other way back to it in a round runs through the other chambers' switch-offs or the team's rerolls (a combo piece, not a typical Rapid one); the exit is that a stepping-die piece that ever chains for sure gets a limiter of its own. Heat limits a chain only while nothing in it cools, so a Rapid vent of any kind is a free way out of the heat system, and Ryan is very wary of one. Ryan, 20 October: the Wake Diffuser, the one Rapid vent, lost its Rapid and became a vent that also shields, the action-economy row's cantrip by density; a Rapid vent, if one is allowed to exist at all, has no place in the early tiers, never below tier three, and a Rapid shield is never below tier two ("not nearly as toxic as rapid vent"). Volatile carries its bite as a number (Volatile 3 bites 3 hull), so each piece's price is its own, a numbered keyword as Burn is; the form it replaced, one constant bite on every entry, priced every piece alike.

Decided

Support and status entries everywhere, Aphelion's most.

Stock is picked by design and balance, never to give a component a home.

Decided

A locked component is never a blueprint.

Being powerful and unique is why it is locked: no hardpoint stocks it and it has no tier. Its power still reads at its chassis's depth (Ryan, 21 October: components grow in power as tiers go up, so a locked piece on a tier-three chassis is tier two or tier three in power, and the Giant Petrel's Reheat Uplink may outdo the Overcharge Coil, a tier-one piece).

Decided

A shared basic kit.

One plain component a job, one maker each, stocked across makers, while everything else a maker makes carries something of its own (a keyword, a reach, strikes, an odd number): plain entries differing only by number and name are no decision. Tier zero (Ryan, 12 October): what comes off the assembly line, every basic's blueprint owned from a run's start. A tier-one chassis stocks basics beside its locked piece where it has one, since the rule underneath is the budget's: a tier one shows its identity with basics alone. The Arsenal makes none. The eight: four guns (the Twin Autoguns and the Rivet Gun Direct, the Ankle Mortar Indirect, the Laser Carbine Direct and warm; rolled and flat were the pairing until 2 November); the shields and vents split self only, the bigger number (the Canopy, Minerva's shield; the Bleed Valve, Kestrel's vent), from self or ally, the smaller (Aphelion's Halo Shield, Wainwright & Sutler's Coolant Jacket), and none for allies only. The Radiator Sail is Aphelion's ally vent at tier one. A basic never copies a real thing's name (Trench, never Trench Gun).

Decided

One stock pass brought the kit in.

Landed 22 October (its brief: git show a550c2e:docs/briefs/stock_pass.md); the content holds its table and its numbers. What it settled beyond the table: the old close weapons came down to Direct's band; the Rivet Gun became a basic, on the Armadillo; the Zenith Emitter folded into the Laser Carbine, its idea free to return as a tier-three Aphelion piece (the briefs README's bench); the Coolant Dump and the Blowout Stack retired; the Stingray and the Baboon stand at tier one on basics, though both read as tier two until Aphelion has more chassis, and the Baboon may stock a shield again. Kestrel's holes were Claude's to fill (Ryan, 21 October: "stock changes as you see fit"): the Cyclic Cannon, a Direct multi-strike gun; the Transonic Rifle, the flat step between the Laser Carbine and the Railgun; Trim Tabs, a tier-two module, one free reroll of its own die a round paid in a stat; the Overclocked Railgun kept, and the Buzzard stocking the hot steps.

Decided

The Laminar Deflector takes Rapid, and the Entropy Condenser Volatile.

Ryan, 12 October: the two plain pieces that were not basics took identities and left the basic pool's way. The Deflector is a shield with Rapid, Kestrel's tempo: shield, roll again, act; the Condenser a vent with Volatile, the Arsenal's way: its biggest number paid in hull. Ryan, 20 October: a Rapid shield is never below tier two, so the Deflector is a tier-two blueprint that no chassis stocks; its Rapid stays.

Open

The basic kit's names.

The two renamed basics keep working names, the Canopy and the Bleed Valve, until Ryan's name pass, and the Airburst Shell, the Flak Cannon and the Decoherence Veil with them; the Blast Door's old name went to the Barrier piece.

Decided

The Faraday Hull left the Armadillo, its shock kept and its discharge gone.

The Hull read too strong against rank and file, and the discharge was the larger share (measured on the lone Armadillo; docs/measurements.md), so the discharge went first. Ryan, 12 October: retribution is the Arsenal's thing, and the Armadillo is armoured, not a porcupine. Ryan, 14 October: the Hull moves to "a new unit that still wants to get hit, but actually has to worry slightly about its own hull", a lighter Minerva tier two, and the Armadillo becomes the form changer (landed 20 October, the Armadillo row). Nobody carries the Hull: its idea is the echidna's if Ryan takes it, a session left open for now (Ryan, 20 October; docs/briefs/minerva_hedgehog.md), and the Retaliate buff keeps the engine's shock.

Tags

Decided

Tags carry no rule of their own; modules and passives read them.

Shown in the codex as the piece is built and in the die's readout as its machine shows it, a passive's change in (Ryan, 26 October: “If a passive or other effect alters labels or tags, the same rule applies”). The set: Ballistic, Missile, Beam, Plasma, Flame, Arc, Blade, Melee, worn by each piece's fantasy (assigned 22 October, the stock pass's table): every weapon and debuff wears its tags and a shield, a vent and a buff none, and the validator holds every component to the set; five locked pieces the table did not reach (the Pinion Driver, the Lorentz Contractor, the Syrinx Drone, the Fovea Designator, the St. Elmo's Regenerator) wear none yet. Melee means short range: a flamethrower may wear it, and it reads as "this doesn't shoot far", not "a sword". Ryan, 12 October: most players read Melee as a sword, not a shotgun, but the word stays: the words of range (Close-quarters, Point-blank) are too long, and no hand-held subclass is needed. Narrowed to contact it would hold locked pieces and hardly a blueprint, so a module reading it would only feed fixed components: of the seven pieces that wear it, five are locked and two unstocked. Only the screen reads it now: a melee weapon lunges only as a blade with no tag that sends its blow across the field (Ballistic, Missile, Beam), and any other fires as a gun, kicking back, flaring as its tags look and drawing a shot's trail where those show (Ryan, 3 November, of the Bullion Fragmenter, melee and ballistic: “It's a shotgun, thematically”), so a shotgun fires as the gun it is, a flamethrower as its stream and a claw the Cottonmouth's drum fires as its shot; melee then shows nothing of its own, where on a fist or a claw it throws a blade's sparks. What reopens it: a melee piece built neither as a blade nor as a gun.

Decided

Tags are read through soft synergies.

One tag makes a state another tag reads: "beam weapons deal extra to burning foes" beside "missile weapons add a stack of burn" pulls toward mixing, on one die or across the team. A module or passive may name two tags, a cheap way to widen it. Components never read tags. No threshold or count that makes one blueprint the build ("with three melee weapons"; a bonus only one tag's pieces feed): those are lynchpins that dictate a build, the player farming one blueprint to make it work. A count every piece of the tag feeds, where nothing a tag grants feeds that tag (the gunship: each weapon fitted raises a number by its tag, Ryan's, section 7), is no lynchpin and passes.

Decided

A tag is flavour the screen and the sound read; no mechanic needs to read one.

Ryan, 4 November: tags are hooked to the animations and the sounds (Melee's lunge, a flare's look and a blow's sound by tag), so a tag no longer needs a mechanical reader; at worst it reads as flavour in the codex, a small part of it and harmless to keep. A module or a passive may still read one (the catalogue row). Retired: “a tag earns its place once two things read it” (12 October).

Decided

A chassis passive reads a tag only if it opens build space.

The Nemesis's melee bonus was the model (any melee, paid for by any hull damage) until its rage replaced it on 12 October; no chassis passive reads a tag today.

Modules

Decided

Modules carry stats and stay simple.

Stat lines up or down plus at most one rule: a small number and a stat line, never a wall of text. No parity with each other, and no universal must-pick (every chassis fitting the same module flattens the game); pick share in the runner guards it. Four archetypes guide, unenforced: high stats and no text, low stats and good text, massive stats and bad text, negative stats and amazing text.

Decided

Module slots are a chassis number, never bought.

Two; the Clydesdale three.

Decided

Platings are modules.

A hardpoint carries something mounted, and armour slapped on does not track.

Decided

The Muzzle Velocity Booster and the Tactical Databank are mirror images.

They were: one raised its rolled weapons' highest roll, the other their lowest, with no stats, parting by matchup (the Booster where a spike crossed a kill, the Databank where steady damage beat armour and long fights), which was the asymmetry wanted; they measured the same within the noise on a rolled machine and nothing on a flat one (docs/measurements.md). Rolled damage went on 2 November (section 5), so both are planned until they have rules. A flat increase to damage stays rejected: good everywhere, so never a choice.

Open

The catalogue again as soft-synergy pairs, on the tags assigned 22 October.

Why it reopened: without a rule the Ram Frame measures the best module on every machine (section 10's prices), the universal must-pick the first row on modules forbids, and the two "bad text" modules measure as losses on nearly every machine, so their stats are not paying for their text. Decided 22 October (Ryan, on Claude's second proposal) and built: the Ram Frame and the Artillery Rangefinder are mirrors, as the Booster and the Databank are, each a reach read, the Frame +1 on Direct blows aimed at the front rank, the Rangefinder +1 on Indirect blows aimed at the back; the Frame's armour line is gone, and both pieces' stat lines come from the pricing run. The Melee read proposed first fed only locked pieces, as the tags row says. The rest of the catalogue is the open part.

Makers and names

Decided

Five makers at most, each an identity through what it builds.

Never a battlefield niche; a voice and a history, never marketing. Ryan places machines and components under them. The table says what each maker tends to build, a guideline and never a rule (Ryan, 12 October): a machine may cross it while it still reads as its maker's. The name families hold. A profile is an identity, never an exclusion (Ryan, 13 October): a keyword or a mechanic fits wherever it fits the piece's fantasy, so a Wainwright & Sutler spider paralyses because spiders do (Aphelion would hardly build one), and statuses are Aphelion's strength, never its alone. Such leanings are marked Lean in this document, and a Lean is never a reason to refuse a piece.

MakerBuildsVoiceNames
Minerva Heavy WorksTracks (nearly all of them), heavy wheels, quads, perhaps heavy bipeds and one flying brickA maintenance manualRugged mammals
Kestrel DynamicsSmall and mid flyers and hovercraft on big boosters, all the fast jets, nothing bigA flight-test logBirds, raptors first
Wainwright & SutlerWheels, quads and a heavy on six or eight legs (Ryan, 28 October), perhaps a helicopter, no bipeds but the Cassowary's chicken walk; old-West iron, simple and straightforward: indirect fire and areaField notesThe frontier's working animals and pests
AphelionThe deep-space programme come home: hovercraft and bipeds, aquatic; it flies on thrusters and space-age rockets rather than rotors (the Stingray's flush fans are its own silhouette)Haughty jargonPrimates, insects, sea creatures
The ArsenalThe Council's house: creepy walkers first, borrowing other makers' shapes; hot plasma, big single hits, burn, self-damaging kit, retributionLiturgyFigures who punish, from any culture: justice, vengeance and retribution, Hell's wardens and avenging spirits among them; never war
Decided

A nickname is a real Earth animal a player can picture.

Living or extinct (a fossil when it dodges the trope: Dunkleosteus over Megalodon). The Arsenal names its machines for figures who punish, never animals, so a name tells the player which side of the makers built it: a mythic name on a city maker's machine would blur that read, which is the reason and the whole of it (the invasion brings the genre's high fantasy, and the occult is the Council's). No vehicle has an open cockpit.

Decided

Each chassis has a dull maker's designation.

It names the job, never a stat, in its maker's shape (EX-45XLE Combat Miner, K-7 Mk III Interceptor, Pattern 1 Combat Walker, Perihelion Flight 7 Support Frame, Arsenal Instrument IV), shown small above the nickname. Each maker's word for the thing is its own ("Frame" is Aphelion's, "Instrument" the Arsenal's): a lean of the designations.

Recommended

Claude's designations are proposals for Ryan to move.

Every designation Claude filled, the Kite's and the Clydesdale's among them, stands until he moves it (Ryan, 21 October: "Designations as you see fit"); content holds them.

Decided

Names are ours, or a knowing homage.

No chassis takes a machine name from BattleTech, Heavy Gear, Warhammer 40,000 or Halo. Iconic names are never used, known ones are judged case by case, coincidences are kept. Ryan, 21 October: the two passes the roster carried close. The Sparrowhawk (a BattleTech aircraft, a coincidence) is the Kite, the light raptor that twists on its forked tail, and the Goshawk (an obscure nickname's) the Buzzard, the broad-winged one that soars and stays; the ids stay, since nothing the player sees reads one. Out with them: the Shrike, the Merlin, the Gyrfalcon and the Peregrine (BattleTech's), the Albatross (BattleTech's and Halo's) and the Condor (Halo's); a name too specific to picture (the Hobby, the Saker) is out too. The Butcherbird, the strafer's name at its go, turned out a BattleTech 'Mech's that evening, so the strafer is the Skua (Ryan, 21 October); a plain Owl is Heavy Gear's, so Kestrel's owl is the Snowy Owl.

Decided

Component names follow each maker's vocabulary as a guideline.

Minerva blunt, Kestrel air and speed, Wainwright & Sutler plain military beside something silly, Aphelion physics nonsense, the Arsenal medieval and occult, sanctified on the face and sinister on reflection; general words (cannon, laser) belong to no maker. A name is a piece of kit, never an ability; its pseudo-tech may contradict itself; it approaches a real thing without copying it; the codex never explains the joke.

Decided

A maker leans toward keywords; it owns none.

Ryan, 13 October: a maker's keywords are identities, never exclusions, and a table bucketing keywords by maker as rules is a poor guide to design. A maker leans toward the keywords its pieces most often carry, and any piece carries the keyword its fantasy calls for, whoever makes it. The leanings: Minerva toward Spread, Lure and Armoured (heavy industry holding a line); Kestrel heat as a resource, rerolls, strikes and risk, Overshield and Exposed, with Rapid its gated cantrip (Ryan, 20 October: "the maker didn't really have an identity before other than the look, and now we are indeed looking at heat, rerolls, multi-strikes, and risk"; 28 October: "Kestrel holds Exposed"); Wainwright & Sutler Flak (its signature status, Ryan, 19 October; 28 October: "W&S holds most of the Flak"), Penetrate and strikes, Suppress on its Battery (indirect fire, volume, the on-hit); Aphelion Disrupt, Shock, Chain, Cryo, Cleanse and Primed, and the Paradox (the status house; Ryan, 28 October: "Aphelion holds a lot of the other status effects - shock, chain, cryo", so most Shock and Chain setters land in its content); Minerva most of the armour buffs (Ryan, 28 October); the Arsenal Volatile, Burn ("Arsenal gets a lot of the Burn"), Empowered and Retaliate, and the talons' kill rule and rage (pain, fire, retribution). Ryan, 28 October: "Crossover can and should happen, to increase cross-maker diversity when building. Leans, not rules, not rails." The merges across the two sides still open are section 12's questions.

Decided

A machine wears its maker's livery.

A repaint is a palette swap; extra colours are free Sponsorship unlocks (section 10).

Decided

A prototype hangar comes before the economy.

Repaints and loadouts tried on every machine, the loadout carried into battle for play tests. The harness fits loadouts and modules today, and the quick campaign's hangar a run's (section 3's quick campaign row); a repaint is a debug switch (RIFTGATE_PAINT).

The chassis

Each chassis's maker, tier, size, numbers and kit are content's (content/chassis.json, the hangar's Codex tab); the rows below keep why each machine is what it is.

Decided

The cars trade strengths.

Their map rules are passives (the Prairie Dog is never hunted, the Dingo acts twice), and each car's weakness is the other's strength. A decoy every hunter picks, the Prairie Dog's first passive, stays out: it scraps the machine unless it may kite, and kiting is a dangerous road. Alone, a map rule is a detachment tool on a die that cannot detach (docs/measurements.md, 13 October), so each car fights by its locks: the Dingo's Megataser Harpoon Shocks, the pin the setup for an ally's blow (Ryan, 14 October: a taser shocks), and the Prairie Dog softens with Flak (its row below). The want of a fast machine that fights alone stays open.

Decided

The Buzzard splits sustain from burst; the Clydesdale's big die earns its reroll; the Wild Boar dives rather than tanks.

Decided

The Stingray is Aphelion's hot status amplifier.

Cooling 0 and a hot fixed gun keep its Exothermic Suite fed. Its strength at high heat and its double dip through Primed are settings to tune, and its burns feed the Baboon's meter on purpose.

Decided

The Puffin is Kestrel's shield escort, and its shields read the ally's heat.

Ryan's go, 21 October, on his worry that it was toxic and boring (a big overshield and a vent on the key ally every round, whatever that ally did). Its passive, the Slipstream Projector, grows every shield it spends on an ally by a share of the heat that ally carries, so it multiplies what shields do: Kestrel's heat as a resource, converted for others (the Buzzard converts its own). Its fixed Backwash Ionizer is the big one through it, allies only. The cooling it gave went as the toxic half, which also fought the heat the passive rewards. No cap: the ratio bounds it, the die limits shielding itself, and overshield fading at the round's end is the safeguard. The decision it makes: the hot ally or the threatened one, and the team's order, since an ally that fires its hot gun before the Puffin spends is shielded for it. A shield specialist is a niche; a shield-bot is not. Tier one since 21 October, to balance the line as the Kite moved to tier two. What reopens it: a larger share of the ally's heat if it plays as a trickle; the Peak's Burning and Scorched become shield through it, the domain that should fear an escort. Its rounds: git show b4dfbe5:docs/briefs/kestrel_puffin.md.

Decided

The Kite at tier two: a flying accident that kills to stay alive.

Ryan's direction, 18 and 19 October (the data read it as a tier two, and double damage and double heat "feels thin and abusable": a cool hard-hitting gun doubled was a free lunch), and his go, 21 October. Its passive doubles every weapon it fires (the printed number, buffs once; section 5's blow row keeps it so and notes its buff if it ever needs one), and every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe runs a flat heat hotter whatever its own and goes dark two rounds, a buff on an ally its way out (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row), and each attack that killed makes its shots hotter for the fight; its locked Pinion Driver, its four in two shots at half, one from each wing tip (Ryan, 29 October: "the same total damage as before, same everything. Just in two shots. A minor change"; both go at the one foe, armour comes off each and Flak adds to each, so an armoured foe takes less, and a shot after the kill lands on nothing), on a kill brings everything back and the Kite acts again, and a team reroll back onto it chains it again with every kill, with no limit (Ryan, 24 October: "Let the Kite reset infinitely. There are other ways to nerf it that aren't more rules exceptions"): the kills, the team's rerolls and its heat are the brakes, since every shot pays the passive's flat heat and every kill a point more for the fight, and if the chain proves too strong the levers are its numbers, never a cap. The flat heat answers the cool-gun abuse, since every shot pays it; a d3 (the die minimum loosened for it) makes every dark entry felt, the team's rerolls its fix; a paper hull (the scales row's one-off) caps how much it fires. Its niche: the trash chain, and a steady doubled gun on the boss; the caster-killer is the Needletail's Exposed, on which the doubled shot loses nothing to armour. The old finisher left for the pool as the Coriolis Accelerator. No shield on a kill in the first cut: getting out is going dark while the Puffin and a Barrier cover it, the kill-shield the lever if it dies too often. Its runner rows are its team rows, since alone it loses what it used to win. The passive's four effects are its one-off over the cap of three. Its brief: git show 95d4ed3:docs/briefs/kestrel_kite.md.

Recommended

The Baboon is Ryan's brief.

Long arms, stubby legs, part orangutan and part troll, its suite on its back, control through statuses; numbers and kit filled in for him to move. Its passive is decided (the Pocket Paradox); its flavour text is his to write. Its move to tier two is in session (docs/briefs/aphelion_baboon.md).

Decided

The Nemesis: "Taking hull damage banks a rage for the duration of the skirmish."

Ryan, 12 October, in place of the Crucible's melee bonus a hull hit: all hull damage, a blow, a bite of its own, an overheat, since that opens the most build space and reads the simplest. Its fists spend the rage (below). Rage pays for actions only, so the die stops collapsing onto the fists: they took two thirds of its spends with the Crucible feeding them, half with rage.

Decided

The Nemesis's fists are the Knuckle of Fury; the gravity piece is shelved.

Ryan, 12 and 13 October: fists that spend rage to roll again are not gravity, so the Nemesis keeps the word knuckles, in the Knuckle of Fury (Ryan's name, a Bruce Lee reference), with no Burn ("it has no place"). The gravity piece, a tier-three blueprint with an area effect and Volatile, is shelved with lots of potential and comes back later; the Gravity Knuckles entry stays unstocked at tier three until then (as a blueprint it measured a sidegrade on other machines, and the Nemesis's best build only because the Crucible fed on its bite). Ryan, 12 October: no hard guard against a build that breaks the game. It is not a PvP game, enough stands in the way that a busted machine needs support, and a piece that is generically that good can be rare or dear to build.

Decided

The Nemesis's signature: rage that keeps its fists swinging.

Ryan, 12 October: its locked fists spend a rage when they strike, and the die rolls again instead of being spent: "it's not a choice, you just get rapid", with no cooldown, since the rage is the limit, which reads far more berserk. The roll is a plain roll as the round opens, not Rapid's reroll, so a fist may show again, the very fist included. Rage buys actions, never damage: a content call for this machine. A rage a hit, since rage per damage taken would barely differ and would cost the player a half rage to track. The levers if it should hit harder: the fists' number, or two rage a hit (Ryan, 13 October: the fists as they are until play, without Burn). An optimised build feeds it more in a group (the Tithe Coupling); a lure build waits on content and on a bot that values being struck. The roll again shows (Ryan's ask): the Rage chip bursts as its stack goes and the die flips back with the reroll's own flip and sound. Banked up to five (Ryan, 22 October).

Decided

The Honey Badger's hopper: every foe its weapons damage loads it, and the loads it holds are its plate.

Ryan's calls of 15 October, built on his go of 31 October (“agreed on all proposed. Build it”). Its passive, the Ore Hopper, puts a load in for each foe a spend of its weapons damages, never a load a strike, up to four, boxes under the heat; every two loads held are a point of armour, the Ore Plate, the first buff a store grants (the store row), so a purge sleeps the plate and leaves the loads. Base armour 1, so 1 empty and 3 full. The Chainsaw Claw lost its Spread and strikes twice, flat 3, the chase spend against a boss: a load needs the foe damaged, and two strikes pay a boss's 1 or 2 armour twice. It digs two loads a foe, the most efficient fill and not the most effective weapon. The Bullion Fragmenter fires half the hopper (Ryan: “half of max”), two loads at +2, the plate they stood for spent with them. So the round holds the decision: hold the ore and stand behind it, or fire it and hit; it lives in long fights and against bosses, since armour bites only past the shield. Ryan's numbers of 15 October (a point a load, ten loads, 5 empty and 15 full) were set before the scale's rescale of 21 October, where ten points would blank nearly every blow. Their shape at today's scale, a point a load to 5, read well past the old Badger alone (docs/measurements.md, 31 October: random play past half the lone big foes it used to lose), and the plate is what carries it; so the plate is Ryan's second fallback, landed the same day as his sequence said (“I would probably go to 2 base armour and 1 for every 2 loads, to a total of 4. If that's still busted, back to 1 base”), since the first barely moved it. Its base shield stays 8: his question that day (“Badger probably deserves less base shield from this, right?”) read within a point or two at 6. Still his to judge in play: its lone big foes falling to random play more often than not, the detachment niche or still too easy; the claw's number is the next lever measured. Ore Hopper and Ore Plate are working names.

Decided

The Breaching Plow armours the Boar.

Ryan, 12 October: "Give the plow braced. It fits." (Braced is Armoured since the one-name convention of 16 October, section 6.) Spending it Armours the Boar until the round's end, in place of the Glacis, an armour line meant for front-rank blows only, which measured no different from plain armour and so was hidden base armour (docs/measurements.md). Charge and brace is a decision; plate that is always there is not.

Decided

Locked components may work together.

A chassis's fixed pieces that feed each other are design space of their own, kept in mind for every new chassis: what only this machine can do, in a sequence the player plays round by round. Several machines grow through a fight (the Buzzard, the Nemesis, the Ammit, the Stingray, the Badger), which is fine for now, a symptom of scaling being easier to design (Ryan, 12 October); what matters is that a machine's pair fits its identity.

Decided

The Honey Badger's identity: a scrappy machine that keeps on coming, its ore its plate and its shot.

Ryan, 12 October: scaling threats are fine for now, but scaling is not the Badger's identity. Settled on his go of 31 October (the rounds in its brief, retired: git show c05c41b:docs/briefs/minerva_honey_badger.md): the hopper is the identity (the hopper row), leaning to area and the detachment beyond “bruiser”; “never destroyed” left the passive for Minerva's module, the Reinforced Skeleton, at tier two with the Ram Frame's +8 hull beside its rule (Ryan, 15 October: “Can have positive stats since that's not that impactful an effect”). The bench, each with the rule that set it aside: the last stand (binary, and rare in a fight); campaign insurance (no job in a fight, so a module's text, now the Skeleton's); salvage from its kills (an economy engine, and the economy is unbuilt); melee hits only (five of the seven melee pieces are locked); Armoured in place of the plate (a derived Armoured muddies “lasts the round”).

Decided

The funnel-web: an ambusher that binds and bites.

Ryan's go, 18 October (its brief: git show bfa8cf2:docs/briefs/ws_funnel_web.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier two. Its passive: its blows on a foe showing no intent on it lay Vitriol, a share of the foe's current HP a stack at its action, so it opens on a boss, never finishes a foe and never sets off a last act. Its locked Web Bomb is its stun, kept under the stun row (Ryan: it fits the kit): the webbed foe acts once, then the web bursts, Suppressing it and the next foe to act beside it; no cooldown (on one foe Numbed is in effect its cooldown, and a stun is priced by a gate, here its timing). Its two uses are the decision: web the foe left of the threat and the threat skips now; web the boss and its blow lands now, it is held next round and shows no intent then, a window. Per hit, because per spend the stacks never happened without Rapid, and strikes as bites give multi-strike pieces a home (the Maxigun its first chase piece); the payoff is the team's windows (an ally's Lure or Suppress mid-turn, a bare-hulled tank), with Vitriol the reward and the Paradox a bonus, never a reason (Ryan). Set aside: fangs, a missing-HP finisher, which closed the role on itself and repeated the Coriolis Accelerator's pattern. Its weapon hits lay the Vitriol, as its text says (Ryan, 25 October: "That's not a global passive rule, that should be a vitriol specific rule, and documented in the passive text"; "The important part is that it was a damaging weapon"), so the web feeds the ambush only through the window it opens. What reopens it: the ambush condition must bite (a soft limit on a boss, a hard one alone and in swarms; the runner reads the share of its blows that bite and the web's share in formation fights) and a window's Vitriol must be worth the spend that made it; it waits on elites in fights to matter outside the boss fight.

Decided

The Cassowary: the gunslinger whose die steps instead of rolling.

Ryan's go, 19 October (its brief: git show 021f075:docs/briefs/ws_revolver.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier one, the maker's damage dealer. Its passive (Ryan's wording, 12 October): "Whenever its die rolls, it moves to the next entry instead, in sequence." Each fight opens on a random chamber (Ryan, 18 October: "like a revolver spinning, the only random factor we're able to inject in an otherwise deterministic chassis"), so the plan is adjacency, not sequence, and a team reroll is its dial: its one source of randomness, seen and shifted. Every sixth weapon or equipment it aims at a foe counts double, so the player steers which chamber takes it. Its locked Spin-Up spins the whole cylinder, itself included (his exception to the reroll rule, a design one-off), and primes its next weapon; its limiter is the cylinder and its odds (Ryan: a combo piece, not a typical Rapid one): a spin lands on itself one time in six, to be spent again, the high roll he allowed, so a spend averages a fifth of a spin more. Squishy on purpose (Ryan, 19 October: "we're packing a lot of firepower in it"), so the maker's tender deal holds no front, accepted: the Clydesdale moved down a tier is the lever if play tests want one. Only a weapon spends Spun Up, equipment leaving it standing (Ryan, 26 October: “Spun up only interacts with damage, so change it to Weapon only for now until balance says otherwise”); retired: a debuff spending it for nothing, a bonus that is only damage taken by a piece the die grammar (section 6) lets deal none. Its passive's name (the Indexed Cylinder) and its designation are working names. What reopens it: a stepping-die piece that ever chains for sure gets a limiter of its own; if the pips or the next-chamber chip clutter a phone die, the counts retire to the die's readout. Its model's first cut landed 31 October, and its second round 2 November on Ryan's notes (“the unit is more of a symbolic revolver than a literal one. I think the drum should represent some kind of capacitor, charging up for the sixth hit of it's passive. It could spin for the spin up component”; the toes “not mechanical enough”): a capacitor bank on its flank, a cell lit for each counted shot and discharging on the sixth, turning while Spun Up, on mechanical feet, to judge running; the bot plans its cycle since 3 November, and its rows read a tier one under the Cottonmouth alone, carrying a decision, neither a wall nor a free win (docs/measurements.md).

Decided

The Cottonmouth: versatility of reach, every weapon through the drum.

Ryan's go, 19 October (its brief: git show 021f075:docs/briefs/ws_fan_boat.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier one, the fan-boat: the maker's versatility in a third form (Ryan, 19 October: the Clydesdale's is loadout, the gunship's stats, this one's reach), so it belongs at tier one, where it plugs holes in many draft compositions. Its passive, the Adaptive Munitions Drum (Ryan's name): every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe leaves the drum as one Indirect, flat shot with its own keywords (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row), punching through the back rank or spraying the front, so a column or a row is the decision on every shot, with full information and no state to track. The drum is the passive's rule, not an entry on the die (Ryan: "the deck gun isn't actually a locked component"), so the machine locks nothing. The fixed number is what makes any shape balanceable, and the price (a big gun is wasted on it), with the light hull and the heat. It spreads statuses well, which steps on Aphelion's toes and is fine (Ryan: "Status spread isn't a monopoly, nor is it a complete identity"); beside the Baboon it is a Paradox engine. Set aside on the way: everything Direct through a deck gun (free reach once danger close went), the magazine, the echo, the sponge, the sum and the hopper (the brief's bench). Its hole to read: every status shell becomes a two-or-three-foe status, so the gate principle holds per piece. Its shape is the shot's whole (Ryan, 28 October, on a Spread piece fired through it): the drum's Spread 2 or Penetrate 2 replaces the piece's own number, never the higher of the two (“that's gaming the build”), “it forces the weapon into its own shape and then adds any keywords and effects it doesn't already have”. Ryan's picture for its model: a big rotating set of barrels with the guns mounted invisibly inside, future-tech, a hover hull under a drum and the one machine whose hardpoints show no mounts; its first cut landed 2 November (a hover barge under a turning drum of six barrels, two ducted fans astern, its mounts hidden), and its third round the same day on his calls: the drum keeps its idle turn (“it looks good and unique”), carries no index mark (“How on earth does six rotating barrels look still?”), parks on a chamber to fire after a quick spin forward, two barrels for a weapon's real shot, “so it looks like it's slotting the chosen component but doesn't actually need to”, and one at a time between its ranging act's test shots (Ryan, 3 November: “Ranging can just move one barrel at a time during its animation. Shots moving two makes sense”), lights only the top barrel's mouth and the lamp behind it for a shot, and lights its strip through every spin that is not the idle turn. Its guns' own fire effects stay off at its hidden mounts (“to avoid any clipping or effects bleed through the drum”; the hookup the to-do's). To judge running.

Decided

The Dingo gains a Combat Translocator: two foes or spaces trade places.

Ryan's go, 19 October (its brief: git show 021f075:docs/briefs/ws_dingo.md): "Dingo winch is perfect. We could increase it's flexibility by allowing lateral movement as well, unique from the Minos. Target two foes/spaces and swap them? Useless against a boss, sure, not sure if that matters." Two foes, or a foe and an empty slot, trade places, no damage (the Swap keyword): distinct from Minos by pattern (Minos turns whole ranks over involuntarily; the Dingo chooses two). It exposes a caster to Direct fire, sets the acting order (the spider's web, a stun's timing) and bunches foes for every area piece, so a swap then a shell is a cross-maker combo, and soft control. A massive foe does not move, so bosses are out by rule; useless against a lone boss, which does not matter for a tier-two team piece whose harpoon still works there. A teleporter in the maker's register, not a winch (Ryan: a cable cannot swap two things): "guys this totally isn't just a teleporter", the Combat Translocator (his working name). Tier two with it, a second locked piece reading as a pure team piece. Its interaction, drag to the first target and tap the second (Ryan, 21 October), is the form any later multi-target piece shares; a tap on the ground sets it back, the die flying home, once no readout is open, since the drag back alone was hidden (Ryan, 30 October, agreeing). The bot reads a swap that bunches foes under an ally's area, not yet the acting order; its look is the settled looks row's. It makes the Dingo's die a d5 where it was a d4 (Ryan, 29 October: "I guess that's ok").

Decided

The Prairie Dog's fight half: its Flakshot lays Flak, the maker's on-hit.

Ryan's go, 19 October, on his own shape (its brief: git show f5a085a:docs/briefs/ws_prairie_dog.md): "Flak repeater - if it was a low damage, spread, maybe rapid, weapon with a new debuff: flak, target takes X damage per stack when attacked ... Could be part of a new Flak family of W&S weapons, their signature status." Flak makes every blow of a weapon the foe takes deal a point more a stack, a blow of 0 too (Ryan, 25 October: "Flak and overcharge exist in part to get past armour"), halving each round (Empowered's clock, so a steady feed settles and a boss never ramps; reopened for Flak alone on 22 October and closed 4 November, the halving kept, Ryan's call on readability); on-hit only (Ryan: "on-hit only"), since a tick at the foe's action is Burning with another chip. A flat on-hit is the kind Ryan named missing on 14 October; Shocked stays the multiplier one blow spends, and the two pair. What it gives the maker: its many small kinetic blows get a status that makes often matter, with routes in from any spray or multi-hit piece and payoffs on every small blow. The decision it makes: lay before cash inside a round; spread it across a rank for the area pieces, or stack it on the one foe the Maxigun will chew. The pivot (Ryan, 20 and 21 October, under the action-economy row): the Flakshot Repeater lost Rapid, since the squad's setup-then-cash is the team's order and a gated Rapid bought the Dog's solo loop alone; Flak 1 (Ryan, 22 October; 2 the lever if 1 reads as nothing). The car's fight half went on its passive beside the map rule: the Prairie Dog takes less from any foe carrying Flak, whoever laid it (Ryan: on the passive and not on the piece, so any Flak piece covers it). So one status does both halves: in the squad the Dog softens the rank; caught alone it sprays and the swarm hurts it less, survival rather than kills. Two clauses on one passive is Ryan's call for this car. What reopens it: the halving clock settling too high on a boss (a cap), or the maker's small blows making Flak a must-pick (price, never scarcity); Flak's clock is section 12's.

Decided

The Whip Scorpion: the boiler whose vents are its guns.

Ryan's go, 20 October (its brief: git show 82ff916:docs/briefs/ws_boiler.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier two (Ryan, 19 October: so that not both of the maker's damage dealers sit at tier one, and it is the more complex), the maker's sustained damage dealer ("a sustained dps engine"): a future-tech machine that behaves like a steampunk device, its names in the maker's register ("Keep heliographic cause its dumber"). Its passive (Ryan, 12 and 13 October): its vents blast a random foe for the heat they take off it, a weapon's hit that does all a weapon's hit does, its Cryo cashed among them (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row); the heat is its own, so a vent it spends on an ally blasts nothing and an ally's vent on it blasts nothing (letting them blast is the first buff if the row reads weak); the random target is the rider's limit and the player times it (section 2's damage-rider row). Nothing else cools it, so every point of heat stays until a valve opens and the fuel is a decision, never a drip (a passive gain a round went on 20 October as a free blast a round; a small pilot light is the exit if the row reads cold). The Heliographic Siphon, Ryan's shape taken as a lure-all ("Riskier, more rewarding"): no damage, no Rapid (too easy), every foe in the fight turns on it for the round, it is Armoured for the round, and every blow landed on it adds heat, next round's fuel. The counter-profile is the point (Ryan, 20 October): the heat a blow is poor against a boss, which its vents already rip when it keeps its heat up, and good against a swarm; the reward is capped by the heat maximum and one vent a round, the risk the whole formation's blows and the overflow burning at the next turn's end. Claude's objection (no reward for being struck that scales with the enemy's blows) was withdrawn on Ryan's argument: that line struck free offence riding on a turn a tank takes anyway, and the Siphon spends the machine's own turn, deals nothing that turn and pays out a round later at random (the retaliation row carries the condition). The decision it makes: run hot on purpose, whistle at the swarm whose blows bounce off its armour, never at the brutes, and open the valves into the crowd; the Peak's Burning and Scorched are free fuel. What reopens it: the boss cells falling too easily (half the blast), the solo row reading cold (the pilot light), and the lure-all proving too swingy (the rank form, Spread, the bench's fallback). Read 3 November (docs/measurements.md): none is reached, its solo row in the middle of the tier twos' band and its bosses alone below it; the pilot light measured no lever, since heat is not what limits it, so a cold row would want another; and the lure-all reads no wider a swing than the rank form, at the few spends in a hundred the bot gives the Siphon in a team. Its model's first cut landed 2 November, and its second round the same day on Ryan's notes (“Scorpion is too literal. Ditch the whip tail and the claws and the vestigial front legs”): a tall six-legged machine, the cab's glazed nose and floodlights where the claws were, the brown boiler behind it with its vents as guns, the signal mirror on its mast, heavier on the map than the Funnel Web. To judge running.

Decided

The Swallowtail: the helicarrier, a muster its pieces spend.

Ryan's go, 21 October (its brief: git show 5d989be:docs/briefs/ws_multirotor.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier three, an assault flyer on four rotors, the maker's first capstone: the helicarrier, "the apex of steampunk-silly military technology" (Ryan, 20 October): "It carries things, obviously, but it also operates as a mobile command, and fire support. It doesn't rain death, but it directs units to rain death, offers in-fight refueling and support, and gives them a lift when needed." Its passive, the Muster Deck, has two clauses. On the map, the machines beside it ride and land as a block (Ryan, 21 October: "They move as a block, if the block doesn't fit, it can't go there"; a landing animation or a redistribution on landing may come later), reach and never a reset, a strategic piece and no movement abuse. In the fight, the Muster fills a sliver every time any die rolls (Ryan: "it takes MANY rerolls to gain a charge", "ultimate tier", "you have to work for it"), a kill by its own guns worth several rolls, each fight from empty: any die's roll plays up the team player, and per fight so the player never farms charges. Retired on the way: the multi-rotor that lent its die (Ryan, 20 October: the more he thought, the more ways he found to abuse it, and it was a boring combo piece) and the equipment slots that replaced the lending (tedious to play and to balance); the hull as the price of its pieces, the command bar (Ryan: "The carrier is not the commander entering the battlefield"), the wreck recovery as a charge, the dock's lure.

Every locked piece has a plain form and a charged one that spends a muster, which goes out unasked whenever a charge is up and it has somewhere to land (Ryan, 22 October: "Don't prime the muster charge. It just gets used if it's up. If it's an option to activate it becomes abusable, player can hyperoptimize their turns without interacting with the die RNG"; "Not using it is the player's issue, and if its always a bad call I think we need to just change the effects"). The Mooring Screens shield every ally, the team shield, honest by its number, where Armoured on all hands four machines the dearest stat and a Barrier on every ally breaks the Barrier row; charged, Phased on every ally, only while none has acted this round (Ryan, 22 October: otherwise "it can be used as a selective protection which is not the intent"), with no cooldown, since it is paid three times (the muster, the docked allies' actions, the whole enemy turn on the carrier), the lever its price, never a clock. The Spotting Flare Exposes, and charged Suppresses too, a stun gated by the price and clocked by Numbed (nothing on the helicarrier is called Mark: Ryan, 21 October). The Sutler's Hoist shields and cools an ally, and charged resets it whole. The Reveille Horn is a team reroll, and charged an ally acting again, the action-economy row's tempo lent, paid in a banked resource and the carrier's action. The Hoist reloads nothing on the Musk Ox (Ryan: a locked pairing would take the artillery's levers). The names are Claude's on Ryan's pick, the chassis's his. What reopens it: the fill reading as farming (the rate), the charged dock as a free round (its price), and three bars on one maker reading badly on a phone. Read 3 November (docs/measurements.md): the charged dock is the rarest charge the bot buys (Phased at one muster about once in fifteen fights, never at two, the rows unmoved), so it is no free round; the Flare's Suppress takes about half the musters. For later balancing (Ryan, 22 October): an ally acting first keeps the charge for the other pieces; he would rather make the forced spend work than add a control for one chassis, so the pieces may be reworked first (Phased on every spend of the Screens, or dropped for the global lure), and the primed form may come back. Its model's first cut landed 2 November from the helicarrier deck Ryan picked on 13 October, and its second round the same day on his notes (“it's a barge with fans glued to the side right now”): the rotors turn in wells through one deck, its rim running round them. It moves as a carrier, never banking or pitching, drifting and slipping through its turns (Ryan, 2 November: “it shouldn't bank at all, unlike other fliers. Drift and slip yes, but no bank and pitch”). To judge running.

Decided

The Musk Ox: the artillery, its guns fired from forged ammo.

Ryan's go, 21 October (its brief: git show bc97727:docs/briefs/ws_artillery.md). Wainwright & Sutler, tier three, the maker's second capstone and the raining death the Swallowtail leaves whole: the cold price the heat row asks for, on the maker whose kit is cold. Ryan's picture: "a cross between a Big Bertha, a M270 MLRS, and/or a truck-mounted MEADS launcher. Big. Big wheels"; wide, the old cannon on the centreline and the high-tech guns on flank sponsons ("the 'high-tech' W&S design where they tried to resolve their inherent bias against energy weapons"). Its passive, the Desublimation Forge (Ryan's name): its damaging guns spend ammo instead of heating it; the magazine opens each fight low and ramps (Ryan, 22 October, of the first round: "make it a total of 3"), and a vent it spends on itself reloads. Everything else keeps heat on a low heat bar (Ryan: "Scorched and Burn are the only way it really interacts with the heat system unless it opts into heat-generating shields, buffs, or debuffs"). Ammo is a resource beside heat and never a buff, so no Silence or Purge reaches it, and only its own pieces reload it. It keeps three rules heat cannot have: it opens low and ramps, the early rounds earning and the later spending; its old gun pays for its new ones; and a full magazine buys a salvo.

The Shrapnel Recuperator, a cluster cannon whose Flak is the team's and whose refund is ammo for each foe it hits, never a setup for its own salvo (Ryan: "a one-two that's way too obvious"); the refund repeats the Honey Badger's hopper (a crowd as ammunition for the boss) and passes the pattern-in-context rule, since here it prices every gun. The Unrestricted Expenditure, the order to spend everything: the whole magazine as strikes walking the rank it is dropped on (Sweep since 3 November; at random live foes until the randomness pass), a boss killer when the boss stands alone and the trash obliterated when it does not ("The artillery piece isn't a precision weapon"), steered by thinning the field or firing at a lone boss; the biggest per-hit multiplier in the game, so the foes' Flak multiplies with it and the strike's number is the lever. The quick reload (for testing, a working name): Rapid, ammo back, heat its limiter, so with the foes' heat it threatens an overheat on the low bar. The siege battery keeps its charge, power bought by using components; the Musk Ox's ammo is the price of firing, so spending more for keywords stays the siege battery's. Retired on the way: the reloader gated on an empty magazine, Flak 2 on the cannon (the one-two), the big gun with no refund (the closest to inverse heat), and the Hoist as its loader; the bench keeps ammo carried between fights, an overflow shell, a ranging ramp and shell types. What reopens it: ammo running plentiful (the reloads or the trickle), the salvo multiplying the foes' Flak past its worth (the strike's number), and the quick reload kept or cut once tested. Read 3 November (docs/measurements.md), once the bot fired the salvo it had been rerolling away: the team's Flak is worth a point or two beside it and the strike's number moves it most, so the Flak exit is not reached; the trickle moves its lone bosses most, the boss killer as meant; and the Quick Reload cut reads as nothing on a bot that never counts a plain Rapid entry's action again, so that test waits on the bot. Its model's first cut landed 31 October, a wide battery on four big wheels, an act ejecting a spent cell; its second round 2 November on Ryan's notes (bigger missile racks, its back vents clear, no horns), to judge running.

Decided

Wainwright & Sutler's roster: ten machines, three, five and two.

Ryan, 19 and 20 October, the tiers 3-5-2 (if 4-4-2 worked better, the Dingo would be his move down). Tier one: the Prairie Dog, the Cassowary and the Cottonmouth, a scout, a gunslinger and the reach piece, no front, accepted (the Cassowary row). Tier two: the Dingo, the Clydesdale, the funnel-web, the gunship and the Whip Scorpion. Tier three: the Swallowtail and the Musk Ox. Ten, eleven being too many (Ryan, 18 October). Nine are in content and the engine with their models (the Cassowary's, the Cottonmouth's, the Whip Scorpion's, the Swallowtail's and the Musk Ox's first cuts of 31 October and 2 November, to judge running). The gunship, whose fitted weapons raise its numbers by their tags, is a proposal (docs/briefs/ws_gunship.md), free to run since the tags and the rescale landed (Ryan, 21 October: it waited on them, "since that's its whole deal"). What reopens it: a tier one reading as a wall or a free win in the runner's bands; a fourth tier one wanted, which is 4-4-2 with the Dingo down.

Decided

The Armadillo seals: Minerva's form-changing tank.

Ryan's go, 20 October, on his framework of 15 October (its brief: git show 63487a9:docs/briefs/minerva_armadillo.md). Tier one today, pegged for tier two (Ryan, 2 November: “armadillo is probably too much of a wall for tier 1”; 4 November: tier two, and he is designing Minerva's replacement tier-one tank or tanks himself), the move landing with the replacement so Minerva keeps a tier-one front. Benched with it, Ryan's to overrule: a floor of 1 past armour on every blow that reaches hull, on both sides, since flat armour at or over a tier's blows is immunity at any number; the seal harder to hold at tier one; tier one's blows raised in the compositions. Its passive, Lockdown: a shield it spends on itself seals it, armour up, its own cooling stopped and hotter each turn; a vent or a weapon opens it; a shield on an ally and equipment leave the form as it is. The seal never stacks (Ryan: the granted armour is tied to the mode); an ally's vent still cools the sealed shell (Ryan, 20 October: allowed). Its locked Cryo Flingers (Ryan: "It launches a sticky cryo canister with a really annoying beep or something, for the lore reason") lure and mark: any machine's weapon hit on the mark cools the hitter, so the tank's output is cooling the team contends for (the Armadillo to reseal, a hot hitter to keep firing), never an ally cooling the Armadillo for free. Ryan marks Cryo for reuse ("probably a healthier way to grant ally cooling than venting directly") and allows it a minor rider later, only if the roster has room. The Faraday Hull and the Lockdown Shutters left it: the Hull's idea is the echidna's if he takes it; the shutters are the look of the seal. The forms refused: the hypertank at tier two whose shutters drew blows (a build of nothing but lures); the blanket draw while sealed ("a free pass for 3 or 4 turns"); team armour or a team shield on opening (an unkillable team by another name); the stored blows cashed as damage ("back where we started") or as rerolls ("that doesn't fit with the theme"); heat a hit so fast a seal lasts a turn (tedious); and the Hull in any form on it (the discharge again). What reopens it: the seal protects nobody without the lure, so the pair's rate is watched in play; the all-in cooling team; and the greedy bot values a lure for allies at nothing, so its runner rows wait on its valuations (the to-do). Its model shows the seal (the seal row, under Motion).

Open

Parked ideas.

Burning terrain; statuses carried between fights; player formations; two rerolls or ten entries if the Clydesdale's passive is thin.

Open

Aphelion's next chassis.

Ryan, 12 October, and his go on the roster analysis (14 and 20 October): a Gundam homage, never too on point (a shield, a handheld rifle and a beam sword make one), its passive a combo buff that grows with every blow it lands until a turn without an attack breaks it ("Every blow counts"; not the Beam tag, which "is just setting it up for a different synergy that it doesn't start with"); a jellyfish flyer in the Science Vessel's register, its width narrowed to soft control, its spread carrying Lure, Disrupt and Purge and its EMP Purge with Disrupt ("neat, good direction, needs refinement"); the Carrier, the Protoss homage, at tier three and designed on its own (the battlecruiser's session is "not related at all"). A Zoids homage on four legs, a fast walker, is a candidate whose maker is open (Aphelion or the Arsenal). A Voltron or Power Rangers homage is one of their single machines, never a combination. Cats and wolves stay out of Aphelion's names. Parked: a large hovercraft; long guns while the engagement range may change; a time-shifter, Ryan's spacetime manipulator ("soft control, moving or delaying hits as a whole identity"), its holes in the briefs README's bench. Each candidate's brief holds his calls and its open questions (docs/briefs/aphelion_*.md, docs/briefs/zoids_walker.md); nothing is built before each session's go.

Open

The Arsenal's next chassis.

Ryan, 12 and 14 October: more Arsenal machines, medieval, demonic, eldritch and sinister, none of them another Overcharge ramp. Misharu: justice, a foe whose blow breaks a shield punished, its payoff Penance, a weaken on the breaker. Mastema: fire that passes from the dead to the living, its locked pieces tools rather than a gun, leaning to fire that runs through the team (its Ewer carries the allies' Burning and Scorched onto a foe). Minos: every foe its weapons reach changes rank, not optional, a power the team works around. The Onryō: a curse on whoever wounds the team (Grudge was a working term from the myth, never the machine's word: Ryan, 31 October), never popped for burst, its kind open again since Flak took the flat on-hit. An Arsenal cleanse that moves debuffs onto a foe (the Scapegoat Brand, a rare after the vocabulary pass) is possible since Burning means something on either side. Each has a brief with Claude's first round and review, unanswered (docs/briefs/arsenal_*.md); nothing is built before each session's go.

Decided

Kestrel's line: the strafer at tier one, the bomber above it, ten in all.

Ryan, 14 October, from the roster analysis's gaps (docs/roster_analysis.md, section 8): the strafer, every weapon it fires striking twice at half, the Kite's bargain in count instead of magnitude; each strike loses the foe's armour, so it slaughters trash and bounces off plate, a detachment the map can read, and riders per blow love it; Kestrel's second tier one, so its tender deal varies. The bomber above it at tier three (Ryan, 15 October), the Lammergeier (its row below). The line decided and named on 21 October (the hub: docs/briefs/kestrel_line.md): three quarters of it flies (Ryan, 20 October), Kestrel's lean heat, rerolls, strikes and risk. Four are in content since 23 October: the Needletail runnable, the Skua since its engine of 25 October, and the Storm Petrel and the Giant Petrel since their engines of 30 October (Ryan: “go for the ready to build ones”; their briefs retired: git show d84ad16:docs/briefs/kestrel_shield_spender.md and kestrel_gun_platform.md). Their models: the Skua's, the Puffin's and the Storm Petrel's are Ryan's picks from five rounds of look tests on his notes (23 to 27 October, docs/looks/kestrel_*.png), built 28 October: the Skua the jaeger, the Puffin and the Storm Petrel on each other's hulls (his swap of 26 October: the research boat is the Puffin, at the light size; the wide hull on the giant boosters the off-tank, at the medium), and the Lammergeier the flying wing drawn first for the Giant Petrel (its row below); the Needletail's two modes (in fast, mark, hover and vector to dodge, out fast: "a fox in a henhouse") and the Giant Petrel as the catamaran are his next session's, from the fifth round's looks.

The Skua (its brief: git show f87b831:docs/briefs/kestrel_strafer.md): the swing-wing strafer at tier one, a flyer parted from the Kite by its silhouette, the seabird that harries in repeated dives (the Butcherbird turned out a BattleTech 'Mech's). Its Split-cycle Breech rolls each weapon once and halves the roll, rounded down and never below one, for each of two strikes, every buff and the foe's conditions counting on each strike and its armour too (Ryan, 24 and 25 October: "Don't roll both halves. Roll, then split", and rounding down, since equal strikes keep the die's "1 ×2" readable: "if it proves weak, we buff it in another way. The synergy it has with Overcharge and Flak alone might make it a moot point"; 27 October, the buff: "rounded down, min 1", so a Twin Autoguns' 1 strikes 1 twice where it struck 0 twice, and the die reads "1 ×2" and never "0–1 ×2"). So with basics alone it is level with a plain gun on an even number, a point down on an odd one and nothing against plate, and every stack of Empowered or Flak counts twice, on a halved 1's strikes of 0 too. A debuff fires twice too, its riders on each strike (Ryan, 25 October: "Debuff should fire twice regardless of damage. That's kind of the whole point"). Its Flechette Pod sweeps (3 November, in place of the spray of 16 October, "Choose first strike, subsequent random", retired by the randomness pass): the strikes walk the rank of the foe it is dropped on from its first filled slot, left to right, one a slot, wrapping, a kill clearing its slot so the walk skips it (Ryan, 2 November: "I would make target a rank, not a unit. Starts left to right always"; 3 November, Sweep a reach of its own: "Not direct or indirect. You pick a rank", section 5's Sweep row). Its runner rows (25 October, docs/measurements.md): alone, third of the nine tier ones on the rank and file, quick on the small packs, falling to the swarms on its 20 hull and bouncing off the big foes, the Pod its most spent entry; beside the Puffin with the Overcharge Coil, a flat buff on each strike is its way through plate, the Treant and the Bone Wyvern taken in nine rounds where they took twenty-four. What reopens its numbers: Ryan's play of its start.

The Storm Petrel: Kestrel's tier-one body, so a tier-one body comes from a second maker and the Armadillo is no must-pick (Ryan, 19 October), and a generalist that can zoom off and fight a pack alone. While its shield stands every blow on it loses a little, and its weapons burn shield in place of heat, the target taking a zap of what the shield paid, so a cold gun is how it stands still; its lock lures a foe and regenerates its own shield, self only, never Rapid, the die its brake. The plate walls the packs that strike small and is no wall against a swarm; the lever is the plate's number before the regen's stacking. Built 30 October: the shield pays a weapon's heat as the spend adds it, overshield included, the rest reaching the gauge; the zap is a plain blow after the strikes, through the foe's damage taken and armour, lost on a kill; the plate reads the shield each blow meets; the regen is a timed grant of its own each spend, bought, so a foe's purge takes it. The die shows what the shield pays for a weapon's heat in its passive box, beside the shield's mark in its colour, one number for the spend and the zap (over a foe through that foe's armour; section 6's die-entry row), never as a cost along its foot (Ryan, 4 November: “Storm petrel having shield as a cost isn't entirely correct, since it uses up the shield it has but still let's the attack go, unlike a 'true' shield cost ... Either way, the passive shield cost can't go there”: the burn behind the shield's mark in the heat slot retired); a shield cost on its weapon is paid first and never burnt into the zap, the shield paying the heat from what the cost leaves, the preview and the forecast in that order (Ryan, 4 November, asked whether it should turn every shield cost into damage: “no, lets not do it. Scrap it. It pays for heat with shield like it was doing. A shield cost must be paid before a heat cost, so it only has what shield is left over to pay for the heat with shield and convert to the bonus damage”; the cost turned into the zap too, for one rule and a home for shield-cost guns, declined). The bot reads the burn as shield lost (the hull its gap lets through and the shield that would have stood), the zap as damage and the regen as hull kept over its rounds. Its first rows (30 seeds, a direction, docs/measurements.md): alone in the Armadillo's band and above it on the ladder's recolours, at half the rounds; beside the Puffin about half again the Armadillo's pair on the big foes. Its burn on basics stays as built (Ryan, 31 October: “No, leave it as it is. Heat is underpriced, that's a global thing that's being worked on”): the heat session's prices are the lever, not the passive.

The Needletail (its brief: git show 9ced5e5:docs/briefs/kestrel_interceptor.md): the spotter at tier two (its gate reads buffs and debuffs, which no basic is). Equipment, a buff or a debuff, is Rapid on it once a round, a cantrip on every maker's setters and buffs, and its Fovea Designator Exposes a foe: in every machine's Direct reach and its armour counting for nothing, through the next round, refreshed and never added (Ryan: "go aggressive"). Exposed is the debuff's word alone: a back-rank foe with nothing in front of it is in Direct reach by the layout, and neither a chip nor the codex says so (Ryan, 26 October: a chip for it "steps on the debuff and is very confusing"; "It's not a condition. It's a layout. It's obvious what can be hit or not. Adding text around that is clutter"). Declined: the flat on-hit half of Ryan's first shape, which is Flak's kind; benched: a clock that waits for the foe to act.

The Giant Petrel: the formation leader at tier three, an ekranoplan, aggressive where the Swallowtail is the supportive mobile HQ, pushing its allies to act and keeping the tempo up: it banks the team's unspent rerolls, and its locks sell them for a reroll, a spent ally's second action, a kill's rerolls and an ally's Empowered roll, the Petrel never trading its own turn for the ally's (Ryan, 20 October: an ally going again that is not one turn traded for another). Its must-pick guard is the runner's team rows with and without it, the levers the Booster's price, the bank's cap and the Salvo's rerolls. Built 30 October: the bank carries what the player's turn leaves unspent, up to three, while it fights, a roar's cost coming off the whole; the Booster's ally use lands only on a spent ally while the team holds its three, the ally's die rolling by Rapid's path; the Salvo's two come once a spend that kills; the Uplink's roll never lands on the entry showing, and a spent die rolls nothing. The bot's look-or-action valuation: a reroll's worth is one yardstick, a share of the team's average blow; an ally's action is its die's average blow; the price is kept from the looks as the round opens while an action is worth more than they are, the Booster held for an ally to spend first; and since the bot rerolls only as a round opens, a reroll a spend pays or brings later is worth what the bank carries of it. Its first rows (30 seeds, a direction, docs/measurements.md): in Kestrel's four level with the Lammergeier's under the greedy; in Wainwright & Sutler's four over the Swallowtail's on every formation, most of it its own guns, a fifth of an action bought a fight: the team to watch for a must-pick.

The designations are Claude's (Ryan, 21 October: "Designations as you see fit"), in content. The last two of the ten, each with a brief and a first round unanswered, their tiers open: the Magpie (a working name), a support engine leaning to the thief or to its intelligence (docs/briefs/kestrel_magpie.md), and the Snowy Owl, a stealth unit, the silent hunter on the map or in the fight (docs/briefs/kestrel_owl.md). A look parked for either (Ryan, 28 October: "a good candidate for the owl or magpie"): the ornithopter blade, two F-14 pairs on their own pivots that open into an X of four wings and beat, and close along the body into a blade (docs/looks/kestrel_ornithopter.png, a look test, never content until its chassis lands). Names, numbers and looks are Ryan's.

Decided

The Lammergeier: the bomber, its shots waiting in the enemy's slots.

Ryan, 14 to 21 October (its brief: git show 62192a4:docs/briefs/kestrel_bomber.md). Kestrel, tier three (Ryan, 15 October), a heavy flying-wing flyer, big for Kestrel (a Lean bending as Leans do, as the Giant Petrel's does): the bearded vulture, which drops bones from a height to break them. Every weapon and equipment it aims at a foe may also be spent on an empty slot in the enemy's formation (Ryan, 26 October, the weapon and equipment row), and the shot waits there for whoever steps in, with its keywords; a kill clears its slot at once and the reinforcements step in at the round's end, so kill-then-mine is a tempo decision in one turn, and the Marsh's Reraise and the Meadow's Gel halves walk into it. Its locks: the Lorentz Contractor, the pull, gathers every mine on the field into its target's slot and sets them off on a foe standing there, or gathers them into an empty slot as a setup (Ryan, 21 October); the Syrinx Drone, the mark: the next weapon to kill the foe leaves its shot in the slot, Rapid on a foe already Exposed, and otherwise it Exposes the foe, never refreshing it (Ryan: "So no refresh"), so the Needletail's Expose or the Swallowtail's Flare makes the mark free. The readings Ryan agreed (21 October: "The point is to try and cheat out an opening"): the landing waits until the reinforcement step has placed every arrival, then runs left to right; a stun mine takes the arrival's first action; shots still waiting when the arrival falls wait on for the next ("if it turns out strong, that's easy to change"); a Swap and the drone take a foe, never a slot. Worth most where fights bring reinforcements, which the foes' fresh start is to make common; the pull pays in a fight with none. Its stock is stand-ins by kind, which the stock pass's table missed (the to-do). Its model (built 28 October on Ryan's call of the 27th: the chrome chevron drawn as the Giant Petrel's hovercraft goes to the bomber, airborne, "ditching the tandem wing is fine"): one flying wing with four booster drums across its trailing edge, the bay under its body with a lit seam, the Contractor's dish and the Drone's tubes modelled in, the four mounts on the wing clear of the drums (his one note). Its designation reads Flying-Wing Minelayer since; the name is his to change. Benched: the tandem wing of its go, two wing pairs fore and aft, never drawn.

Each of the cannons spends charge to gain power, such as armour piercing at one charge, penetrate with two charge, and so on, and perhaps the cooldown increases with each charge spent as well"; and the battlecruiser, the second tier three (docs/briefs/minerva_battlecruiser.md), designed apart from the Carrier (Ryan, 20 October: "not related at all").
