Pinned

Transforming chassis.

One reads only with a form-change system, which could be built and is not a priority (Ryan, 12 October).

Models

Decided

Every model is a function in code.

tools/lowforge/models_low.py holds each machine as proportions; Ryan judges renders; a Blender edit lasts only once it is written back into the code.

Decided

Hardpoints are gun mounts.

One hp_n pin each, pointing forward; fixed components are modelled in, with no pin. A shaped component takes the next mount in die order as one of five classes in its maker's metal: barrel, twin barrel, rack, blade (katar style) or dish (shield projectors, buff coils). Armour and vents show nowhere; a spare hardpoint carries a bare pad. A mount holds one component at one place (Ryan, 28 October: "we don't have variable-width hardpoints"), so a stock part is never wider than a fitted one (the Dingo's one Twin Autoguns spread across both nose ports were two hardpoints). A model names the component each pin's stock part is, and the game wears a fitted die against that record, on the map as in the fight, never against content's stock: a stock that moves on shows its class shapes until the model follows (the stock pass of 22 October had left thirteen models drawing kit their machines no longer carried, found 28 October); every model has as many pins as its chassis has hardpoints, a test holding it. A model may hide its mounts, its pins counted and naming what they carry with nothing worn there, stock or fitted (the Cottonmouth's drum, Ryan's picture of its guns “mounted invisibly inside”). Every mount takes all five classes at a size that reads, so a refit never looks worse than the stock (Ryan, 2 November: “if the player ever swaps the stock loadout, the chassis then just looks terrible? Feels like we need to fix the root problem”): what a stock gun sits in or on is the chassis's and stays when the gun is swapped, and a hardpoint declares, for each class, the variant it wears there and its scale on the pin's own (“Hardpoint just needs to declare shape (if offered) and scaling”), every other class wearing itself at the pin's scale. A variant is a class modelled in another form for the mounts its standard shape does not fit, named for its form so any mount may take it (“Can't we just model different shapes as options, as needed?”): the first, the round launcher, for a round port or a pod's nose (“some hardpoints want circular missile racks, that fixes the armadillo and i'm sure is useful somewhere else”). A scale never takes a class below what reads on the phone: a mount too small for that grows, or takes a variant. The fit sheet (tools/fit_sheet.gd) shows every class on every mount before a model goes to Ryan. The hand-built stock guns stay, each machine's own look out of the box (“Yes to keeping hand built guns”). Retired in proposal the same day: classes built by rule to fill each mount's room, his “tedious and probably an inferior result”. What reopens it: mounts of more than one width, a system of their own.

Decided

A machine is drawn as big as its class by mass.

Light, medium, heavy or assault, each chassis's size in content (the Honey Badger heavy: small and scrappy, but heavy machinery). An assault machine overflows its hex, drawn south over north; the codex and the hangar frame every machine whole.

Decided

Models are seen from above.

Anything the player must read goes on top. A launcher shows its missile tile on its front face only; a booster's exhaust is one glow, pale at the centre and deep red at the rim; Wainwright & Sutler parts look bolted on and squared off.

Decided

Settled looks.

The Prairie Dog a closed buggy on a high stance; the Baboon's fists at knee height from behind; the Clydesdale's turret pairs keep their spacing; the Stingray's fans flush, never rotors on booms; the Puffin a long chined boat with its skirt and stripe, its side plates level as repulsors under way and folded up near plumb to amplify its shield in a fight (Ryan, 27 October, on the swap: "really looking like the Puffin"); the Storm Petrel the wide hull on the giant boosters with the vents on their covers, its Riot Field shown as emitters and lit rails and never a plate, St. Elmo's fire the lit port in its snub nose; the Skua the jaeger, a jet's straight nose and wrist-swung hands, never an F-14; the Nemesis a Pacific Rim homage; the funnel-web a turret on stilts in Wainwright & Sutler's military camp that creeps one foot at a time (Ryan's good to go, 20 October); the Kite's Pinion Driver a matched pair, one at each wing tip, streamlined and symmetrical, its nose mounts the hardpoints (Ryan, 28 October), each a ramjet-gun, a drum like the boosters' tapering to a lit exhaust and the barrel forward out of a toothed inlet whose teeth run aft as strakes tapering into the drum (Ryan, 29 October: "something that looks like it gives an extra boost of speed on a kill"); the Stingray's Photosphere Lance on its dome turret (Ryan, 28 October); the Dingo's Combat Translocator a riveted cabinet with two raised prongs and a lit coil threaded on a core where its rear tube stood, totally not a teleporter (Claude's lean, taken 28 October; the coil floated between the prongs, 29 October), and its rear mount on a gimbal on the flat of its roof, left of the harpoon (Ryan, 29 October); the Armadillo's Cryo Flingers a twin canister rack in its hatch, their beacons blinking (the beep; the lean, taken 28 October), the barrels bored, bore and rim in the guns' dark steel (Ryan, 29 October), the hatch's striped collar meeting the deck on a skirt in the livery (Ryan, 30 October, of the stripe run down to the deck: “that's now a lot of hazard stripe in one area. I don't mind the extension, but maybe this is a good place for livery instead”), its stack become a louvred radiator sunk flush in each flank's chamfer above the hind hip, sized to what the slope holds there, and two near-square louvred vents standing out of the back panel low down, all of which throw their louvres open over flaring heat a pair at a time, hold them open together, shut them a pair at a time a beat apart and seal with the ports, and a vent on each flank's chamfer just ahead of the rear ports, a closed dark protrusion that slides out to vent, its thin-slatted faces glowing between the slats to the cameras ahead and behind alike, every vent's glow an orange of red and green alone, #ff7800 at its height (Ryan, 29 October: the stack's rain flap was “more W&S comical than heavy Minerva”; the vents that replaced it “big boxy radiators sticking out at an angle”, “in a darker colour, not the default hull colour”, the tail band square again and its bevel's taper liked, kept in mind for the next pass; “new vents on the sides”, “small rectangular protrusions when sealed” that “either slide out, or lever up using the forward edge as a pivot”, slid since a lever on that edge shows its face behind only, with “a larger footprint to sell”; the glow to “lean way more yellow/orange” from the ember's red, then, golden, “way too yellow”, so an orange under the line past which an orange reads to him as yellow; 30 October, after the radiators had stood as boxes about the tail's back panel and top corners: “Place them flush on the chamfered face above the leg joint, and resize to fit. Split the long rear vent into two squareish ones”, then, asked whether an idea of his was still to come, “nope, i liked what you did”; then “The rear ones can protrude a bit more”, “desync the vent openings, then close them all at the same-ish time”, “Open them in pairs, not one at a time, and leave them open longer once they're all open”, and “slow down the standing back up at the end and start it as the vents are closing. the vents can stay open a touch less long. desync the pairs closing just a bit”), and every weapon it carries running in and out along its line of fire, never pitching (Ryan, 29 October: "they should only move along their axis of firing, in and out"); its seal is the seal row's, under Motion.

Motion

Decided

Every machine has its own idle acts and its own movement, on both screens.

Four acts each, five for the Baboon, the Kite and the Skua (Ryan, 28 October: the Kite's treatment, bigger animations that show off its agility, the Kite's own among them), for the Nemesis and the Ammit, whose turns came as acts (the footwork row), and for the Dingo, whose coil has one; six for the Armadillo, its come-about and its vents; and the sealed Armadillo plays three of its own (the seal row). Foes stay painted pictures that breathe and lunge.

Decided

Machines are piloted, not animals.

An act is what a pilot does: looks round through the head, turrets or sensors, flexes limbs and weapons, test-charges a weapon, shuffles impatiently. Cockpits are fixed, except the Clydesdale's head; the Ammit's head is still by design.

Decided

Heavy and mechanical, with inertia.

MechWarrior or Star Wars, not Gundam or Evangelion. Every part chases its act on a servo slightly under critical damping, timed by the machine's heft; a blow is keyed as a slam that accelerates into the hit and stops dead.

Decided

Acts are loud and never in step.

Each moves several joints and the body at once, with glows hot enough to read across the map on a phone. A machine picks acts at random, never the same twice running, with long rests between.

Decided

A grounded machine stands still between acts.

Only a flyer rides a slow swell; a hovercraft balances like a platform on water, tipping in gusts, catching itself and settling with a heave.

Decided

Turrets turn slowly, not far, and pause before turning back.

At most 70 degrees from the bow, 35 degrees a second, and a hold of 0.6 seconds before reversing; lowforge refuses an act that breaks this. Under way a turret turns with the hull.

Decided

Walkers stomp and plod.

A planted foot never slides; the body vaults over near-straight legs and nods onto each foot, never rolling side to side; a walker turns at the waist and steps round to turn in place.

Decided

A move on the map is one smooth curve with real acceleration.

The machine turns onto its way, accelerates at its own rate, holds full speed through corners and eases to a stop; pace follows the move value, so a longer route takes longer. Drifters (the Dingo wildly, the Puffin tamer) nose in and step their tails out; the flyers and the Stingray bank; every other machine follows its heading. Packs keep quick straight steps.

Decided

An act may name the places it plays.

Travel that leaves the hex (the Kite's loop, circle and figure eight, the Dingo's strafe) plays on the map only.

Decided

Motion is data beside each model.

Joints, acts, gaits and cornering are authored in models_low.py, written to art/models/<id>.rig.json and read by MechRig; no runtime code keys on a chassis id. The hangar page's motion port changes in the same commit.

Decided

Motion is judged running.

RIFTGATE_ACTS_DEBUG=1, RIFTGATE_MOVE_DEBUG=1 and the hangar page's Models tab. Adding joints leaves the geometry identical (tools/lowforge/glbcmp.py).

Decided

Planted footwork for the walkers' turns and sidesteps.

Ryan, 20 October, after the funnel-web's pivot: the Dillo gets a turn of its own, and the bipeds lateral motion in their acts, old or new; more joints never hurt. None were needed: a hip takes the yaw and roll that keep its foot planted and pointing where it landed, the knee and the ankle the rest, the foot flat (_footwork in models_low.py). The acts: the Armadillo comes about, turning its whole shell to look (no head and no waist) in the diagonal pairs it walks in, and holds with the port guns up and the cowl lamps lit; the Nemesis braces for the meteor, a foot back as the whole machine turns on its planted foot under the waist's turn (the waist alone had turned it, the feet planted), and squares up to something at its left, a wide stamp into the stance, fists up, then back round (a sideways pace each way was built first; Ryan: it looked like dancing); the Ammit circles its prey, the snout kept on it, two moves round to one side, a pause and two back (one move each way through the centre was built first; Ryan: two to one side and back is more effective). Ryan's calls on the others: the Clydesdale keeps its look-round and its waist, since by its lore it is not supposed to pivot well; the Baboon's acts are fine and not special, so nothing for it yet. Judged running: the Dillo's come-about is good (Ryan, 20 October), the rest his to judge (the to-do).

Decided

The weapon that fired is the one that moves: it turns onto its target where that is cheap, then kicks and flares in its tags' look.

Ryan, 31 October: “It would obviously be really cool if the actual weapon from the die fired.” A spend names its component, so only the mounts carrying it move: their joints kick, a gun on the body kicks the whole machine back along its line, and a weapon on no mount (a fixed one, the fists and claws) fires with a slice of one of its machine's own idle acts, played from just before its blow with snappier servos than an idle act's, the cheapest first, where an act already shows the weapon (“For the locked weapons, go for ones that are cheapest to do first, where pre-existing animation work has already been looked at that you can leverage”: the Nemesis's fist pound, the Stingray's lance charge, the Buzzard's cannon charge, the Giant Petrel's salvo, the Storm Petrel's St. Elmo's fire and the rest the model's sidecar names); an idle act that fires a weapon stays an idle act too. One without such an act swings or kicks as every weapon did before, unless its model marks its business end: then the joint carrying it kicks and it flares there (the Honey Badger's Bullion Fragmenter, the Prairie Dog's Flakshot Repeater; Ryan's go of 3 November, “Build the rest of the to-dos”), and a piece of equipment built in plays its slice as it is used, with its sound (the Puffin's Ionizer sweeping its dish, the Giant Petrel's booster banks and its uplink). The Wainwright & Sutler pieces that fired with nothing fire the same way from the acts their models already had (Ryan, 3 November, of them: “which of those missing firing animations make sense?”, first cuts): the Whip Scorpion's mirror flashing, the Swallowtail's flare away, its screens out, its hoist lowering and its horn sounding, the Musk Ox's racks rippling and its spent cell out, the Cassowary's drum spinning up; the Musk Ox's howitzer with an act of its own, since laying the gun creeps the whole machine forward. A weapon fired often may have slices that take turns (Ryan, 3 November, of the Ammit: it “can also afford two different Claw animations since it's got 4 and will use them often”: its bite and its rake; the Nemesis's jab and cross, since it “needs to actually punch, not pound”), and a slice may wind up before its blow, the blow and its sound waiting for it (the same day: a weapon that “won't be fired every round” is “worth a little longer time”: the Buzzard's cannon, with an act of its own, its idle charge sped up, the spine lit tail to nose and the cannon flaring to life, its blow at the full heat and all of it out within a third of a second, its booster flare and its nose lift cut; the second notes that day: “charge spine up, cannon flares to life, then fades out fast as it discharges”, the slice of the idle charge it had fired with holding everything lit past the turn back). An act may be a weapon's firing alone, never played at rest (the punches; the Skua's burst, in place of a slice of its strafing run, a flown path that had carried it round its circle in the fight's frame). A firing cuts any act playing short and the servos carry the joints on from where it left them, so nothing jumps; once the slice is over they come home (the hangar's paused stage had left a claw raised). What a weapon fires out of the machine may fly: the Lammergeier's drones leave their tubes, tails lit (“I guess a new drone model that fires out, something simple”), and such a piece is wanted elsewhere (the second notes: “Lammergeier drone animation looks great. Keep those types of micro-models in mind for other animations”; 4 November: “Go for some micro models and let's see what they look like”): the Swallowtail's Spotting Flare sends a burning star up and out over the bow from each tube, which hangs there sinking as under its chute and dies, the Musk Ox's racks empty a rocket from every tube on their back-blasts, and its howitzer sends a shell down the barrel's line and throws the spent casing back into its forge mouth, which flares as it takes it (the piece's fantasy, the forge pulling its casings back as ammo). Once a piece leaves it is the world's, never dragged by the rack or the hull it left, and a piece thrown into the machine finds its place however the gun has turned. First the machine turns onto the foe where a pivot is cheap (“If there's a cheap way of aiming (torso twist, hover pivot, gimbal) then yeah lets do that. If a quad has to step around to fire like the armadillo, then don't bother, that takes too long”): the nearest joint above the mount that turns about its up axis (a turret, a waist, a head with guns), for a weapon on no mount the joint its model names or the waist, and a machine that turns on the spot (a hover, a flyer, a tank's tracks, the Dingo's casters) its whole body; a quad and a car fire where they point (the Armadillo, the Clydesdale's lance and back rack, the Prairie Dog's chassis mounts). The turn comes before the blow, and the blow, its kick and its sound wait on it (Ryan, 2 November: “make sure that the turns or aims happen before the sound effects. It's part of the whole immersion package”); it holds until the blow lands and the firing slice has played out, and eases back (3 November: the Buzzard's cannon had “turned back before the hit is complete”). Every gun flares at its business end, a class's at its own places (a variant's at its own) and a stock gun built by hand where its model found its muzzle, in its tags' look (“If the flare colour of the weapons can follow their tags, that would be really cool”): a colour as that kind of shot looks, a hot middle and a beat of its own, a gun's yellow-orange star, a launcher's slow orange puff, a fire weapon's flickering orange-red gout, a laser's held red streak, plasma's swelling green star, lightning's strobing electric blue, a blade's yellow sparks. A component with two tags blooms both, the way it delivers its blow first and what the blow carries over it, smaller and a beat behind (an incendiary shell flashes and then burns); none, its class's look. The colours carry no meaning in play, so they are free of the colourblind rules and never tuned (Ryan, 2 November: “Choose colours that make sense, I don't need to tweak them”); laid over the hull, never added, they hold on white as on black. Retired: the attack acts of before, an act a weapon kind, for the cost of an act per kind; the flare and the firing weapon's own mount carry the read instead. What reopens it: a flare that hides what it should show, or an aim that reads twitchy.

Decided

A blow on a machine moves the machine.

Ryan's go, 3 November (the weapon-fire package of 31 October; “Build the rest of the to-dos”). The model jolts away from the blow and rocks back on its body's servo; the side the blow came from flashes for a beat in heat's red-orange, laid over the hull so it holds on white paint as on black, never white, which reads bright rather than struck; a blow the armour turns takes half of each; a blow the shield holds leaves the hull still and ripples over the machine's hex in the shield's blue, so where it landed reads at a glance; a machine lost has its lit parts gutter out with a flicker and its outline dim before the wreck shows, the kill's own beat, and then it slumps (Ryan, 3 November: “they should slump or collapse after, limbs dropping, flyers and hovers landing, fans stopping, idle spins stopping”): a walker sinks on the limbs it stands on, every foot kept where and as it stands, its knees and ankles taking the sink (a hand on the floor, the Baboon's knuckles and the Ammit's low hands, kept planted too), as far as every such limb reaches within its limits and its belly clears the floor; a biped slumps forward at the waist and nowhere else; every other loose joint hangs what it carries where its weight takes it, the outermost first, so an arm goes slack at the elbow and hangs rather than swinging back, a gun dips and a head droops, never below the floor; a flyer or a hover comes down until what hangs lowest rests on the floor; nothing tips the body; and its fans and spins run down. Retired the same day, its first form (each loose joint falling most of the way to its limit, a knee folding on its own, the body sinking and tipping nose down) failed Ryan's read of it: “don't fall through the floor, and don't do weird impossible leans like the clydesdale is doing - I get that you were trying to get it to kneel, but it can keep its feet planted and just sink using its ankles and knees. Same with the bipeds, sink using knees and ankles and only slump forward at the waist. The bipeds also swing their arms behind them when they should jsuit go slack at the elbows.” One rule every model shares, judged running; a model that collapses wrongly by it gets a pose of its own. Its motion carries the hit as much as its colour, so the read never rests on a hue. RIFTGATE_HIT_DEBUG=1 plays them all in turn. What reopens it: a reaction that hides the gauges' read, or one that reads twitchy at the enemy's pace.

Open

Whether a shot crosses the field.

Tabled (Ryan, 3 November: “Table shots crossing the field for now, it might be too busy. Maybe just build a simple version so I can judge”). The simple version is off unless a debug build's Settings turns SHOT TRAILS on (RIFTGATE_SHOTS=1 on the desk): from the muzzle of the weapon that fired, as its view projects it, to the middle of what it fired at, in its flare's colour, landing as the blow's sound lands; a tracer for a direct shot, a lobbed arc for an indirect one, bowing out to the side as it rises, since the field runs up the glass and a rise straight up lies along the shot and reads as a tracer; a laser's beam held through the blow; nothing for a weapon that lunges (a melee blade, the tags row), whose lunge is its travel, so a shotgun tagged melee fires a tracer; none for the foes. What settles it: his judgement on the phone, whether it reads or only adds noise between the dice and the foes.

Open

A render margin round each skirmish machine view.

A travelling act leaves its frame on the phone: render larger and overflow (a fill cost, perhaps flyers only), or keep acts inside the frame. Discuss first.

Pinned

The helicopter style of movement stays in the engine, unused.

Decided

The Armadillo seals on screen.

While it is Sealed its guns sink along their lines into the shell, then each port's iris plug rises out of the port's floor and shuts it just under the rim, in the ports' stripe (the Lockdown Shutters live on as the look of the seal, 20 October; leaves drawn back across the shell showed outside it, so they rest under the floor, Ryan, 29 October), the louvres of the radiators and the rear vents shut flat, the side vents stay home, the cowl lamps and the vents' glow go dark and the whole machine hunkers down on its planted feet, the shell low and its brow dipped: its defensive base pose, from which it plays acts of its own (Ryan, 29 October: "When it's in sealed mode, the animations set shouldn't be the same, its base pose should be hunkered down in a defensive position"), so no open act clips the sunk guns. The flingers' rack stays live, since a sealed Armadillo still lures, and it never vents sealed (Ryan, 29 October: “I don't think a vent animation should run during the sealed form”); it opens in reverse. A model may hold a form while its machine carries the status of the same name; the form may carry the whole machine with it and acts of its own, and held it plays those, else the machine's own less any that moves what it holds, and none while it turns; RIFTGATE_SEAL_DEBUG=1 holds every form and lets it go in turn, to judge running. Three leaves a port, where a camera has more: a phone pays per draw call, and the shutting reads the same at the machine's size. Ryan judges it running (the to-do). Open, its vents stand ajar, each louvre half its throw and each side vent half out of its well, the vent act opening them the rest of the way and the seal shutting them (Ryan, 2 November: “When unsealed, the vents should be open, and then more open during the vent animation”): a model may stand in an ajar pose while a form is not held, its share giving way as an act swings the joint so the act ends where it was authored to. The form reads off the screen too (“armadillo needs indication when a die face will transform it, like a die chip or something. It also needs some highlight or something when it is transforming, and a better indication of what form it's in”): a shield it may spend on itself wears the seal's chip on its die while it is open, and a weapon or a vent the opening's while it is sealed, each read in the readout; Sealed wears the same mark, a seam with arrows driving shut onto it, the opening's thrown off it, so they part by structure; the sealed machine wears a steady hexagon shell in the shield's blue, and as it seals or opens rings close onto it or are thrown off it under the word SEALED or OPEN. RIFTGATE_SEAL_DEBUG=1 plays the shell and the burst with the model.

8

Commander and city

The commander's layer is not built; it waits for a balance baseline.

Decided

The commander's tools do what machines cannot.

Manipulation, mitigation, information, movement: never a fifth gun. A reusable ability deals no direct damage; a one-shot may, and damage one-shots belong on the battlefield (soften a pack before contact), so the machines' dice stay the killing power in a skirmish.

Pinned

The commander layer waits for a baseline balance.

It needs heavy UI work and a place on the skirmish screen.

Open

A command die, or one-shots only.

Recommended

One command die, rolled with the machines' dice.

Its entries are rechargeable abilities that cannot be hoarded: smoke blanking the back rank's intents, a drone screen, focus fire, a scan of a pack's next two rounds. Its size (d4, d6, d8) is a research path, the larger dice carrying a backfire entry or two.

Decided

Command entries come from the hangar, never from rewards.

They would be wrong as loot and would dominate every pick.

Decided

Spending a consumable must not feel bad.

Ryan hoards potions, so spending is the default.

Recommended

One-shots expire when the rift closes, and the pouch holds three.

A fourth forces a use or a discard; a starting stock chosen in the hangar restocks every battle, research raising capacity and quality. Doom Eternal's short loop is the touchstone; saving a full pouch for the boss stays a legitimate blitz, paid for in missed upgrades.

Open

Whether each Fracture of the Rupture meter grants a reward pick.

Unweighted by domain (section 4). The meter, the Fractures and the seal were built without the pick on 30 October (Ryan, 24 October: the picks are “designed only in high-level theory, and maybe not warranted at all”), since they pace the battle on their own; whether a prize rides each Fracture is decided after they are played, with the economy pass (section 12).

Recommended

The pick is one of three.

A battle-long modifier on a carried component, a one-shot, or a battle-wide boon (an extra reroll a round). Temporary upgrades amplify the build, never replace it.

Decided

Battlefield abilities belong to the city.

Sprint, airlift, teleport, tractor beam, orbital strike, on cooldowns counted in map turns, legible and impossible to hoard; city research shortens them, adds charges and unlocks more. No command die rolls on the battlefield. A skirmish component's cooldown is a printed property of its own, counted in rounds (below).

Decided

Cooldowns in a skirmish: a printed property on specific pieces.

Ryan, 14 October, after the roster analysis weighed it (its section 7): a cooldown is a printed property on a specific piece, never a general mechanic, shown on the entry as dark with the turns left, so it is paid in the die's reliability and read like any other rule; he designed the funnel-web's web bomb and the Gundam's parry with it the same day. It prices what heat cannot: a price per die rather than per spend, dear on a small die and cheap on a big one, so it falls on the cold kinetics and the big dice that heat ignores, and it is the revolver's native currency. It also fixes a reading (Ryan): a Rapid component prints its own cooldown or pays a cost, Rapid carrying no cooldown of its own (section 7's Rapid row, 2 November), and the Nemesis's fists roll again on rage, no Rapid at all (section 7). No cleanse lifts a cooldown: a price, as heat is. No cap on where a cooldown goes or how many a die carries: Ryan, 15 October, struck the recommended cap of one cooldown entry on a die below tier three as artificially limiting, and the rest of Claude's list (never on a basic) goes by the same reason. The exit: if the counts clutter a phone die or read as hidden, the shape is reworked (its first fallback, Rapid's one-round switch-off, retired 2 November).

Recommended

Some city abilities reach only so far from the gate.

Artillery covers the southern half, an airlift leaves only from the gate, so pushing north leaves support behind.

Pinned

Command entries with a battlefield meaning too.

Rejected for now: it doubles the learning per entry.

9

The invasion: domains and the party

The other world is a JRPG world, and a rift is where its overworld pushes into ours. From their side, our city is the dungeon.

Decided

The JRPG genre invades.

Final Fantasy is the spine and the genre is blended. Its flat sixteen-bit sprites against our gritty 3D machines are the clash the game is built on.

Decided

A rift's domain dictates what spawns.

A biome of the classic overworld with its bleed (the tiles the flattened ground paints), wildlife of one tactical signature, a boss and a hero. A rift rolls one lead and at most one guest, shown by the front card and the bleed, so scouting is the land.

Decided

Four domains, and the answer is tactical, never a damage-type triangle.

Elements are flavour, so which front to take is a build question. The names are working names.

DomainIts signatureThe build must answerWildlifeElitesBosses
The Meadow: grass, forestNumbers that split and swarmArea damage, armour against volume, kill orderGoblin, Dire Wolf, Gel, Mandragora; the Treant's line (massive); Rotbloom (massive, tiers two and three)The Goblin Shaman, Warcraft's on his wolf; the Elder Treant; the Leshy; the Goblin Nob, the Spire's; and the wanderersFloraison, the King Gel, the Mantis Ant
The Waste: desert, badlandsFixed damage no shield answers; theft of rerolls and entriesHull and tempo over shields; redundancy on the dieNeedler, Antlion, Cockatrice, the Djinn; the Sandworm's line (massive)The Old Man of the Desert, the king sandworm; the Pilgrim, the hunter; the Rust Monster; and the wanderersThe Ruby Colossus, the Fallen Weapon, the Emperor Scorpion
The Marsh: swamp, graveyardHard to keep downKill order, reach to the healer, burstRevenant, Ghost, Zombie Knight; the Barrow Giant's line (massive)The Fell Rider, a winged hunter; the Skeleton King; the Antiquarian; the Bone Wyvern; and the wanderersThe Matador, the Phantom Train, the Vampire Lord, the Marsh Serpent
The Peak: volcano, fire caveHard hits on a telegraph, and heatShields for the telegraph, cooling, a lure onto a cold machineBomb, Salamander, Drake; the Magma Elemental's line (massive)The Cinder Tortoise; the Thunder Ape and the Bane, hunters; the Firelord; and the wanderers (Gilgamesh, the Reaper, the Wild Hunt, the World Eater, the Shuffler, the Astromancer)The Gargantuan King, the Fiend of Fire, the Obsidian Tyrant
Decided

A domain reads on the map by structure.

The bleed's tiles and the disc under every pack, so a sprite's hue means nothing. Each domain paints its own tileset (Ryan, 29 October: “They all need their own tileset, first of all. Shouldn't all be painting green meadows”), its water and walls included (30 October: “walls and water certainly should change with tileset, even if they have no functional change”), and each ground its own tone of the domain's, two steps at most in a channel of the compositor's grid and three in all, so the grounds differ without jarring (30 October, of one ground for all: “the different types need different backgrounds. Its very monotone now. It just needed to be less jarring”; of the first build's fills, wildly apart: “the ground can differ where appropriate. Just not wildly different like it was”; and of the Waste's and the Marsh's rough, “a bit more separation from the plains colour”). Water is always the domain's liquid with a rim and ripples, and the walls always a range standing out of the top of its hex into the hexes above (30 October: “The wall objects on all can be larger, or at least taller”), so a hex's rule keeps one shape in every domain; the paint never sets or leans the map's shape (the shapes row). Retired: the first tilesets of 30 October, a fill a ground with marks drawn in rectangles and water and the walls alike in all four, which failed his read (“some of the decorations are just weird or bad”; the Peak wanting “a more volcanic palette”; the Marsh’s that “does not read undead marsh or anything really”). Built again the same day (DomainGround; “Overall the art is a huge improvement though”): outlined pixel sprites, the Meadow's woods, cottages and snowy peaks, the Waste's cacti, sandstone ruins, oases and red buttes, the Marsh a dark bog of dead trees, graves and crags, the Peak black rock split by glowing fissures, a fire temple, lava and volcanoes; the Marsh keeps no violet, the genre's poison swamp, since violet is the rift's intent. The Peak's lava is an orange-red, never the yellow-orange that read as acid (30 October: “it looks more like acid than lava (yes, lava is hot orange-yellow in real life, but this is a painted game)”), and stops short of red, where a red lake hid a pack's red mark from a deutan; since the enemy's marks went violet (30 October) a redder lake would hide none of them, and he kept the orange-red with the fight's backdrop on the same palette (Ryan, 30 October: “lava looks great”). Every colour sits on the compositor's posterize grid and is scored by the three colourblind simulations on the ground it is drawn on (docs/measurements.md). Judged running (the to-do; RIFTGATE_BLEED_DEBUG=1 paints the whole field).

Decided

The Gel is a wall, not an attacker: its halves step up in its place at once, a pair in one slot.

Ryan, 25 October, from play: "when a gel dies, it should spawn the half-gel immediately in place, not wait for the reinforcement phase. Gel doesn't need breach, it's a wall not an attacker." So a kill on a Gel never opens its hole, and its Engulf lost the breach. The halves stand as a pair in its own slot (Ryan, 25 and 26 October: going to the pool "is so anti-thematic"; "bend the 4-column rule for the gels ... Cramming two small slots into a single slot so we don't have to redo all the AoE rules (I.e. aoe treats the double-slot the same as a single slot, but single-target weapons don't)"; "half units should exist as a pair taking the place of one unit"): one slot to the formation, an area blow reaching both, a single-target blow one; full while either stands, so no mine goes in and a drone's half leaves none while its twin stands ("Keep it simple, a half is treated as occupied"); one falling leaves the other the slot alone, and with both gone it is a plain slot ("The split state only exists for the units that created it"). Claude's leans he agreed (26 October): a Swap moves the pair together, a mine waiting in the slot goes off on them as on one (a single-target shot the left half, an area shot both), a web on the left half catches its twin, the left acts first. The twin slot is drawn as the slot's two halves, each half its own view at its pixels' whole scale, so a half is drawn smaller than a whole foe on the phone (his read), and a half alone keeps its own half of the slot until both are gone (Ryan, 2 November: “a half unit should not take the full unit slot when it's other half dies, only when both are vacated does it return to a full size slot”). The halves' round is over as they step up (Ryan: "Halves, or any summons, shouldn't attack the round they're spawned, no"): no intent, no Lure or Disrupt to turn, a stun taking their next action, striking from the next round, so a kill never adds blows to the round it was made in. And "we need to make it clear when something joins the pool": a foe joining the reinforcements (a half with no room, a summon, a foe getting back up) flies off its pack into its rank's count in the gutter, which goes up with a burst as it lands (RIFTGATE_POOL_DEBUG=1 keeps it going). Ryan, 26 October: a summon "should take a slot if available, else go to the pool" (the nearest its summoner's column; "ideally it would take a slot immediate always"), and a foe getting back up does so "in-place, immediately, just like the gel splitting. It makes clearing that column harder, which is a good thing", its round over too. A summoner needs no condition to summon (Ryan, 26 October: "Just a lower weight and the guarantee that they can't do it twice in a row is enough. They should be a priority target, not left because you know they won't summon till the pool gets low"): the Ghost's Raise weighs a special's 1 against its bolt's 2 and never comes twice running, and the condition that counted the pool left the engine with it. An area weapon in hand brackets a twin slot's pair as one slot ("show both halves selected as if they were a single slot"), whichever half it lands on behind the scenes. A half stepping up sets off a mine in its slot, as any way in but the reinforcement step's does, so a Syrinx Drone on a Gel mines its own half. Exit: the Meadow's fights read in play, the Gel as a wall that volume or kill order answers.

Decided

A tier's rank and file are recolours and their own units: a repaint, a name and a bigger number, with a twist where one is marked.

Ryan, 28 October: the roster fills by tier with placeholder monsters or classic palette swaps "so that ramp balance can begin", ideally with more threatening mechanics as well as more stats "so the game isn't just a numbers race, but for now a numbers race is kind of what we need"; a recolour by hue is fine "as long as the colour is sufficiently different and they have a modified name or prefix or something", and no other marker. So a recolour is its base's sprite shifted to a hue the three colourblind simulations keep apart from the base's, lightness with it, named by a prefix or a word of the ladder's (Gel, Bubble Gel, Metal Gel), at the tier's numbers (section 3's row), and carries one twist from the mechanics the engine already runs where Ryan marks one (a rider, an aim, a condition, a special, a countdown, a last act), none where he does not; its tier is content's tier, and since 30 October the compositions are the lists (section 5's pack row), each dealt at the tiers it names, the spawn lists by tier derived from them for the harness, the runners and a boss's escort. Built 28 October: twenty-six recolours, two on each base type, their sprites by tools/recolour.py; six more since 31 October, the massive lines' (the stand-ins' with their third moves back as the twist), and the Marsh's massive line drawn as rough placeholders. A placeholder monster is an entry with no art, drawn as the views' placeholder shape, for a role a domain lacks. Retired: the recolour tier held open against section 3's rule, which is an aspiration now.

Lean

The domains' levers, and their identities sharpening by tier.

Leans by each domain's fantasy, never exclusions: a boss anywhere may carry any, and a guest's members stand in a formation from tier two (section 3's row) so no hard counter steamrolls a domain (Ryan, 28 October). The Waste purges, theft of buffs beside its theft of rerolls and entries, and sharpens by breach on more of its members, bigger fixed blows and a mirage's Barrier. The Marsh cleanses, "debuffs are hard here": its healer's cure and cleanse, reraise, regeneration, a sanctuary's Ward and a delayed heal, hard to keep down and hard to mark. The Meadow silences, the shaman the commander, and sharpens by count, the swarm's add and a wall's armour, numbers that split and swarm. The Peak burns, and sharpens by the telegraphed spike, primed claws and countdowns. HP is the general ramp's number and the damage's shape the identity's (section 3's row); the ladder of units that carries each, and the enemy's buffs, are git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md's.

Decided

The leaders are the party: heroes on a quest, played straight.

In line, stepping forward to strike, never mugging. When a domain's Anathema fills, the commander is the dungeon's boss and its hero comes, in its rank beside its wildlife.

Decided

The party is dealt at the run's start.

One hero to each domain from its bench, shown on a party card (four silhouettes and job names); each Anathema bar names its hero. The deal keeps two front jobs and two back; no job rolls (only the player's dice do). It draws on a stream of the run's seed of its own, so the rest of the run rolls as it would without it (sim/campaign.gd); the party card waits on the mission map.

Decided

The benches.

The Meadow: Warrior, Martial Artist, Tamer. The Waste: Thief, Lancer, Merchant, Gunslinger. The Marsh: the Cleric, the Augur, Rune-bearer. The Peak: Black Mage, Summoner, Mage, Sorcerer, Dragon Heir. Any domain: Blue Mage, Psychic, Dancer. The first two of each are built; the Marsh's Necromancer, the weakest homage, was dropped for the Augur (16 October). Robots stay off (or one late wildcard); flying heroes are hunters with reach. What each brings, in a line (names are working names): the Martial Artist two strikes and a counter; the Tamer a back-rank commander calling the wildlife up every round; the Lancer a Jump announced this round that lands next, breaching; the Merchant blows that take salvage off the tally; the Gunslinger a line blow into our back rank; the Augur (Clair Obscur's Sciel by archetype, Ryan, 16 October; a working name, the omen-reader's job) an Omen named at one machine that lands two rounds on, and below half every omen due on every machine at once, the countdown and the every-machine blow on one move; the Rune-bearer an execute on a machine below a quarter of its hull; the Summoner its summons, each a move rather than a companion, from the Norse world its rune circle opens on (Ryan, 15 October: names not the series' own, full Norse): Skaði's frost and Surtr's fire in its final, in Shiva's and Ifrit's places, and Fáfnir its promoted form's, in Bahamut's (his suggestion); the Mage a second caster's kit; the Sorcerer shadow for fire (corrupt-all and heat-all on one hero); the Dragon Heir the pair in one body, massive below half; the Blue Mage learns the components used against it; the Psychic the old die attacks in a child's hands; the Dancer another action for the wildlife. Hero-plus-beast pairs (a summoner's esper, a dragoon's dragon): the hero human-sized in the back as the brain, the companion massive in front as the hitter, and killing the hero first makes the companion dumb; a pattern for a later hero, not a rule. The heroes' tropes are to come from beyond Final Fantasy (Ryan, 15 October: the heroes need to focus on tropes that are not the series'), balancing the series out a little and never purging it (Ryan, 16 October): the Lancer stays the dragoon, way too iconic to lose; the Warrior stays the plain fighter, Dragon Quest's hero being ambiguous; and the Thief takes Persona 5's Joker under its plain name, for the extra tongue in cheek. The rest is proposed in docs/heroes_proposal.md. The Cleric is the homage to every white mage and priest, the Priest folded into it (Ryan: their kits would be much alike; the Cleric his pick, the genre's generic word), and the Martial Artist stays for now.

Decided

The Cleric carries Dia, Cure and Holy; promoted, it readies its allies.

Ryan, 15 October, of the five moves written for it. Cure is the question a Marsh build must answer, reach to the healer. Reraise went from its final, since getting back up is the Marsh's dead's (the Zombie Knight carries the same move, the Vampire Lord gets up once), and came back as its promoted form's one move more at the finale, cast on its allies, the other party members, never itself (Ryan, 16 October: appropriately threatening): a healer that readies the party to get back up is the finale's own escalation. Its first pick is a party member, the heaviest hitter among them. Protect left its kit.

Decided

A hero is human-sized in its final and massive at the finale.

In its final, a chibi party sprite: fighters in front, mages behind the wildlife, so killing the healer is a reach problem. At the finale its promoted form (the class change) is its own entry, in the boss register: what it holds of its own is what the class change adds, its size (2), its armour and one move more, and the rest is its hero's, its name included (Ryan, 21 October: the same names, flagged if they feel thin), since section 3 wants a new foe, never an old one with a bigger number. A job costs two drawings. Its one move more: the Cleric's Reraise and the Summoner's Fáfnir, whose mechanics are open; the other six are open (section 12). Its art is its hero's until the boss register's is cut. On the map a hero only ever commands the boss unit, which patrols a hex a turn whoever commands it, so a hero carries no map move and no hunt of its own (Ryan, 28 October, of the Thief's: “it's a boss so it doesn't need it”); a hero that walks the map would bring them back.

Decided

The finale is the dealt party, promoted, at the capital.

The classic four (Knight, Ninja, White Wizard, Black Wizard). A hero beaten in its final leaves its column to boss-tier wildlife of the domains still standing. The Convergence is the party's level, and its last tier wears the final dungeon's cosmic register, home of the Lovecraftian dial. The stock pass's bands read it a wall for every rung on a team of three (docs/measurements.md). It stays one for a stock trio, since it is fought at a run's end by a squad of four with a run's kit, and it is read once the campaign runner fields built teams (Ryan, 23 October, agreeing with Claude's lean; the runner's player fits nothing it finds today, section 12's campaign question). It reopens if a built squad of four is walled there too.

Decided

Three bosses beaten set a domain's final: every rift it leads is its final.

The third boss beaten turns every standing city's rift the domain leads final at once, its hero the boss the mission select shows, and every rift it leads after is dealt final (Ryan, 26 October: "If the final is set, then all open rifts should change to show the final as the boss in the mission select"; built that day), so no rift opened before then brings a plain boss after. Four finals must be impossible, so there is always a choice. A final counts only when the hero dies and its fight is won. No hero flees: the Thief's Flee, planned since 15 October as the catch a final paid for, is struck (Ryan, 2 November: it “just raises complicated questions”, whether he comes back for the finale and whether the rift even closes), and Showtime, a blow at every machine below half, is its third (2 November: “showtime is great”). The battle is won when the hero's fight is won, its escort down with it, and a team wrecked after the hero falls has lost it (Ryan, 27 October, closing the mechanics audit's 26 the engine's way: the whole escort must die, since a final's hero is the smallest boss and dropping the escort would make the easiest fight easier still; the text that said "whatever is left of its escort" was written while the fight could still be left after the kill). His reason: "it removes the requirement for any boss to be a super tank to be difficult. A buffer or healer like the white mage or whomever now becomes a real threat if you have to also take out its entire escort. And the player must be careful to not rope in other packs when engaging", the roping-in retired with the rings on 6 November, since nothing joins a fight, so the escort alone stands with the boss (section 4’s boss row). Each domain needs three distinct bosses before its hero at least, and more are welcome (Ryan, 3 November), drawn without repeats, from the genre's iconic bosses, each a named entity (the register row below); the engine already draws from a list, so the slate lands as content alone. The slate's twelve landed 30 October as placeholder kits, three a domain (the tiers row below), ten of them bosses since 3 November with the Fiend of Fire (Final Fantasy I's Marilith by archetype, in the Cinder Tortoise's place: “an obvious insert for the peak”), twelve since 4 November with the Mantis Ant (the Meadow's third) and the Marsh Serpent (the Marsh's fourth), the Waste's third the Emperor Scorpion since 4 November, and the stand-ins went where Ryan put them (24 October; 30 October: “they are rank and file after all”): the Treant, the Sandworm and the Magma Elemental into their domains' formations at tiers one and two, two moves each as the rank and file carry at tier one, and the Bone Wyvern into the Marsh's elites.

Decided

A boss scales to the battle's tier: its numbers and its escort.

Ryan, 25 October: "I think we need a way of scaling bosses to tier. We don't need to add moves to start, just scaling stats and upgrading the escort. Else we'll end up designing ourselves into a corner, unable to change how many battles in a tier, how fast anathema rises, general scaling, and so on." On 26 October, after hearing section 3's row against it: "Scaling bosses doesn't feel nearly as bad to me and seems like a fine compromise for the sake of run diversity" (the row now carries the exception), and "Make sure that boss escorts are composed of units from the tier. I expect they will be hand tailored at some point though." Built for a domain's boss, a final's hero and the finale's line alike: their HP and attack by the tier, and more escort, drawn from the domain's spawn list at the tier (the lists by tier since 28 October, the highest tier's under it standing in while it has none; never massive, the columns being few), a named escort kept for the hand-tailored ones to come; every number a placeholder. A run keeps its bosses unique where the content allows ("which we should try to"). The ramp's share waits on the ramp (the curve's form). Retired: the slate tiered, each domain's three bosses one a tier (Claude's lean), which repeats a boss when a domain is fought twice at a tier, leaves the finals untiered, and would hold a final for the last tier against the spawn flow ("you'd have to wait until tier 3 with full anathema? Doesn't make sense"). What reopens it: a boss that reads as the same fight at every tier, whose next levers are riders on its blows or extra attacks (Ryan, 26 October), or escorts that want hand-tailoring, each boss's own in content. An escort composition at a tier, when one is written, is the boss's hand-tailored escort in place of the tier's units round robin (the hook, 30 October): the King Gel's court at every tier, its merge's fuel, and since 31 October every other boss's at tiers two and three, at the tier's counts, each picked to sharpen its boss's mechanic (Needlers with the Pilgrim, so every kill feeds Karma; Bombs with the Gargantuan King, a second blast to shield for), placeholders for Ryan's marks; a boss stands alone at tier one but the King Gel, whose court is three, five and six Gels (six at tier three since 2 November, so the court fits the field's eight slots). The quick campaign's boss brings the same escort (2 November). A boss whose kit is armour grows it a tier from a lighter start (Ryan, 31 October: “just the ones that have it”, when “2 is a massive hurdle for a first battle”): the Cinder Tortoise's 1, 2 and 3, since at 2 its base was the tier-one hurdle for the small-blow kits and its shell was not (docs/measurements.md), and a tier-one team has little against armour; every other boss carries none since 2 November (Ryan, agreeing Claude's lean on the read of 31 October: a boss's armour by its fantasy, as a machine's is by its role; the Ruby Colossus, the Fallen Weapon, the Phantom Train and the Obsidian Tyrant keep a point, placeholders for his marks).

Decided

Anathema is the Rupture meter's weighted tally.

Each kill by its menace, never capped, every battle however it ended (built 30 October, section 3's tally row); the count of three bosses still decides the final, judged against the tally and the game's length when Ryan reads both.

Decided

Every foe move wears the genre's name where it has one.

Protect, Shell, Cure, Fire, Fira, Flare, Meteor, Holy, Steal, Mug, Stone, Self-Destruct, announced on the plaque. The engine's words and the player's components keep ours.

Decided

One rule set for foes.

Each has an aim. Rank and file carry one or two moves at tier one and up to three at tiers two and three, the twist the third (Ryan, 28 October, on the ladder's move counts: a numbers race with a twist is the ladder's point, so the Antlion Queen's swallow, the Spectre's and the Wraith's hallow, the Death Knight's grave guard and the Wyvern's dive stay, and the Red Dragon trims to three, its Stoke the one to go); a boss or hero three, the third a signature opening below half. A special never fires twice running, replaces the blow, and is an engine effect never keyed to one foe. Ryan, 24 October, of these and the move counts: "The rest is fine." A condition reads the foe's own side (below half, hurt, its kin standing, its queue, one of its own hurt or not readied) or what its move needs (a machine carrying a buff, for a silence): it keeps a move from doing nothing or saves it for its moment, and a move without one fires on its weight. None gates a threat on the player's state (Ryan, 24 October: every special needing a condition "was never a rule I made, nor does it even make sense at all to have", and "I don't want to overcomplicate the enemies"). Retired: the gates that read the player's state (a machine at a heat, a machine showing a weapon, a reroll left), which held back the very threats they gated, the Drake's Stoke never in a first round or against a cold team, the Black Mage's Fira all but silent and the reroll gate never closing (docs/measurements.md, 24 October). The enemy mitigates by armour or recovery, never by a shield of its own, and every foe on the field can be reached by a die the player has, every round it stands there; a foe that leaves the field (a flee) says so on the plaque before it goes, and none does but a loot-runner whose prize goes with it, the treasure goblin's shape (Ryan, 2 November: “Don't give foes flee unless its like a treasure-goblin equivalent ... a pop-it-for-loot-before-its gone kind of deal”; whether the archetype is wanted is open, so none is built), so the Needler's Scatter and the Thief's Flee are struck (the Thief's on 2 November: it raises the questions of his return at the finale and of the rift's closing); a move with a countdown (the Lancer's Jump) gathers on the field, reachable, its warning shown and its blow landing the round its count runs out (15 October). Its interrupts attack the die, and its front rank is the lure that reach answers.

Decided

The roster is a working set.

The foes got their fresh start on 30 October (compositions, the ramp, the elites: section 4's rows); roles and the elites' numbers wait on Ryan's marks (section 12). Ryan, 13 October: the compositions and the engagement rules together set the fights' shape (section 4's pacing row), so the fresh start is upstream of the area keywords, the reach premium and any chassis that reads a rank.

Decided

The roster comes in tiers, and size, rank and complexity are apart from the tier.

Ryan, 15 October, his terms sharpened 23 October: an elite or a boss may be any size, the rank and file may be massive and stand in either rank, and a front-rank foe need not be fodder. The rank and file are the genre's tropes (goblins, skeletons, ghosts, drakes, slimes, wolves), simple and advanced. An elite is a spawn of one or more unique packs outside the usual spawn list, a mini-boss tier (23 October): a visible curveball that demands the player adapt, by a mechanic on the map (a dangerous hunter, a fast blitz on the gate, a sudden swarm, an aura or a battlefield effect) or by being a harder fight that threatens the gate while it holds the team, which is what a tier-one elite looks like; a guaranteed miniboss event in the middle of a battle “to break up the flow, add depth and drain the player's resources” (24 October), about one an early battle and more later, worth double on the Rupture meter and in Anathema. The bosses, three a domain at least, are a who's who of the genre's iconic bosses and its signature monsters; no story's final boss (no Sephiroth, no Paintress, no Lavos). The finals are the heroes, homages spread beyond Final Fantasy. A massive foe of any tier fills both rank slots of a column and may stand in a spawn list (24 October): the Treant, the Magma Elemental and the Sandworm are rank and file in their domains' formations, each with a line of recolours at tiers two and three, and the Marsh has a massive line of its own, the Barrow Giant's (31 October: “We also probably need more massive foes, yes?”). A boss's mechanics stay inside the skirmish until there is a good reason to let one bleed onto the battlefield (24 October: it still stands); an elite's may work on the map by definition.

How the tiers run (built 30 October to 4 November; the rules say the form): an elite's moment is a Fracture, so every team meets it at the same point of progress however fast it plays; a domain's elites are compositions led by a foe flagged elite, each once a battle and again in a later one (Ryan, 2 November: elites are reusable, bosses never in a run), every elite scaled by the tier as a boss is (“Scale the elites by tier”, since a tier-one elite met at tier three would be a rank-and-file fight); a wanderer is one composition in one domain's list that every domain's Fracture may draw, one time in three in place of the domain's next own elite and always once its own are spent, at random among those not yet sent (3 November; ContentDB.next_elite, WANDER_CHANCE a placeholder), so every domain may meet them, none waits on them and nobody learns an order; a wanderer never waits for a domain's own to run out (2 November). Each domain holds three elites of its own at least and three bosses (the slate, docs/bosses_proposal.md, which lists every one with its slant and its source; the register row below says what qualifies). The Matador stays a boss on Claude's call of Ryan's “wherever his mechanics fit more”. The elites' numbers were set at the rank and file's band and read as free wins at 30 seeds (4 November, docs/measurements.md), so they are section 12's question: the massive band, an escort each, or the curveball read on the map. The elites session's rounds, Ryan's words on each foe and the demotions are git's (git show 1fa1155:docs/design-bible.html, this row; git show e75be15:docs/briefs/elites.md).

Decided

A foe carries tags, as a component does: flavour the codex shows and the sound and the animations read, never a mechanic.

Ryan, 4 November, from Jenny's foe-down pools (undead, demonic, eldritch), which were keyed by the retired families: “Seems like we're looking at enemy tags as well, for flavour reasons. I like that. 'Undead, skeletal' 'Animal, demonic' 'Undead, skeletal, flying' and so on. Cool codex-like stuff, and really the only hook is for audio AND possibly attack/death animations, same as how we're using weapon tags.” So a foe's tags stand beside its name in the codex, Jenny's pools and a death's sound and look may read them, and no rule does (the tag row, section 7). The working set and each foe's tags are content's, proposed in chat first; built with the re-keying of Jenny's pools (the to-do).

Decided

A boss is a named JRPG entity; an elite is the domain's own grown, or a homage from anywhere, made retro.

Ryan, 3 November, reading the slate by homage, trope and generic. The bosses are “pure JRPG homages only”, each a named entity: a species is none, so the Malboro, the Tonberry and the Adamantoise left the lists that day (the Tonberry “an obvious elite”, the Malboro “could be a rank and file”, the Adamantoise demoted since “it doesn't represent a named entity”), and the Behemoth stays as the King Behemoth, the form its games name a boss, renamed since its name is “too on the nose for players” even where the rule allows it, as Ifrit's and Shiva's were: a name in the vein of the word that became the adjective for the thing, with King on it (“Dreadnought King, Gargantuan King, Monstrosity King, Colossal King, and so on”), the Gargantuan King Claude's pick of his pattern for his name pass. “It's also fine to have more than 3 bosses in a domain.” The elites are the “domains own grown where it makes sense and plays well” (the Elder Treant a Treebeard nod, the goblin shaman Warcraft's, pushed into Thrall on his wolf), “pop culture homages mixed with JRPG homages”, and the properties are open, Dungeons and Dragons, Warhammer, the Witcher, “other modern roguelikes, or modern fantasy games/JRPG/tactics games”, made retro; “things that don't make the cut ... can be considered for rank and file as well”. Monster Hunter with care, his favourite series: Deviljho the original invader, a wanderer, and Rajang placed. No suicide boss (“I don't like suicide bosses in the same way I don't like fleeing bosses. You either bring burst, or you suffer”: the Mother Bomb out). The roster the register gave is the tiers row's; every name and number is a placeholder for his content pass, which is what reopens a call. The Meadow's third boss is the Mantis Ant (Secret of Mana's first boss by archetype) and the Marsh's fourth the Marsh Serpent (Final Fantasy VII's Midgar Zolom by archetype), both 4 November (“Mantis ant is a fine addition, so is Marsh Serpent”); the Waste's third the Emperor Scorpion (Golden Sun: The Lost Age's King Scorpion by archetype), landed the same day on his word (“If there's an iconic golden sun boss or elite candidate, we should include it. Whether that's the waste boss or something else”), the first Golden Sun homage on the foes' side, the series one he wants pulled from though “Manticore isn't the strongest one”; its other candidates are section 12's.

Decided

The boss mechanics run inside the skirmish.

For the bosses' slate, each an engine effect any foe may carry (the one rule set row), never keyed to one boss: a countdown (a move gathers, its warning chip counting, and lands that many rounds later; Suppress holds it, Disrupt breaks it off); a boss in parts (Ryan: parts that regrow, or stand, until the core is killed, so the player chooses what to focus: the core is Guarded while all its parts stand, or any, a fallen part may grow back, and the core's fall takes its parts); a last act (a blow the moment a foe falls, before anything else and even as the fight is won, so the shield goes up before the kill); foes helping foes (a cure on the most hurt of its own, armour on every foe through the next round). Built 30 October with the slate's first seven: a follow-up (a move names the move its foe announces next round once it lands, so a warning comes a round ahead, the Matador's cape before its flurry and the Cinder Tortoise's shell before its quake; a Suppress or a Disrupt on the first breaks the pair, never by the roll; the second, once announced, cannot be Disrupted, only Suppressed (Ryan, 2 November: a stun on so telegraphed a move was his worry and Disrupt the bigger one, so “cannot be Disrupted” on the follow-up's chip; a Disrupt held over the foe strikes through as a gated keyword does, the bot values it at nothing, and the countdown stays Disruptable, since interrupting the big hit is its question); a steal (Gilgamesh's: the entry a machine shows is switched off and taken, and swung back next round at its own number, a fake for 1 when it was no weapon, so what the die shows is a decision when he comes for it); and a swallow (Floraison's, and the Ruby Colossus's Whirlsand since 2 November: a machine out of the fight for two rounds, until the swallower falls or until it has lost a quarter of its HP since, never the last machine in the fight; a second machine taken joins the first's count). Built with the last five: a merge (the King Gel's: every Gel on the field taken into it, regrowing what they had left and lifting its blows a point for each, Empowered that halves at the round's end, so half of it reaches the next round, so thinning the Gels first is the answer; its court is its own escort, an escort that names its boss); a drain (the Vampire Lord's: a blow that regrows what it took from the hull, so a shield starves it, since one of shield and hull walled the slowest trio whatever its HP, the shield no answer to the heal; no Ward turns it away since it lays nothing on the machine, Burning its race, as section 2's burn never stops healing); a grudge (the Pilgrim's Karma: a point heavier for every foe the team has killed this fight, so kill order turns over); a condition that reads a fallen part (the Obsidian Tyrant's countdown of five to a firestorm on every machine, opening once its rider falls); and a move used once a fight (the Vampire Lord readies its rise once, where the Zombie Knight's line may ready again). Built 4 November on Ryan's reads of Monster Hunter: a mode (the Thunder Ape's Golden Rage: Golden for the rounds the move says, its blows harder under a point of plate, a quarter of its HP lost since or a Suppress breaking it and the stagger its next action lost; the swallow's release with the lost action the swallow's row refused, since the bold play's pay is the mode's point, and the lost action the exit if it reads as a free stun; a Purge strips it, and it goes gold again only once calm; a stun that lands breaks it, and one a Numbed stack absorbs does not, Ryan, 4 November: Numbed is the stun lock's price and the rage's own clock and quarter still break it) and a crush (the World Eater's pin: Crushed on the machine, Defense Down by archetype, a point more on every blow it takes a stack through the next round, Flak's shape on the other body so the chip is a number and every machine feels it; an ally's Cleanse lifts it and a Ward turns it away). The swallow is hard control on the team, the first: answered before it lands by a Ward, a Lure, a Suppress or a Disrupt, and after it by focus: the swallower lets its machine go once it has lost a quarter of its HP since the swallow, its chip counting the damage left, or as it falls (Ryan, 31 October, agreeing Claude's lean on his Clair Obscur stagger: a damage threshold, a round of the team's focus where a third would be as long as waiting; the swallower loses nothing else, a lost action on top, the stagger's bigger form, waiting on the plain release reading weak). What reopens it is a swallow that reads as helplessness in play (section 2). The first last act in content is the Gargantuan King's Meteor, the first bosses in parts the Fallen Weapon, its two eyes Guarding its core, the Obsidian Tyrant, its rider on its back, and the Ruby Colossus (2 November), its two tentacles guarding nothing, parts that hit and grow back, so the arms or the body is its question. None leaves the skirmish (the tiers row). The greedy bot values each; every weight is a placeholder for the runner, and the bot values no swallow, since it prevents no damage, so a boss cell reads the bot there.

Open

Foe roles.

A rank is a foe's only role until Ryan proposes a system.

Pinned

The genre's superboss.

If one follows the party through, it is an optional fight past the Rupture, never the finale.

10

Meta and resources

What a run earns and spends, and what outlives it. The numbers in sim/campaign.gd are placeholders until the economy pass.

Decided

Two currencies, split by what earns them.

Salvage (materials), from kills and loot, makes things. Favour (the Council's fee), from won battles docked for gate damage, buys services and stock. A loot detour earns salvage but risks Favour. A resource per domain would let the map solve itself, so there is none.

Decided

A kill is a kill.

Every foe killed pays salvage however its fight ends, tallied only at the battle's end, so there is no count to farm against. Kills are to be weighted by danger, like the Rupture meter. Hit-and-run farming went with the withdrawal (6 November): a fight ends in the pack’s fall or the machines’ wreck. Weighted by menace since 30 October (two salvage a point): the reinforcements pay a trickle and the rift's bounty, the field at the start, the elites and the boss, is where the salvage is, one of the three prices on a stall (section 4's stall row).

Decided

Loot lies on the map.

Wrecks and alien remains, common, seeded with the map, claimed by ending a move on one. Each holds salvage plus components or, in the rarest kind, a chance at a blueprint (modules too, never a research token). A tap shows its kind and likely contents, never the contents: the decision is the detour. Not built.

Open

The loot's shown names and density.

Working names Derelict, Carcass, Relic; never "chest".

Decided

Only a blueprint builds.

For salvage, without limit; any other component is one copy. No chassis blueprints (unlimited machines flatten the roster), and a locked component is never a blueprint.

Decided

Tiers are depth, and a maker never maps to a tier, with one exception.

A chassis's tier is complexity and a component's is power (below). Any maker may field a tier three. The one exception, a chassis rule only (Ryan, 13 October): the Arsenal fields no tier-one chassis and makes no basics, because it is the Council's trickle (section 1), and it is never strictly better, or it becomes the endgame chase; its tier-one components exist, and the Nemesis stocks them under "a chassis stocks only below its own tier" with no rule changed. A run starts with three tier-one machines, four squad slots and the basics' blueprints (tier zero).

Decided

A chassis's tier is complexity, a component's power; modules are flat.

Ryan, 12 October, agreeing Claude's lean: a chassis's tier is a sharper passive and more locked pieces, a higher ceiling and a lower floor, never more power, so the starting machines stay viable all run; with unlocked components free to move, a new chassis is a new sandbox if it is compelling enough. A component's tier is power: blueprints are the growth curve and the basics are meant to be replaced. Modules are flat, their worth in fit and their count capped by slots. Blueprints of tiers one to three are found, or their components come on a bought chassis of tier two or three. A tier-one machine's stock is basics beside its locked piece, Slay the Spire's opening (Strikes and Defends beside a card of its own); most tier ones lock one, and a tier one shows its identity with basics alone, with a locked piece or without (Ryan, 15 October, keeping the roster analysis's test). Ryan, later on 12 October, agreeing Claude's proposal: a component carries its tier in its entry, and a chassis stocks only below its own tier (the validator holds it), so tier one needs no special case and a tier-three component is only ever found, free to be a real heavy hitter or a game changer. Tier three holds the most unique effects and the most raw power, tier two most of the synergy pieces, so no build has to farm tier three for its capstone. Ryan, 24 October: tier three is for "highlight, chase pieces", and four machines in all tier-three gear is not "a healthy endgame", so the run deals it as a chance (by the rift's tier, in git show 85e28fd:docs/curve_proposal.md). A bought chassis brings one copy of each component it stocks, free upper-tier pieces to play with, never their blueprints (owning a component never teaches building it, 7 October; only a blueprint builds, above).

Open

Modules as a priced stat bundle and a priced rule?

Ryan, 12 October: what if a module were a random stat bundle and a random rule, each priced? Claude: possible, and the prices (below) are the tool. Generated to one budget, a rule's price taken from it and the rest spent on stats, no module is bigger than another, only shaped differently, so an offer of three asks which fits rather than which is best, the decision rolled stats lack. The holes: a rule's price is an average over machines (the Booster is worth nothing on a flat kit and a great deal on a rolled one), so fit decides, which is the point; the generator must not pair a rule with the stat that cancels its cost; generated modules need names; and a module that is a single item, never a blueprint, keeps a lucky shape from being farmed. A middle way: the catalogue's rules, authored and named, each with a stat line generated to the budget. Discuss first.

Open

More turnover in the roster.

Ryan, 12 October: making machines easier to scrap would turn the roster over. The holes: turnover by punishment costs the player a favourite; turnover by attraction (offers worth taking) costs nothing. Wrecks are rare while a team of three wins nearly every fight, so the foes' damage against the team's durability is the open axis that sets the wreck rate; the time a wreck sits out (one turn) is the other lever. Permanent loss stays refused (wrecks are recoverable). Discuss first.

Decided

Every stat and passive has a price, measured.

Ryan, 11 October: the way into the module economy, and the same for components. The method: every machine alone, greedy, each bump given to every machine against the same run without it, the mean change in win rate read in hull; the runs are in docs/measurements.md, read as bands. What the first prices said, which the design leans on: armour is the strongest line a module can print and flattens past a point; a shield line is worth less than its number; heat and cooling lines are filler except on a hot machine; a rule is worth what its kit makes it; a passive ranges from nothing alone (the cars', the Badger's, the Puffin's, which work on the map or on allies) to the Kite's, worth a chassis; a module's tier tracks its worth no better than a chassis's. Caveats: one greedy bot, a win rate that saturates, and a foe mix of many small blows that flatters armour. Components by knock-out runs, then tier ladders (the to-do).

Decided

Offers, never trees.

After a won battle the host maker offers three things, one of each kind, a chassis, a component and a module, all from its catalogue, and one is taken free (Ryan, 13 October; the component a single copy, never a blueprint, confirmed 22 October), so a machine is on the table after every win against better kit; the boss drops a research token that unpacks into a choice of three blueprints of its domain's theme at the Convergence's tier, so choosing a front chooses the build path, tier-three blueprints come from late bosses, and host-maker offers shape the roster by the fronts chosen, on purpose. Blueprints come from the tokens, from a rare and dear one on the shelf, and from the rarest loot (the loot row above); Salvage builds copies of owned blueprints. Procurement sells components and chassis for Favour, refreshed each turn, a chassis on the shelf more often than not, since a maker whose city is never fought at reaches the player no other way; the capital sells the Arsenal's trickle, gated by the Convergence; a fallen city's maker leaves the shelf. Not built.

Decided

Components are never modified.

So a destroyed one stings once. The chassis is what is tuned, by modules kept through wrecks; its passive is never swapped.

Decided

Wrecks are recoverable, and the loadout is the cost.

Permadeath is refused, so no loss ends a campaign early.

Decided

Between campaigns the pool carries, never power.

A blueprint found once may be offered again but must be found again. Only the pool, the codex, difficulty tiers (Slay the Spire's ascension, each clearable from a fresh start), one Sponsorship piece and livery carry.

Decided

Sponsorship.

At a campaign's end, won or lost, each maker whose measure the player topped offers one rare component for the next run's kit, with a plain notice in its voice; the player takes one. The measures are relative and never shown live: Kestrel hexes moved; Minerva damage taken on shield and armour; Wainwright & Sutler dice spent on entries that deal damage (a vent that blasts counts: Ryan, 22 October, "it should count"); Aphelion dice spent on entries that deal none. The Arsenal's notice comes only to a campaign that reached the Convergence's top tier. The tallies are built; the offer at the run's end waits on the catalogues.

Decided

Livery is unlocked for good, free on any machine.

Sponsorship unlocks colours; no rule reads paint.

Recommended

Each maker the player topped unlocks one colour, its offer taken or not.

Decided

The loop aims at fifteen to twenty-five real decisions over twelve battles, none a chore.

Open

Every number.

The economy pass (docs/economy_pass.md), with the runner's spending policy built first. If components lost per won battle pass about one, the destruction threshold moves, not the rule.

11

Technical plan

How the game is built, measured and kept honest. README.md has every command.

The engine

Decided

Godot 4.8 in GDScript, exported to Android in portrait.

Hexes are axial (cube implied), pointy-top. The project runs 4.8-beta2 until 4.8 ships, for the fix of the Compatibility renderer's MSAA leak on Android (Ryan, 30 October: “Let's update Godot so we can get the msaa fix”; the phone's rows below), and follows Godot's releases: a session checks for a newer build every two weeks and brings it to Ryan with what it changes here, the move his call (30 October: “check for godot updates periodically”; the fortnight Claude's).

Decided

The simulation is pure.

sim/ never touches scenes, runs headless and draws every random number from a seeded generator, so rules test headless and runs reproduce.

Decided

Numbers are questions, never stored totals.

Every stat, damage figure and component property is read through sim/effects.gd, in one fixed order, and nothing writes to a stat or a component. Every buff, debuff, reward, passive and module is a modifier with a source and a scope (the order and the scopes are in docs/rules.md).

Decided

Content is data the build validates.

sim/content_db.gd checks content/*.json in the tests, so a broken rule fails the build. Internal text lives under design; a draft the engine cannot run carries "status": "planned"; runtime defaults come from content, never a hardcoded id; a new term enters the vocabulary first.

Decided

A battle saves after every change and resumes where it stood.

A phone kills apps unasked. sim/save.gd keeps the map by its seed, every unit and live modifier, the turn and both random streams, so a resumed fight plays on blow for blow; another version's file loads nothing; scene runs and tests never write. The run and the profile save too.

Balance

Decided

Balance is measured by a ladder of bots and read as bands.
