Random play (never rerolls, spends anywhere legal) beating a pack means too easy; greedy play (the best one-round trade on the player's own forecast) losing means a wall; the rungs landing together mark a cell with no decision; personas sit on top; two more rungs read the coordination gap, blind play below the greedy and the planner above it (the next row). tools/balance.gd runs every chassis alone, the starting team and each maker's first three against every pack, boss and domain into docs/balance.md; since 4 November --packs elites fields every elite composition at each tier it names, its elites scaled as a battle scales them, for the elites' cells (their first read, 30 seeds, docs/measurements.md). It is read by stage (Ryan, 23 October): which foes and compositions are harder for an early stock trio than for an endgame four, never how hard the roster is as a whole; the starter is a trio the tender deals, stocked, and the endgame four is fitted from pieces and modules up to tier two, tier three being powerful and rare by design, until the components' power curve is known. Pick share is the component signal. Play tests decide feel; no win rate is chased to a decimal. What a run can say (13 October): one cell near 50% carries a band of about 18 points at 30 seeds and 7 at 200, a row mean over twenty packs about 4, so a cell is read at 200 seeds or as a band, a quick run is a direction and a row that cites one says so, and every run a decision cites goes in docs/measurements.md, dated with its seeds, where the row keeps the reason and the exit. The greedy bot values only what it knows: a verdict on a mechanic it cannot value (a sentence, a doubling, a turn of the ranks, a wound) is a verdict on the bot, and says so.

Decided

Two rungs read the coordination gap: blind play, and a planner that looks two rounds ahead.

The greedy rung is a one-round bot that sets up only where the setup pays within the round, so the gap could not open on it (docs/measurements.md). Blind play is the tests' own (section 2: "a team that spends each die on the nearest foe"): the greedy's rerolls, then each weapon and debuff on the nearest foe in reach, each shield, vent and buff on its own machine, a die held only where its heat or its Volatile bite would wreck the machine, the gauge being in view. The planner plays the greedy's game and, at every spend with more than one worth making, plays its best few out on copies of the fight through the next round and its enemy turn, the greedy rung playing the same seeded futures for every candidate, and keeps the spend whose fight is then worth most, the greedy's pick on a tie (sim/policy.gd). So it values a mechanic the greedy cannot price by what two rounds of play make of it, and nothing past two rounds. It costs about four greedy fights a fight, so it runs on the team rows. Its rollouts once priced a Shock at the team's average blow, so a Shock debuff read as a bad roll and was rerolled away; a blow's Shock is now worth the biggest blow an ally still to act shows on that foe (23 October). What reopens it: a changed share near nothing where setups should pay, or a mechanic slower than two rounds (a boss's), which a longer horizon or more futures would read at more cost.

Decided

A component is balanced against the components of its tier; a chassis alone is a diagnostic.

Ryan, 12 October: chassis serve different roles, so a solo win rate is no main indicator, and a weapon is never balanced because a lone Prairie Dog or Kite loses four on one, a fight it should lose or win only at a real cost in hull. The measures that matter: a component against its tier's others in the same hardpoint of the same machines (its value and its pick share), the team rows, and whole runs. One machine that warps its kit never dictates the whole roster of components (Ryan, 13 October, of the gunship).

Decided

A piece is priced by its effectiveness, read in vanilla points; heat is priced up from the heatless vanilla; a rolled gun is its mean.

Ryan, 28 October, on the pricing run (docs/pricing_proposal.md; its runs in docs/measurements.md, 27 and 28 October): a keyword carries a price as a number does, so "the real metric is average EFFECTIVENESS. A tier 2 shield that shields only 4 but grants 4 overcharge is a strong component. So is a 1 damage splash 1 weapon that applies 4 flak" ("It's not a precise science, we have to best guess"). The baseline is the vanilla at each tier, a plain number with no keyword, as a card game prices its vanilla creatures by cost; vanillas are real pieces in the pool ("Sometimes you just need a big stick to make the build reliable") and sit low on the scale ("otherwise the game flattens to nothing"), the floor of their tier, every keyword piece and every hot piece above it by what it offers and what it pays. Heat is priced from the heatless vanilla up: "A component is allowed to have higher effectiveness for higher heat", and what a point of heat buys in effectiveness is estimated empirically, never set as a floor by hand (the proposal's first floors were "entirely arbitrary"). A rolled gun is its mean: across a run the roll flattens to its average, "so effectively, at the asymptote, a rolled gun is just a heatless flat gun anyway. Rolled damage is flavour", believable bullets and explosions, and a cool random gun against a reliable hot one "is perhaps a choice of preference. But statistically, it really isn't"; so a range is priced at its middle (and since 2 November is its middle, section 5), and the heat row (section 5) no longer counts it a price. The measure (the pricing driver's vanilla ladder, tools/pricing_run.py): a flat heatless gun in a bench hardpoint at every number, every piece read in vanilla points, the heatless number that takes the same rounds off the row as the piece does, its points less its own number what its keywords buy or cost there; the same vanilla across the heat classes for the knee where a kit starts paying, the heat price read past it. Open, Claude's caveats of the same day: a rolled gun's thresholds (a kill its top alone makes) are second order, read on the rolled guns' points against their means; heat's price is a knee by kit and not a slope, so it is read where the kit pays, and a cold kit's free ride is the maxima's and the cooling's question; a shield's and a vent's effectiveness needs a bot that spends them (the planner, a persona), which the greedy is not.

Decided

The pricing pass of 28 October: the floors, the heat as measured, the keywords' prices, and the few numbers that moved.

Ryan's go on Claude's leans ("I trust your leans for this pass"), on the vanilla ladder (docs/measurements.md, 28 October; the proposal docs/pricing_proposal.md, retired with this row). The scale: a piece's vanilla points, the heatless flat number that takes the same rounds off a bench's row; on the car a point buys a third of a round up to 6 and a seventh past it (the knee where a blow kills any rank and file outright), the flat Indirect reads as the flat Direct, and the hot bench reads no number at all, only the keywords that reach a second foe. The floors, each tier's heatless vanilla in vanilla points: 2, 3.5, 5 and 7, about half a round a tier on the car, an even ramp in kill time rather than in points; a tier is a band from its floor to the next, its keyword pieces above the floor by what the keyword buys and its hot pieces by what their heat is allowed. Heat as measured: on a cold die heat 1, 2 and 4 cost nothing, heat 6 about a point (the third round's overheat, the heat row's test) and heat 8 two, or a quarter of a big gun's number; on a hot die heat is paid in hull and never rounds, since an overheat burns the excess one for one (section 5); and on a nine-entry die (the Clydesdale bench) a hot piece never reaches its knee, since its slot fires a ninth of the spends, so heat's price scales with how often the piece fires, the die's size a lever beside the maxima. So the warm classes are flavour on today's kits, a cool gun soft cooling on a hot die, and the allowance a hot piece carries over its floor is a design choice the cold die cannot price; the lever that would make heat 4 bite is the maxima (10) or the overheat's cost, section 12's question, never a piece's number. The keywords in vanilla points on the car: Flak 2 two, Burn 2 two and a third, Burn 3 and the Chain nearly three, the Shock one and a half beside strikes, the Breach about one, Penetrate 3 a third there and the piece no vanilla matches on the hot bench; a rolled gun reads a third of a point under its mean and a strikes gun a point under its total, the threshold a kill needs and the armour each strike pays. The fills, on his go: the Overclocked Railgun 10 to 8 (tier one's outlier at 8.8 points against a floor of 3.5, unable to move up a tier since the Buzzard stocks it; Breach and heat 8 kept, 10 and the capstone 12 left for tier three's finds); the Coriolis Accelerator 3 to 4 (4.2 against tier two's floor of 5); tier one's rolled vanillas to a mean of 4 (the Slugthrower, the Howitzer, the Plasma Arquebus and the Shotfire Rack 2 to 6), since at 3 they read as the tier-zero Laser Carbine; the Howitzer's Splash 1 (the chip Ryan named, 0.9 rounds off the car against 0.5 plain; the Meteor Artillery keeps none while its Burn 3 rides every foe struck, a fire on five foes at either number); the Ram Frame's hull +24 to +8 and the Artillery Rangefinder's the same line, read on the Indirect bench first (the Clydesdale's Howitzer slot, the sixth bench: the Frame 0.4 rounds off its row on the Clydesdale's strikes, the Rangefinder 0.1, its back-rank rule reading on the one back-rank foe type a tier-one formation fields, where tier three's field three; the tiered formations are walls for a stock trio (Wainwright & Sutler's row wins 29% of them), so its rule is read on the per-tier ladders with a tier-matched kit). What stays, with its reason: the Arc Fault Cannon's 4, read on Chain's form of the 29th at about 4.8 points, inside tier one's band, and about 6 on the formations (section 5's Chain row; its 6.7 points of the 28th were the kill and repeat's); the Cyclic Cannon and the Blazethrower within a direction's noise of their bands; the Meteor at tier two's top, its heat paying 0.7; tier two's setups, the Probe and the Condenser until the bot's valuation (the to-do); every shield and vent, since no bot spends one on these benches (the planner and a persona that shields and vents before the overheat are the next run's first part). Each landed delta is read again at 200 seeds (docs/measurements.md, the pass's entries). Exits: a floor moves when the tiered formations stretch the curve (the ladders per tier once the fresh start's ladder lands); a keyword's price moves on a bench whose row reads it differently; the hot guns' numbers move when heat bites more.

Decided

The roster is widened so compositions can be read.

Ryan, 13 October: the chassis proposals serve two things, feeling what mechanics are possible and giving the runner enough machines to see what a team of four can be. A support machine is never balanced against a damage machine as like against like; the unit of balance is the composition, and a solo row is a diagnostic (above). If every machine is a solo carry, a battle with four is trivial, so the roster needs machines that are only good together.

Decided

The battle and campaign bots stack on the fight bot as floors, not players.

sim/battle_bot.gd plays a battle by one plain map rule; tools/campaign_run.gd plays whole runs.

Decided

Every number inside a fight is single digits or low tens, derived from the tests and never from the old content.

The old scale (damage at fives, the rest at tens) came from an upgrade system since cut, and its ratios were never designed (Ryan, 21 October: "those hull numbers sound very high to me compared to the damage numbers"; "let's not make any assumptions based on existing content for any number, whether that's heat, damage, armor, shield, or health on both sides of the field"). The magnitudes, held to section 2's kill-time, coordination-gap and wear tests: a blow 1 to 6, big guns to 10, one capstone 12; an amp a point a stack (Armoured, Empowered, Flak, Burning), which is the floor that keeps blows at 2 to 6 rather than 1 to 3, since two Flak stacks on a blow of 1 would triple it; foe HP 2 to 8 for rank and file at tier one and to 16 at tier three, 30 to 40 massive at tier one and to 56 at tier three, 12 to 24 heroes, the slate's bosses 48 at tier one, 60 and 72 at tiers two and three (30 October; a core in parts 40, a part 8 to 12, a boss that may rise once 40), where 80 to 100 was first written for them (Ryan's instinct: the big foes want more hits, for the gap to show): at 80 the default team took 7 to 13 rounds against each alone and a stock four 11 to 22 where the kill-time test asks three to five, at 48 the default team 4 to 7 (docs/measurements.md, 30 seeds, a direction), and Ryan left the number to the scaling (30 October: “Whatever you think works best for scaling”), so 48 stands until the kill time read on built fours, the squad the player fields, says otherwise; a foe's blow 2 to 8 (the rank and file's mostly 2 or 3), a massive foe's 7 or 8, a hero's 4 to 10, a boss's 6 to 10; machine hull 20 to 50 by size, 20 the floor so a telegraphed finisher never takes a machine from full (the Kite's paper hull 12, its row's one-off); shield 6 to 16, shield regen 0 to 2 (none set yet: the triangle row), base armour 0 to 2 by role (section 5); heat 1 to 20, the maxima 10 to 14 (the Musk Ox's 8), cooling 0 to 2, a gun 0 to 8, a vent 3 to 8 (the Entropy Condenser's 10 the one capstone vent, paid in hull, as the guns have their 12), with 1 to 30 the fallback if heat proves unbalanceable at twenty steps (Ryan: "1 to 30 is fine as well if heat seems unbalanceable"). Still magnitudes, not steps (13 October): any whole number; a move is a count of hexes (section 4). Ryan, 21 October: "I do like the numbers in general. They are readable to a person. So I think that we should work with this for now." Every converter that crosses heat into another axis (the overheat, the Whip Scorpion's blast, the Afterburner, the Buzzard's regen, the Puffin's growth, the Musk Ox's ammo, Cryo, the Stingray's Suite, a machine's Burning and Scorched, the hot threshold) is read on its own merits at this scale, never carried over by ratio ("Just because we have mechanics that currently rely on converting heat to other values doesn't mean that they're currently doing the right call"). The campaign's gate, salvage and Favour are another economy and keep their numbers for the economy pass. How the numbers are set: the first fills, the old numbers divided unevenly, failed the tests (trash died to a die or two, every team won every pack but the finale, a massive foe took three to twelve rounds), so the landing set them by measured loops against section 2's tests on the levers the brief names (the foes' blows, cooling and the guns' heat, the massive elites' armour and regrow), and Ryan marks the structure, never a hundred numbers (Ryan, 22 October, agreeing); our hull and shield down a quarter and cooling 0 were read and set aside. The runs are in docs/measurements.md, the fills in tools/stock_fills.py (the brief: git show a550c2e:docs/briefs/stock_pass.md). Ryan kept the massive HP at 30 to 40 (23 October, section 2's tests row): lower HP would close the room the gap needs, so the kill time is read on a squad of four and the gap on built teams; the gap not opening on a lone massive foe is section 12's. What reopens it: the balance report's bands, the rounds-to-win gap and Ryan's play.

Open

Foe blows against shields, vents, heat, hull and armour.

The axis that sets a fight's wear and the wreck rate (section 10's turnover row). Ryan, 12 October: our armour may be too high; lower our numbers before raising anything, which the scales row did (above). Lower armour narrows the gap between heavy and light machines; what makes a team's fight hard is how much it fights at once, several packs or a formation, so the axis is read with the foes' fresh start (section 9's working set) and the campaign runner.

The phone

Decided

The phone is the reference glass.

A Pixel 9 on the Compatibility renderer, and the desk draws with the same renderer (Ryan, 29 October: “yes, switch the desk to the phone's renderer”), since the Vulkan renderer it had lit in linear space where the phone's lights in sRGB space, so a lit part's colour on the desk was not the phone's (a dark glow tile the phone multiplies up to its colour showed dull). A lit part holds its colour on that renderer: the light a glow multiplies is eased by the power 1/2.2 first, as the Vulkan renderer decoded a colour before brightening it, so a flare builds in its own colour and nears white only at the very top (Ryan, 30 October, of the two side by side: “I liked the colour-holding flash, since a lot of these are supposed to be looking 'hot' instead of 'bright' and white doesn't really sell that”); the plain multiply had turned every flare past a few times the rest light white, the Kestrel ember's and engines', the Baboon's eye and the Buzzard's charge among them. What reopens it: a glow that wants white. A halo for lit parts (Ryan, 30 October, of a halo shell over the other form, a second render of the lit parts alone blurred over the machine: “Sure, let's try it”) is worn by every machine's hot parts: its boosters and vents (Ryan, 31 October: “applied to all 'hot' things - so all glowing boosters and vents. Others on a case by case basis”), the other parts he names hot as he vets them (the same day: the Buzzard's cannon, “a heat cannon”, its spine's cells “aren't vents”; the Nemesis's chest furnace and the condenser venting it, the Ammit's rear vent (its twin, at the tail), the Lammergeier's drone tubes, the Stingray's lift fans, the Dingo's Translocator coil, the Storm Petrel's arch), and a burn weapon's lit parts (“every burn-causing weapon”, on the silhouette classes, so no chassis owns it). A part that must read hotter wears one larger (the Nemesis's furnace: “that thing has to look HOT”). Some burn always, hot at rest and really hot as their flares rise past them (Ryan, 31 October, of the Nemesis: “not burn crazy hot all the time. Just like, hot, then really hot”; at ten times its rest light it had): the Stingray's fans (“always on with heat glow”; their downwash blows under the wing, where no camera looks, so a lit lining round the blades carries the heat from above), the Puffin's repulsors as it hovers, the Nemesis's furnace, and the Nemesis's and Ammit's rear vents (“an Arsenal signature, I think, a creepy glowing exhaust”: a Lean). Round each, a soft shell grown out along the part's smoothed normals by a share of the machine's size, so it keeps that share however the machine is drawn, and drawn a little toward the eye, so a part in a slot glows out over its walls; it fades in as the part's flare climbs over its rest and spills past the machine's outline, the outline pass keeping the soft fringe and every other machine's pixels as they were. It adds its light rather than laying its colour over the machine, so a part's hot core shows through, and its colour is the part's without its grey, held to its hue and leaning red: a glow cooler than its core, never white (bright, not hot) and never an orange that reads yellow or green to Ryan. A flat part's shell spreads round its rim, never off its face, though a flat disc's still stood off it as a copy, so a part that must wear one is given depth (the Puffin's repulsor discs had floated “way far out”, and are pucks now: “if the solution is to give them geometry, then go ahead”); a part standing proud of a thin plate is drawn where it is, since pulled toward the eye its halo showed through the plate when it faced away (those pucks under their level plates, a violet wash over the blue). Its first form, laid over the machine seven units out in its clipped colour, was “way too much” (Ryan, 30 October, in the hangar on his phone); its second, a fifth smaller and a fifth fainter than now, he wanted “cranked up a little bit, for phone scale” (31 October); twice the size on the Stingray's exhaust made “a big ring around it”, and a plume along its thrust replaced it there (the boosters' row below). The Stingray's fans burn three rests over their own at rest, so a surge jumps (3 November: at eight they sat near their full orange, a surge only clipped their red, and their halos, drawn toward the eye, grew through the duct walls: “they just kind of bleed through the fan walls”); their halos are drawn where they are. A form sized on the screen, as a camera's bloom is, cannot read the same in the hangar and the game, whose views are drawn at different densities. Godot's own glow, tried first, cannot tell a lit part from white paint on that renderer and stops at each machine's outline, since every machine is drawn in its own see-through view. A screenshot read is reproduced on the desk machine at the phone's layout and density and fixed once for every glass; several "phone bugs" were layout or arithmetic the desk machine hid. tools/phone.sh (a Web build over the LAN) shows layout and touch; tools/android.sh (a debug APK over USB) judges frame rate, outlines and sound.

Decided

A booster burns in its maker's way: Aphelion's as a plume, Kestrel's as a halo.

Ryan, 3 November, of the plume on the Stingray: “The plume is actually very good … I would apply it to all Aphelion boosters as a rule, and keep the aura glow for Kestrel's boosters, that's the difference we've been missing.” A plume is a soft flame along a booster's thrust, turned to the eye about its line, its core under the outline pass's cut so it is never inked as a solid shape; it is gone at rest (“It needs to dissapear when idle”) and burns full as the booster's light reaches eight rests over its own. Kestrel's halos are larger and hotter than a vent's, and burn hotter on the move (“Kestrel boosters aren't all that impressive when they bloom. It's too subtle at phone scale”). A booster's shape may want the other: the Kite's wingtip drivers burn as plumes from their lit rings, over the exhaust's taper (“kite's wing boosters need the plume due to their shape”), a little larger on his second notes that day (“Plume for stingray is great ... Kite plumes are in the right spot but a tiny bit too subtle”). What reopens it: a booster whose look the rule gets wrong, settled by its model.

Decided

A lit part's colour says what it is, and holds it when it flares.

Heat glows red to orange, a shield's light in the shield's blue, lightning an electric cyan, a fire weapon red, and none flares to white (Ryan, 31 October, of the Puffin's amplifier and the Storm Petrel's field: “since both are related to their shield-related passive, could they glow in a shield-ish colour? Base colour can change to enable that”; of the Dingo's Translocator coil: “classic 'tesla lightning blue'”; “Fire weapons, like the blazethrower, need a red glow, not a white one”; of St. Elmo's fire: “a tinge of something other than white”). A burn weapon's fire sits on its silhouette class wherever one is fitted, swapped or stock, never on a chassis (Ryan, 31 October: “on the actual swappable classes, not on the model ... So its not chassis-specific”): a pilot flame in a barrel's muzzle, glowing shells in a rack's tubes, a hot edge down a blade, dim at rest and flaring as the weapon fires, wearing the halo; content says what burns (its entry's keywords hold burn), and the strike names the component that struck. A lit tile is banked dark enough that its brightest flare still shows its colour: the shield's at thirty times its rest light, the coil's at sixteen, the fire weapon's at eight. The cyan is paler and greener than the shield's blue, so the two part; heat's oranges stay short of the one that reads yellow to Ryan. A part that burns always or flares far glows one even colour: a tile with a hot centre read yellow held on (the Stingray's fans), and one with a metal rim flared white (the Puffin's repulsors). The Arsenal's rear vents, its creepy glowing exhaust, burn a sickly orange-yellow on a tile of their own, brighter at rest than they had (Ryan, 3 November: “The standard vents could use more yellow too, it looked more 'sinister' before when it was more of a sickly orange-yellow than a deep red”; “Resting glow on the standard Arsenal vents can come up”), their flares held to a gold short of the lemon that reads green; the colour carries nothing in play. The Nemesis's chest furnace flares hotter than the vent and the visor it lights with (the same notes: “Nemesis chest vent can get bumped up even more when it flares”). What reopens it: a part whose glow means something no colour here says.

Decided

Nothing is one device pixel thin, and nothing on the map's overlay grows with the zoom.

The 720x1280 design stretches about 1.5 times on a phone: text is linear and pixel art nearest, the safe bands stay clear, and the map and the machines render at the glass's density, always (the fine map, its option gone: Ryan, 30 October, “set fine map on as the default and remove the option”). The map's grid and the machines' outline keep one width on screen at every zoom, smoothed (Ryan, 30 October: “I don't want the outline to thicken as I zoom in”; both grew with the zoom, and stepped zoomed out): the outline is one unbroken line round each machine, through the wall as before, drawn round the machines' own layer, since the engine's outline grew each of the models' flat faces apart, broke into pieces round a machine and grew with the world. So does every overlay mark (Ryan, the same day: “all UI overlay type items should hold at one width. It will look better”): a line's width, a dash, an inset, a glyph, a plaque, a gauge and a tag keep their zoom-1 size, while what a mark outlines or fills (a hex, a region, a route from hex to hex, a ring sized to its hex) stays on the map. His read on the phone: the whole map “looks way sharper”, the lines growing with the zoom “the biggest culprit”. MSAA runs on the phone as on the desk: the project runs Godot 4.8-beta2, and 4.8 stable once it ships, for the fix of its leak on Android (on a phone's GPU the Compatibility renderer set MSAA up again every frame, godotengine/godot#97967, fixed by #124643; Ryan, 30 October: “Let's update Godot so we can get the msaa fix”); the suite and the renders came out the same on it as on 4.7.2. The anti-aliasing is the player's setting, AA MODE: off, FXAA, MSAA 2x or MSAA 4x, the last the default (Ryan, 30 October: “Can we add an AA mode setting, not just for debug?”), so a weaker phone can trade it for frame rate; in the FXAA mode the map's compositor runs FXAA of its own on the 3D (the battle map's alone: a skirmish draws as with none), and in every mode the machines' textures read their mipmaps, so a machine drawn small never sparkles (Ryan, 30 October: “a lot of aliasing on models and hex grid lines on the phone”); edges come from the fine map, rendered at the glass's density at 60 fps. The traces' creases are the one thing still a device pixel wide at every zoom, and the grain that is left (his read: “its the traces that cause the graininess, with AA on it doesn't look too bad”); they stay. A debug build's TRACES toggle draws the map without the creases, to compare. What reopens it: the phone's frame rate or heat with MSAA or the outline on, or the Pixel's memory climbing with MSAA (AA MODE down, then).

Decided

The map's colours.

The HUD colour is the player's own; violet is the rift's and the enemy's, every foe's mark and the packs' intent alike (a striped two-violet field under the intent, light #c8a4ff and deep #6a30c0; the rings, bands and marks on a deep #2a0e4a edge, never black), the red-red only where a unit is harmed (a stop with no room, the gate's integrity low), never a foe's doing (Ryan, 30 October: “keep purple for enemy activities”). The rings part from the routes by structure, a ring of dashed hex edges against a line with its target; what reopens it is the two failing to part on the phone.

Decided

Under the battle map, a strip.

It holds a held readout or the orders board, never an overlay on the hexes; the log sits off screen behind LOG. The readout reads as the fight's holds do, never as a block of text (Ryan, 26 October: “For everything on the map, it all looks text-block-ish”): a name, its gauges with their numbers, then a line a fact. What reopens it: the strip judged on the phone.

Decided

A battery plan: the weld built, the props' fade and the 3D at 30 not yet.

Weld each machine's meshes by material within each moving group; fade props per hex inside the baked bands; drop only the 3D to 30 fps while nothing in it moves fast (the 2D stays at 60). The phone already holds 60; this saves battery and heat. The weld is built (25 October, game/ui/mech_weld.gd): at load, in the skirmish, the codex and on the map, the parts that move with the same thing (the body, a joint, a weapon's pin) and wear one material become one mesh, and nothing on screen changes (each welded mesh keeps its parts' scale, so an outline grows as it did; a group's trace is its parts' own edges on one mesh; a framing reads the parts' boxes; the importer's levels of detail come along). A part that moves, glows, scrolls or hides on its own needs a joint, a pin or its name in the sidecar's glow_parts (lowforge lists the acts' and gaits' glows and the treads there), or the weld merges it with its neighbours. The fourteen models go from 917 meshes to 521 and a machine's draw calls about halve on both screens, the battle map's whole frame drawing a third fewer (docs/measurements.md; frame times are the phone's to read); RIFTGATE_WELD=off on the desk, or the WELD toggle in a debug build's settings on the phone, draws the parts as authored, to compare.

Open

Phone reads owed.

The sound set, the overlay at one size zoomed in (the outline's width, the plaques and tags at their zoom-1 size), the grid back at its old light, the intent field in motion.

The documents and pages

Decided

Each document has one job.

docs/rules.md states the rules as they are now; this bible records decisions and why; docs/status.md says how things stand; docs/todo.md lists what is next; docs/vocabulary.md is generated from the vocabulary; docs/measurements.md holds every run a decision cites, dated with its seeds, so a bible row keeps the reason and the exit condition and never the run. A rule that changes is rewritten in place in the rules and the bible in one commit; neither keeps a changelog. The status legend at the top says what a Decided row may be and what a Lean is.

Decided

One hangar page.

tools/hangar.html holds the Codex, Content editor, Names and Models tabs over the published content; Ryan's saved edits wait in its database until a session lands them. It is republished with tools/hangar_data.py's map after any content, model or render change.

12

Open questions and next steps

What is built, the questions only Ryan can settle, and what is parked. The order of work is docs/status.md ("Next, in order"), kept at every handoff; the tasks are docs/todo.md. A question lives here and a task there, never both; a question answered in chat lands as a row and leaves this list.

Open

Do a foe’s ticks come before or after its action?

Today Burning and Vitriol tick at the start of a foe’s action, so a burn can kill it before it strikes (the forecast leaves that blow out, the intent stays shown) and a Regenerating foe regrows after the tick, a race. Ticking after the action instead would make every doomed foe land its last blow, Burn pure attrition rather than a timed stun, and the gauge’s pending loss a death after the blow. Ryan, 4 November, on the cut that let a burnt foe strike: “it's a fair point about whether dots tick before or after the enemy action.” His to settle; nothing moves until he does.

Built

  • Engine and tools: the headless seeded simulation, the validated content pipeline, every number read through sim/effects.gd, save and resume, the Android build at 60 fps, the machines welded at load for the draw calls; the balance runner, the campaign runner, the map survey, the harness and the hangar page.
  • The battle: the round (moves planned and played on EXECUTE, ENGAGE or END TURN, END TURN asking while a machine can still act, orders by tap or drag, an undo in the order things were done, one pack one fight, fought one at a time from beside it, every pack’s ring in the enemy’s violet until the long wall’s view, hunters that catch), the boss fought after its reveal, movement in hexes (water the one priced ground, the horde wading it too, walls the flyer's, winged packs crossing, packs passing each other and flyers and the ground passing each other, hunters pouncing at a ring of two and taking the machine nearest a prey sealed off), six map shapes with their walls named, their fords and streets three or four hexes in all, their decoration laid to be read and their relief their own, targeting at random by default and by aim where a foe carries one, never over-committing; packs as compositions, the field at the start and the rift's cycle by the tier and the ramp, the long wall’s Phase 1 (the field’s size from the ramp, the siege under way at the start, the tide on the beat with the tears and the seam, the beat halved at the Rupture, a pusher’s segment of the wall, every pack’s threat rating on the hold, a composition’s layout and lead, no withdrawal, the shield the fight’s with the shield regen slot, two rerolls a round), the whole wall struck, the Rupture meter with its Fractures, the elites at them and the seal with its stall guard.
  • The skirmish: the vector HUD (gestures, forecasts, feed effects, codex), every passive, keyword, status and trigger not marked planned, the plaque, and the current weapon rules (no crits, one number a weapon, two reaches, the die showing the blow's number), the Paradox meter and Numbed's clock, the overheat at the end of the player's turn, the Badger's hopper with its cap and its Ore Plate, the Nemesis's rage with its roll again shown, the Storm Petrel's shield paying its weapons' heat with the zap, its plate and its regen, the Giant Petrel's bank of rerolls and its three locks, the foes' heat as Burning and Scorched, a rally landing at once and riding each foe's next move, the intents counting the rallies on their way under a pulsing preview chip; the enemy's three verbs (Silence muting keywords, Purge by source, a foe's Cleanse), Primed, Barrier, Ward and Regenerating on the foes, and the player's Purge; a foe's follow-up, steal, swallow, merge, drain and grudge, a move used once a fight, a last act and a boss in parts in content.
  • Content: five makers, twenty-four chassis, 81 components, 18 modules; four domains with fifty-nine wildlife (nineteen at tier one, the three old stand-ins, the Barrow Giant and the Shuffler Spawn among them, thirty-two recolours and the Barrow Giant's two higher, the Djinn a caster at tier two, Rotbloom a massive at tier two, the Astromancer's three orbs at tier two, Enkidu at tier three, the compositions by tier, and twenty-one elites as placeholder kits, six of them wanderers), thirteen bosses as placeholder kits with their escorts, three a domain and the Marsh's four, and eight heroes with their promoted forms, four foe moves still planned.
  • The campaign's rules, headless only (sim/campaign.gd): cities and gates, fronts, the Convergence, salvage, Favour, the tender, tiers, wrecks and rushes, the party dealt from the benches, its finals and the finale, a boss at its battle's tier, the rift's tier locked and the ramp, salvage by menace and Anathema. No screen shows them yet.
  • The quick campaign (section 3): its run, its screens with the game's first hangar, its save and its runner (sim/quick_run.gd, game/quick/, tools/quick_run.gd).
  • Art and sound: every machine's model by lowforge with joints, idle acts, gaits and size classes, the five Wainwright & Sutler machines' first cuts among them (the Musk Ox's and the Cassowary's of 31 October, the Cottonmouth's, the Swallowtail's and the Whip Scorpion's of 2 November); the first foe art (eighteen wildlife and eight chibi heroes, picked by Ryan, and the ladder's thirty-two recolours by hue), and rough placeholders for every boss, elite, part and giant with no pick, so since 4 November no foe lacks art and a promoted form wears its hero's (tools/placeholder_art.py); the holographic 3D battlefield and each domain's tileset on its painted ground; six tracks; the effects; Jenny's lines; every maker's machine death.

Questions for Ryan

Where the code and the design part, or the records cannot settle a point.

The rules

  • Whether heat below 6 should bite (section 11's pricing row): the vanilla ladder read heat 1, 2 and 4 free on a cold die with one hot piece, since an overheat burns only the excess, so the warm classes are flavour today and the allowance a hot piece carries over its floor is a design choice; the levers are the maxima, what an overheat costs (the excess one for one today), a soft zone above two thirds of the maximum, or the cooling, never a piece's number. Measured the same evening as a what-if (docs/measurements.md, the heat knee by the maximum): the knee moves a class a step with the maximum, 6 at 12, between 4 and 6 at 10 (heat 4 still free in rounds on a cold die) and 4 at 8, where heat 4 costs half a vanilla point, heat 6 one and a half and heat 8 two and a half; a fleet at 8 would overheat the hot kits every round (Kestrel's row at 128% peak on the tiered formations at its 14), so the bite for the warm classes wants a selective lever. Ryan, 28 October: heat is a core pillar and undervalued as a gameplay element, and his frame is section 2's triangle row (every component priced, the basics too; passive cooling the lever, his lean to cut it; vent payoffs); what stays his to settle: the cooling's cut and what keeps its cooling, each free piece's price (the to-do's heat session bench), whether a shield's price in heat is wanted (a hot machine that cannot shield is the spiral, the wrench or the trap by his call), the soft zone or a harder overheat if the prices and the cooling leave the warm classes free, and heat between fights after the skirmish's bite is right.
  • What shown text calls a machine's die, which looks like no die: the problem that kept "face" out of shown text. A session of its own.

Content

  • What the go on the roster analysis left open (docs/roster_analysis.md, 14 October): the tiers the briefs propose (docs/briefs/README.md's Tiers, nothing decided), and each brief in its own session.
  • The bosses' slate (docs/bosses_proposal.md, its calls settled 30 October to 2 November, the last that evening: armour only where a boss's kit is armour, a follow-up that cannot be Disrupted once announced, Showtime the Thief's third, the escorts' moves): the kits, the escorts, the massive lines, the elites brief's candidates and the names his to mark in his content pass.
  • The foe roster after the 13 October picks: whether the Bomb plays as more than an annoyance, judged in play (it stays until Ryan plays it, 15 October; the change on the table if it does not is a burst that also hits the foes beside it, which makes a hurt Bomb a tool for the player); new types from the candidates Ryan marked that have not landed (the shielded skeleton knight; the goblin shaman, the Goblin Nob, the Goblin Guard and the Captain are in content; marked in tools/foeforge/picks/picks.json).
  • Silence's, Purge's and the foe's Cleanse's carriers and Purge's numbers (section 5's row, section 9's levers; the Emitter's Purge 2 a placeholder): the Meadow's shaman the first silence, the Waste's tier-two and tier-three casters and the bosses the purges (the Augur's Dispel a purge special), the Marsh's healer the cleanse; Ryan's marks on the ladder (git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md) place them.
  • The promoted forms' one move more for the Warrior, the Martial Artist, the Thief, the Lancer, the Augur and the Black Mage, and Fáfnir's mechanics for the Summoner's: a slate in docs/heroes_proposal.md (21 October), the Augur's a purge by lean, which would answer the carriers' question too; in a session of its own on Ryan's word (21 October).

The campaign and the economy

  • The front's card, now that the curve's form is settled (30 October, section 3's curve row: the tier locked and shown, the ramp live): the rift's tier where the Convergence's showed, its count of elites, and their kinds once scouted, so scouting buys the kind and an unscouted elite's kind stays the curveball; the runner's front choice by tier and pay; the breadth of components a run deals (Ryan: breadth, not quantity) is the economy pass's, with loot at a battle's end waiting to see.
  • The ramp’s numbers once a turtle’s read is in (the turtle persona went with the defence rule, 6 November): whether the step inside a battle moves off zero, and whether the bounty cap leaves the bench (section 4's stall row). The Fracture's prize is section 8's Open row.
  • The finals' escort (section 9's finals row holds the read): the hero's fall alone never wins since 27 October, so what is left is the escort's size, three wildlife beside the tier's two or four where a formation is eight to sixteen. Claude's lean: the escort first, since the hero scales with the tier already; read again on the merged content.
  • The runner reads all four domains now: is that the baseline the commander layer waits for? The commander layer itself (section 8): the command die or one-shots only, city abilities, what a Fracture offers.

The battlefield and the foes

  • The foes' fresh start, built 30 October with the pacing session (its brief retired: git show 85e28fd:docs/briefs/fresh_start.md; the proposal git show 85e28fd:docs/foes_proposal.md). What waits on Ryan's marks: the twenty-one elites' names and numbers (the Monster Hunter nods (his reads of 4 November built: the Thunder Ape's rage a mode, the World Eater's pin laying Crushed; the Ape's spin and cannonball dive on the bench), the Gargantuan King's name from his pattern; the Fell Rider's mount, the Nob's rage and three names settled 4 November), Golden Sun's elite candidates, since Ryan wants the series pulled from (4 November, the Emperor Scorpion landed as the Waste's third boss on his word): the Star Magician landed the same evening as the Astromancer, a wanderer (the tiers row); which Fracture each tier spawns the elites at beyond the table's; the compositions' partition of the ladder's lists; the escort compositions hand-tailored by tier; roles (his six words, shown or internal); one rider on one advanced foe a domain and enemy Shock, telegraphed, if any; the heroes' extra moves (the Martial Artist has four written: which go); the Rune-bearer's execute against the retired Execute.
  • The elites' numbers (4 November, their first read: every cell a free win, the lone elites dying in two rounds): the lone elites' HP to the massive band (30 to 40 at tier one, where the Old Man and the World Eater stand), an escort each (the Nob's goblins, the Fell Rider's wraiths), or both. Read as a what-if at 40 HP the same evening (docs/measurements.md): HP alone buys rounds, not threat; a lone elite's fight grows from two rounds to four for a trio, enough for a bellow or a roar to show once, and stays a free win for every row, since its blow is a rank-and-file's. So Claude's lean: the massive band in both numbers (the scales row's blow of 7 or 8 with the HP), an escort each, or its curveball read on the map (the hunter's catch, the World Eater's push), which no cell reads; at 200 seeds once marked.
  • Corruption in a pack's wake (section 4).

The long wall

  • Reinforcement inside a fight: cut, or kept as it is (Ryan, 6 November: “what gameplay does it solve?”, since the horde’s fantasy is now the wall’s; Claude’s lean to cut; unchanged until he answers).
  • Carries: whether the roster wants two or three machines that ramp inside a fight, and their shapes (the charge that is a lock, the siege mode, the capacitor); not before the formation pass.
  • Map loot: whether, and as what (bait the seam swallows, a loot-runner pack).
  • The Fracture beyond today’s: the shell, a weakened boss, or picks; today’s Fractures stand until he picks.
  • The long wall’s numbers, each a placeholder moved only on his word after the reads (docs/measurements.md, 6 November): the wall’s length and the depth, the beat, the opening’s rows and pressure (the hunters’ formations of the Meadow, the Marsh and the Waste catch a machine as the first turn opens), the spread, the attack range, the beat after the Rupture, the seam’s creep at twelve deep, the thresholds’ base. The default rating by size is his (a patrol 1, a formation 3, an elite and an escort 4) and no placeholder; an elite must then earn its four, the elites’ numbers above.
  • The refit against the new shape: every foe’s blow, every chassis’s shield and the shield regen values, held to the proposal’s section 4.2 tests and the promise read’s band between short, intended and over; the stock pass’s loops are not run until he calls it.

Pinned

  • Room to expand in the lore (section 1).
  • A secret true final boss: an Arsenal extradimensional superweapon (Ryan).
  • An aim that seeks plate, for a breaching move: a breacher aims at random as the rank and file do, or at the exposed, which a breach finds on every machine alike, so it has no taste for the tank; a plate-seeking aim is free to add where a breacher's threat calls for one (22 October; any foe may carry the aim its threat calls for, 24 October).
  • The gravity piece (section 7): a tier-three blueprint with an area effect, shelved with lots of potential (Ryan, 13 October).
  • Hellfire, free now that no demon faction wears it and the Summoner's move is Pyroclasm (13 October): an Arsenal component prefix, later (Ryan, 12 October); the King James Bible's own words, so it keeps the liturgy.
  • A map taunt for a slow Arsenal bruiser; a machine that moves after it fights.
  • A low-hull berserker or last-stand entry.
  • Transforming chassis; a large Aphelion hovercraft; long guns while the engagement range may change (section 7).
  • Command entries with a battlefield meaning too.
  • An endless mode after the bounded campaign.
  • Jenny's idle nag.
  • Livery by Sponsorship, once the hangar exists.
The design record for Riftgate. The game's rules as they stand are in docs/rules.md; this document says why. History lives in git and docs/status.md, never here.
